Decision Models vs. LLMs as Rubric Judges:
Cheaper, Faster, and Wrong in the Same Places
Delip Rao*
University of Pennsylvania
delip@upenn.edu
Chris Callison-Burch
University of Pennsylvania
ccb@upenn.edu
Abstract
LLM judges now score outputs against rubrics,
in benchmarks and as training rewards. Deci-
sion models, classifier-like alternatives, return
probabilities over permitted answers with a con-
fidence score but no free text. Two such models
are Jev and an open reproduction, Perplexity’s
Decider. We compare each with three flash-
tier LLM judges on nine panels from seven
benchmarks with human judgments, giving ev-
ery judge identical criterion texts. The LLM
judges run in two setups, reading a whole rubric
at once, as decision models do, or one criterion
at a time. Our findings suggest both decision
models can often stand in for them. The LLM
judges cost 16 to 357 times as much and take 5
to 350 times as long, yet in both setups each de-
cision model trails them significantly in at most
6 of 27 comparisons. The two kinds of judge err
alike, agreeing more with one another than with
the human-annotated judgment labels on ordi-
nal criteria. More than 85% of flash-tier LLM
verdicts on either decision model’s most con-
fident errors repeat them, where independent
errors would give about half. Each decision
model’s confidence ranks its errors on most or-
dinal panels, so each should make a natural first
stage of a cascade deferring uncertain verdicts
to an LLM judge. Yet with flash-tier fallbacks,
such cascades gained a mere 2.7 points at best
over the strongest single judge, even with or-
acle thresholds, which suggests they mainly
save cost. A cascade needs a fallback that is
right where its first stage is wrong, but the LLM
judges are mostly wrong in the same places as
Jev, and the Decider is already the strongest sin-
gle judge on most panels. Even each vendor’s
higher-tier model repeats those errors, and as
a fallback it lifted no cascade more than 1.7
points above both tiers’ strongest single judge.
Holding across setups, effort levels, and tiers,
these findings suggest designing future decision
models afresh to get right what LLM judges get
wrong, and vice versa.

Figure 1: LLM-based rubric judges against the Choice
framing of (a) Jev and (b) Perplexity’s Decider (Sec-
tion 3.1; pplx-decider-v1.1-27b). In most compar-
isons with Jev, and in about half with the Decider, the

1 Introduction
LLM judges now return verdicts on the individual
criteria of rubrics, for benchmarks (Arora et al.,
2025) and as training rewards (Gunjal et al., 2026),
and are validated against human labels (Zheng
et al., 2023; Bavaresco et al., 2025). We call the
judged submission aunitand a (unit, criterion)
combination apair. A per-criterion grader sends
each pair as its own request (Rao and Callison-
Burch, 2026), which multiplies the requests by
the number of criteria but raises cost far less (Ap-
pendix N).
Atyped classifieranswers a question about a
structured input with a probability distribution over
a fixed set of permitted answers and writes no free
text. Jev, from TypeSafe, is one; its creators call it
1
arXiv:2609.29769v3  [cs.CL]  13 Oct 2026

a “System One” model1 and a “decision model”,
the term we use for the class. Perplexity’s Decider
(pplx-decider-v1.1-27b), which a community
index lists among open reproductions of Jev, is
a second decision model. Jev, the original, is our
reference, and we do not compare the two with each
other. A decision model judges every criterion of a
unit in one request, with a probability or confidence
for each answer, so it could replace an LLM judge
or pick the pairs that need one. It returns only
probabilities, whereas LLM judges reason before
they answer. That difference in design suggests the
two kinds of judge would err in different places,
and a cascade can beat the LLM judge it defers to
only if they do.
We set both decision models against threeflash-
tierLLM judges, from tiers their vendors call fast
and lower-priced (Appendix A): GPT-5.6 Luna,
Gemini 3.8 Flash, and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash (Luna,
Gemini, and DeepSeek). Earlier work also chose
smaller judges, partly to cut cost (Verga et al., 2024;
Chlapanis et al., 2025; Xie et al., 2026). The judges
all receive the same 5,003 pairs from ninepanels,
sampled from seven public benchmarks with hu-
man judgments (Table A3). Two arebinary panels,
with only binary criteria, and seven areordinal
panels, which rate most criteria on ordered levels.
Every judge reads the same criterion texts, fixed
in advance with no examples, rewording, or tun-
ing. We run the LLM judges one criterion at a
time, on whole rubrics as the decision models read
them, at high reasoning effort, and in each vendor’s
higher tier. The answers to our three questions,
given below for each decision model, hold in all
four conditions, with each condition’s own counts
(Table 2):
RQ1. Can a typed classifier stand in for LLM
judges, and at what cost and speed?
RQ2. How different are the classifier’s verdicts
from the LLM judges’?
RQ3. How well does a confidence-based cascade
from the classifier to an LLM judge work?
RQ1: Often, and far more cheaply.Against
per-criterion LLM judges, Jev differs significantly
in only 8 of 27 paired accuracy comparisons, lead-
ing mostly on binary panels and trailing only on
ordinal ones, and reaches parity (within 5 points)
in 8 more (Figure 1; Section 4). The Decider dif-
1The name comes from the System 1/System 2 distinction
in Kahneman (2011) between fast, automatic judgment and
slow, deliberate reasoning. Vendor documentation: https:
//docs.typesafe.ai, accessed 23 September 2026.
fers significantly in 13 (one of them also at parity),
leading in 8, mostly on ordinal panels, and trailing
in 5, so it is not significantly worse in 22. Across
both judging modes, the LLM judges’ costs were
16 to 325 times Jev’s and their wall times 28 to 350
times. Against the Decider, their costs were 18 to
357 times its own and their wall times 5.4 to 67.5
times.
RQ2: Less than their designs suggest.With ei-
ther decision model among them, the judges agree
with one another more than with the labels on each
ordinal panel. On six of the seven, Jev and every
LLM judge place units lower on average than the
raters did, a pattern unstated rating conventions
could produce (Section 5). The Decider is the ex-
ception. In its Choice framing it places units lower
on only four panels, and in its Score framing it
is about as accurate on ELLIPSE as the raters are
against one another. The judges makecorrelated
errors, the “same places” of our title. When one
judge is wrong, another gives the same wrong ver-
dict more often than independent errors would. Of
the LLM verdicts on Jev’s most confident errors,
96.0% repeat its answer, where independence pre-
dicts 50.3%; on the Decider’s, 86.5% do, against
47.9%.
RQ3: Well for cost, not for accuracy.Both
decision models’ confidence ranks their errors on
six ordinal panels, as a first stage requires, and
Jev’s probabilities are calibrated on the binary pan-
els. But a cascade gains over its fallback only on
kept pairs that its first stage alone gets right. With
Jev first, correlated errors leave few. A Decider-
first cascade meets a different limit, since on six of
nine panels the best single judge it must beat is al-
ready one of the Decider’s own framings. Cascades
cut cost, yet even with oracle thresholds, Jev-first
cascades beat the best single judge by at most 2.7
points with flash-tier fallbacks, and Decider-first
ones by at most 1.7. With higher-tier fallbacks and
oracle thresholds, the largest gain over either tier’s
best single judge is 1.7 points for Jev-first cascades
and 1.4 for Decider-first ones (Section 6).
2 Related Work
LLM judges show position, verbosity, and self-
enhancement biases, yet GPT-4 agrees with human
preferences about as often as humans do (Zheng
et al., 2023), and LLMs given the evaluators’ in-
structions rate consistently with experts (Chiang
and Lee, 2023); on JUDGE-BENCH, which in-
2

cludes both USR sets, reliability varies with prop-
erty and annotator expertise (Bavaresco et al.,
2025). Uncertain labels can make a machine look
human-level (Elangovan et al., 2025). High agree-
ment can hide score differences (Thakur et al.,
2025), and LLM essay graders score below hu-
mans (Kundu and Barbosa, 2024) and penalize mi-
nor errors that humans discount (Mathew et al.,
2026), so we report the offset.
Prometheus and Prometheus 2 train open evalua-
tors on user-supplied rubrics (Kim et al., 2024a,b),
and CheckEval’s binary checklists raise agree-
ment (Lee et al., 2025). HealthBench refined its
criteria for its grader (Arora et al., 2025), and LLM-
Rubric maps answer distributions to each human’s
labels (Hashemi et al., 2024), a layer that would
absorb an offset; we fit no such layer.
Across more than 350 models, larger and more
accurate models make highly correlated errors,
even across providers (Kim et al., 2025). Mistakes
grow more alike with capability (Goel et al., 2025),
and juries of smaller judges from different ven-
dors can reduce bias (Verga et al., 2024). Closed-
set probabilities carry information about correct-
ness (Guo et al., 2017; Kadavath et al., 2022) and
serve to abstain (Geifman and El-Yaniv, 2017) or
to escalate to costlier models (Chen et al., 2024);
Trust or Escalate sets its threshold on a calibra-
tion set to guarantee human agreement (Jung et al.,
2025).
3 Judges, Panels, and Protocol
All judges run in the open-source AutoRubric
framework (Rao and Callison-Burch, 2026), the
harness. An ordinal criterion’s options are ordered
levels, and its pairs areordinal pairs.
3.1 The decision models
A Jev request carries one question per criterion and
a record (the vendor’s “state”) holding the unit’s
input and submission, as an LLM judge receives
them. Jev never sees the harness’s system prompt
and sees options in scale order. It costs $0.042 per
million input tokens; output is free.
The Decider, whose version 1.1 was released
on 5 October 2026, is an open-weight fine-tune
of Qwen3.8-27B (its training data are described
under Limitations) and takes requests of the same
shape through Perplexity’s Decisions API at $0.02
per million input tokens, output free.2 It answered
every request on 7 October 2026, with 8 requests
in flight under the vendor’s limit of 10 requests
per second. Minutes later the requests for the 106
repeat units of Section 3.2 were sent again, to give
the Decider its own run-to-run baseline.
Both decision models offer threeprimitives, each
with adecoding rule: Noul returns the probabil-
ity of yes, thresholded at 0.5; Choice a distri-
bution over option labels, read by argmax; and
Score the expected level over ordered level descrip-
tions, rounded to the closest level, ties to even (Ap-
pendix E). Aframingis a primitive with our ques-
tion wording (Table A2).
On the binary panelsJev Choiceasks Choice
over MET, UNMET, and CANNOT_ASSESS, and
Jev Noulasks Noul, both through thewrapper, a
fixed instruction with the harness’s MET and UN-
MET definitions. On the ordinal panels Jev Choice
gets the criterion sentence as instruction and the
LLM judges’ option texts, theNA option(“Cannot
assess / not applicable”) included.Jev Scorereads
the same texts as level descriptions without that
option and therefore cannot abstain. On the 364
embedded binary pairsof the FED and USR panels,
both use Jev Noul. Jev Choice and the LLM judges
are thematched judges, and with Jev Score thefive
judges. Jev’sconfidence, used only to rank pairs,
is the confidence that Choice and Score return, or
2|P(yes)−0.5| for Noul (Appendix I).Decider
Choice,Decider Score, andDecider Noulare these
framings sent unchanged to the Decider, whose con-
fidence comes from the same answer fields as Jev’s.
The Decider gets its own copy of the main analy-
ses, with its own matched judges, five judges, and
confidence. Unless a sentence names the Decider,
“matched judges”, “five judges”, and “confidence”
refer to Jev’s copy, as do “every judge” and “no
judge” in statements of results.
Our expectations were formed on Jev (Ap-
pendix L). Of the decision models now served
through public APIs, such as Fastino’s GLiDE and
Cloudflare’s Clef, we chose the Decider because
it heads the community Decision Index 3 (first of
112 entries at 62.75, with Jev, jev-1.13.0, shown
tied for second at 60.11) and discloses its weights,
2https:
//docs.perplexity.ai/docs/decisions/quickstart,
accessed 6 October 2026.
3https://huggingface.co/spaces/multimodalart/
jev-decision-index, edition 0.3, data of 6 October 2026,
accessed the same day.
3

backbone, and training data. That index scores
models on classification benchmarks, which need
not predict how well a model judges rubrics. We
report each decision model only against the LLM
judges (Appendix R).
3.2 The LLM judges
Each LLM judge runs through the harness
(DeepSeek via OpenRouter; Appendix A) with its
default system prompts and an undated model iden-
tifier, at temperature 0 except for Luna’s enforced
1.0 (Table A2), and returns a verdict and an expla-
nation, not a probability. A logged seed shuffles
each pair’s options against position bias (Wang
et al., 2024a). Themain runs(19 September 2026)
set no reasoning effort, so vendor defaults applied:
medium for Luna and Gemini, high on DeepSeek’s
own API, and undocumented on OpenRouter (Ap-
pendix O).
There are twojudging modes. Withper-criterion
judgments, the harness default and the mode of our
main runs, the judge takes one criterion of a unit
at a time; withwhole-rubric (holistic) judgments
it reads the unit’s whole rubric at once and still
returns one verdict per criterion. Both practices ex-
ist (Liu et al., 2023; Lin and Chen, 2023). Whole-
rubric judgments make the LLM judges compara-
ble with the decision models; per-criterion judg-
ments stay primary, following work that recom-
mends judging criteria separately to avoid the halo
effects documented in human rating (Thorndike,
1920; Cooper, 1981) and related effects in LLM
judges (Stureborg et al., 2024; Hu et al., 2024;
Feuer et al., 2025).
The whole-rubric runs (27 September, eight days
later) kept the option orders and sent 8 requests at
a time, like the decision models;repeat runsre-
sent the whole-rubric requests for a seeded 10% of
units (106) as a run-to-run baseline (Appendix N).
The same day,effort runsrepeated the per-criterion
judgments at high reasoning effort on all nine pan-
els, and DeepSeek’s also at medium. An effort
calibration on the repeat units supports reading
Luna’s and Gemini’s main runs as medium; with
DeepSeek’s medium run they form themedium
condition, and the high runs form thehigh condi-
tion(Appendix O). On 2 October thehigher-tier
runsrepeated the per-criterion judgments with each
flash-tier judge’s higher-priced sibling, GPT-5.6
Terra (Terra), Gemini 3.1 Pro, or DeepSeek V4 Pro,
keeping option orders, temperatures, and requests
in flight and leaving reasoning at vendor defaults.
Thesehigher-tier judgesmake up thehigher-tier
condition(Appendix P).
3.3 Panels and labels
The binary panels hold RiceChem’s chemistry an-
swers (Sonkar et al., 2024) and HealthBench’s
chatbot replies (Arora et al., 2025). The ordinal
panels hold ELLIPSE’s learner essays (Crossley
et al., 2023), FED’s dialogue turns (FED-Turn) and
whole dialogues (FED-Dialogue) (Mehri and Eske-
nazi, 2020a), HelpSteer2’s assistant replies (Wang
et al., 2024b), LFQA’s answers to ELI5 ques-
tions (Kamoda et al., 2025), and USR’s dialogue
replies (Mehri and Eskenazi, 2020b) on Topical-
Chat (Gopalakrishnan et al., 2019) (USR-TC) and
PersonaChat (Zhang et al., 2018) (USR-PC). Each
ordinal panel is a 10% or 20% sample of units, strat-
ified where possible and drawn with a fixed seed
before any judging (Table A1). The binary panels
keep their benchmarks’ checklists, with question-
specific presence checks on RiceChem and con-
sensus behavior checks on HealthBench. On the
ordinal panels the judges read ourrubric versions,
released (Appendix M); the raters had each bench-
mark’s own instructions. No judge sees the labels,
the benchmarks’ overall scores, or the strata.
An ordinal pair’shuman labelis its raters’ mean
level rounded to the nearest level, halves up (46.8%
of ELLIPSE labels are half-way), and a binary
pair’s the rater majority, or RiceChem’s teaching-
assistant label.Unanimous pairs, whose raters
all agree, andsplit pairsare reported apart, as
rater disagreement can be signal (Plank, 2022).
Therater referencescores each rater against the
rounded mean of the others on the six ordinal pan-
els with several raters; its target averages one rat-
ing fewer than the label, so it does not bound
a judge. Twono-read baselinesignore the unit:
HelpSteer2’s constant predictor (60.0% exact accu-
racy) and LFQA’s provenance baseline, which sees
only whether ChatGPT or a Reddit user answered
(68.1%).
3.4 Metrics and statistics
Exact accuracyis the share of ordinal pairs pre-
dicted at the label’s level, andmean QWKaverages
per-criterion quadratic-weighted kappas (Cohen,
1968). A judge’soffsetis its mean predicted mi-
nus labeled level, and itsgapits exact accuracy
minus the rater reference’s. Binary criteria getver-
dict accuracy, Cohen’sκ (Cohen, 1960), and the
MET-rate offsetin points. As chance-level exact

accuracy varies with the number of levels, panels
are compared by mean QWK, offset, or gap.
Paired comparisons use the pairs both judges
scored, resampling units 10,000 times for per-
centile intervals and Holm-corrected bootstrap p-
values (Appendix C). Two judges areseparated,
or differ significantly, when the 95% interval of
their accuracy difference excludes zero, atparity
when the 90% interval lies within 5 points of zero
(a post-hoc margin; Table C1 gives the smallest
each comparison supports), and otherwiseincon-
clusive. In each condition, a decision model’s 27
comparisons with the LLM judges form their own
Holm family.
Abstentions andjudge-call failures, pairs left
without a usable verdict, leave the denominators,
so pair sets differ between tables (Appendix C).
Cost is list price, the Decider’s as documented the
day before its run, and DeepSeek’s its OpenRouter
bill (Appendix B). Level shifts, cascades, juries,
and other counterfactuals arepost-hoc analysesof
the recorded verdicts, with no judge rerun.
4 Decision Models Can Often Replace an
LLM Judge
4.1 Accuracy in paired comparisons
Of Jev Choice’s 27 comparisons with the LLM
judges, 8 are separated, 8 at parity, and 11 incon-
clusive (Figure 1a; Table 2). Three of its four leads
fall on binary panels and all four deficits on ordinal
ones. Four separations remain significant after cor-
rection, and resampling the prompts, questions, or
contexts that units share leaves eight (Appendix C).
On no ordinal panel is Jev Choice the most accu-
rate matched judge; Gemini is, or ties, everywhere
but LFQA (Table 1). Jev Score is separated ahead
of Jev Choice on four ordinal panels, never be-
hind, and is the most accurate of Jev’s five judges
on FED-Turn and USR-TC (Appendix E). Help-
Steer2’s constant predictor beats each of Jev’s five
judges, and on LFQA no judge, the Decider’s fram-
ings included, differs significantly from the prove-
nance baseline in exact accuracy (Appendix H).
Decider Choice leads the LLM judges in 8 compar-
isons, mostly on ordinal panels, and trails them in
5 (Figure 1b; Table 1; Appendix R).
5

4.2 Decision models are cheaper and faster
than every LLM judge
Over the nine panels Jev Choice cost $0.063 (Ta-
ble B1), Luna 29 times as much, Gemini 325 times,
and DeepSeek 66 times; Jev Choice took 29 sec-
onds, Luna 859, Gemini 950, and DeepSeek 6,298
(30 to 220 times as long). Each Jev framing sent 8
requests at a time and each LLM judge 16 to 32 on
the ordinal panels, so these ratios understate Jev’s
advantage at equal concurrency. Table 2 gives the
multiples against both decision models in all four
conditions, including the whole-rubric runs, which
matched Jev’s concurrency. Perplexity’s rate limit,
not the 8 requests in flight, sets most of Decider
Choice’s wall time (Appendix R).
4.3 Judging mode, reasoning effort, and
model tier
Neither judging mode, reasoning effort, nor model
tier changes the answers to RQ1 to RQ3. Only the
numbers behind them differ between the columns
of Table 2. The runs of each condition, its changes
in the LLM judges’ accuracy, and Jev’s copy of its
comparisons are in Appendices N (whole-rubric
judgments), O (high effort), and P (higher tier),
with the Decider’s copy in Appendix R.
5 Decision Models and LLM Judges Err
Alike
5.1 A shared shortfall on ordinal criteria
On all seven ordinal panels, in either decision
model’s copy, agreement among the matched
judges exceeds their agreement with the labels, and
even the least-agreeing pair of judges beats the best
judge–label agreement on five panels in Jev’s copy
(Tables 1b and N5b). Label noise or a common
offset could produce this pattern. Every judge of
either copy falls short of the rater reference on EL-
LIPSE, FED-Dialogue, and USR-PC but exceeds it
on LFQA, and in both copies the matched judges’
mean QWK with the labels trails the reference’s
only on ELLIPSE and USR-PC.
In Jev’s copy every matched judge’s offset is neg-
ative on the six ordinal panels other than USR-TC,
as are 31 of the five judges’ 35 offsets (Tables C3h
and G1). ELLIPSE’s are largest. Its labels put
36.3% of pairs at levels 4–5, and none of Jev’s
five judges more than 4.2% (Figure G4a). Decider
Choice places units lower than the raters on FED-
Turn, FED-Dialogue, and USR-PC and higher on
HelpSteer2, LFQA, and USR-TC. On ELLIPSE

over the condition’s best single judge or over that of
either tier (picked from the first stage’s framings and
the LLM judges of both tiers; not computed for whole-
rubric runs), in points; panels where a cross-fitted cas-
cade matching the best LLM judge costs under half of
it.
it puts 10.7% of pairs at levels 4–5, and the sign
of its offset there depends on the rounding rule of
Section 3.3. The offset is −0.46 levels against our
half-up labels, −0.23 against the unrounded rater
mean, and+0.004against labels rounded down.
Criteria with larger negative offsets also fall fur-
ther below the rater reference, a link that disappears
once each criterion’s predictions get a leave-one-
out shift (Appendix G).
Rating conventions that guided the raters but are
absent from our criterion texts may underlie the
shortfall; we inferred candidate conventions from
the judges’ errors, so this account is exploratory
(Appendix H). The account fits Jev and the three
LLM judges but not the Decider, whose Score fram-
ing reaches 51.0% exact accuracy on ELLIPSE
against the rater reference’s 51.4%.
6

5.2 The LLM judges repeat the decision
models’ confident errors
We count an LLM verdict that repeats a decision
model’s wrong answer as arepeated error, and the
share of repeated errors on its wrong pairs mea-
sures correlated errors. Since judges sharing an
offset would often agree anyway, theindependence
baselineis each LLM judge’s rate of giving that
wrong answer on other pairs with the same criterion
and label. Over all of Jev Choice’s errors the share
beats the baseline on every ordinal panel, by 18.8 to
34.2 points on six and 8.6 on ELLIPSE, where the
judges’ negative offsets explain most repeated er-
rors (Table I1c). Theconfident-error sampleholds
a Choice framing’s 12 most confident errors per
ordinal panel. For Jev, 242 of the 252 LLM ver-
dicts on these 84 pairs (96.0%) repeat its answer,
against 50.3% expected, and about as many in the
other conditions (Table 2). HealthBench shows the
same (Appendix J). Rater disagreement may ex-
plain repeats on split pairs, which hold 62 of Jev’s
72 confident errors on multi-rater panels, but not
on unanimous pairs, such as a USR-TC pair whose
context holds no fact (Appendix H).
The LLM judges repeat the Decider’s answer
in 218 of their 252 verdicts on its confident-error
sample (86.5%), against 47.9% expected, far above
baseline on six panels (Appendix R). On ELLIPSE
the share stays at or below baseline in every condi-
tion. In the main runs the LLM judges are wrong
on all 36 verdicts there, missing the same pairs
in the same direction, but 20 of the 36 lie fur-
ther below the label than the Decider’s answer.
On HealthBench 44.4% of the LLM verdicts on
Decider Noul’s confident errors repeat its answer,
fewer than half but more than the 24.8% expected.
6 Correlated Errors Undo the Cascade
Advantage
Acascadelets a cheap judge answer every pair and
sends thedeferred pairs, whose confidence falls
below a threshold, to afallback judge; the cheap
judge’s verdicts stand on thekept pairs. Anora-
cle thresholdis chosen on the pairs it is evaluated
on, so its gains are optimistic; across-fitted thresh-
oldis chosen on a random half of the units and
evaluated on the other. A cheap judge whose confi-
dence tracks its errors is the natural first stage, but
the cascade rises above its fallback only through
kept pairs that the cheap judge gets right and the
fallback misses, and for Jev these are few, at most
14 per ordinal panel in that panel’s best cascade
(Section 5.2; Table K2).
6.1 The decision models’ confidence ranks
their errors
Jev Choice’s errors gather among its less confi-
dent verdicts on every ordinal panel but ELLIPSE
(Figure I1a); on those six, the area under the re-
ceiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of
its confidence for correctness is 0.57 to 0.70. On
ELLIPSE it is 0.49, its error rate flat across bands
(Appendix I). On the binary panels, the only ones
where we measure calibration, Jev’s P(MET) sep-
arates MET from UNMET at an AUROC of 0.80
to 0.86 with an expected calibration error (ECE) of
0.056 to 0.096 (Table D1). Yet repeated errors, and
their excess over the baseline, peak on every ordinal
panel in Jev Choice’s top confidence band (80.5%
to 92.8% against 33.6% to 63.7%; Figure I1b);
these are the verdicts a cascade keeps. For Decider
Choice the AUROC is 0.60 to 0.78 on the same
six ordinal panels and 0.52 on ELLIPSE, where its
error rate is flat too. The Decider’s P(MET) has
an AUROC of 0.78 to 0.89 on the binary panels,
and Decider Noul is calibrated on RiceChem (ECE
0.084) but overconfident at both ends on Health-
Bench (ECE 0.148).
6.2 Cascades lower cost but add little
accuracy
We replay cascades with each decision model first,
its Choice framing on the ordinal panels and its
Noul framing on the binary ones (Appendix K),
against thebest single judge, chosen from the LLM
judges and that decision model’s framings. Cross-
fitted thresholds use 50 halvings, each half choos-
ing its own fallback and best single judge.
With cross-fitted thresholds no Jev-first cas-
cade’s average gain over the best single judge ex-
ceeds 1.5 points (HealthBench; Table 3); these cas-
cades trail it on six of nine panels, by 0.1 to 7.3
points. Oracle thresholds lift the largest gain to 2.0
points (HealthBench), but neither it nor the next,
1.3 (USR-PC), is significant in a kept-pair sign test
(Table K2).
Decider-first gains stay small for a different rea-
son, since one of the Decider’s framings is itself
the best single judge on six of nine panels (Ap-
pendix K). On the two ordinal panels where De-
cider Choice is more accurate than all three LLM
judges, its cascades lead their fallbacks by 17.4
points on ELLIPSE and 8.9 on HelpSteer2, through

Gemini, the fallback on ELLIPSE, is right on 9.4%
of the pairs there that Decider Choice gets wrong
and on 52.8% of those it gets right.
Whichever decision model comes first, the cross-
fitted cascade that matches a panel’s best LLM
judge saves over half of that judge’s cost on most
panels (Table 3), but on only four of nine in the
whole-rubric condition (Table 2). With flash-tier
fallbacks, no condition’s Jev-first cascade gains
more than 2.7 points (oracle) or 2.5 (cross-fitted)
over its panel’s best single judge (Table 2; Appen-
dices N and O). Higher-tier fallbacks lift a Jev-first
HelpSteer2 cascade 3.7 points, but only above its
condition’s weak best single judge, and lift no cas-
cade more than 1.7 points above either tier’s best
single judge (Appendix P).
Pooling judges’ verdicts in ajuryfares no better.
In no condition, and with neither decision model,
does the 95% interval of a median jury’s difference
from the most accurate matched judge lie above
zero (Appendix J).
7 Discussion
Prior work finds LLMs’ errors correlated across
providers (Kim et al., 2025; Goel et al., 2025); we
find them correlated across two kinds of judge, and
they stayed so with each vendor’s higher tier. The
Decider, from a second vendor on another back-
bone, showed the same excess of judge–judge over
judge–label agreement, correlated errors on six of
seven ordinal panels, and small cascade gains, so
these patterns are not peculiar to Jev. Two decision
models are still too few to show what holds for the
class, and the Decider’s ELLIPSE result remains
unexplained. TypeSafe warns that Jev may read
scope and negation literally,4 yet LLM judges told
to heed both (Appendix A) also placed units below
the raters and repeated nearly all its confident er-
rors, even at high effort. Literal reading by every
judge, unstated conventions, shared training pri-
ors, a reluctance to grant top levels (Appendix H),
and, on split pairs, rater disagreement (Section 5.2)
could each contribute; our data cannot tell them
apart. Practical recommendations that follow from
these findings are in Appendix Q.
8 Conclusion
We compared two decision models, Jev and Per-
plexity’s Decider, with flash-tier and higher-tier
LLM judges on nine panels and replayed cascades
that put each decision model first. A cascade can
beat its LLM fallback only where its first stage
alone is right. It therefore needs a first stage that
knows when it is likely to be wrong and a fallback
that is wrong somewhere else. The confidence of
both decision models meets the first need on most
panels. For Jev the second went unmet. Despite
Jev’s different design, the judges were wrong in
the same places, and the LLM judges repeated al-
most all of Jev’s most confident errors, about twice
as often as independent errors would. Even ora-
cle thresholds lifted no Jev-first cascade more than
2.7 points above the best single judge with flash-
tier fallbacks, or 1.7 above either tier’s best single
judge with higher-tier ones. The LLM judges also
repeated most of the Decider’s confident errors out-
side ELLIPSE, and no Decider-first cascade with
flash-tier fallbacks gained more than 1.7 points,
largely because one of the Decider’s own framings
was already the best single judge on most panels.
Jev is often a cheap substitute for a flash-tier LLM
judge but a poor complement to one. A judge built
differently need not be wrong differently, so the
next decision model should be designed to get right
what LLM judges get wrong, and vice versa.
4https://docs.typesafe.ai/model-jaggedness/
jev-1.13.
8

Limitations
Observational account.We formed our expec-
tations about Jev on the binary panels and tested
them on the ordinal panels, and our account of
the judges’ shortfall came afterwards and is ex-
ploratory. No experiment supplied exemplars or
rewrote level descriptions. The account could be
tested by rerunning ELLIPSE with exemplar es-
says for each level and with a grammar scale that
names observable features and drops the native-like
label for level 5 (Appendix H), and by rerunning
every ordinal panel with its benchmark’s own in-
structions to raters in place of our rubric version.
Giving the same texts to untrained human readers,
or giving the judges exemplars, would also dis-
tinguish missing conventions from priors that the
judges share through their training data. The candi-
date conventions came from inspecting the judges’
errors (Appendix H), and the cross-panel aggrega-
tions of Sections 5 and 6 were computed after the
offset had been observed. We added the Decider af-
ter the Jev analyses, ran its copy through the same
code, and recorded the claims to be checked on it,
with their thresholds, about two and a half hours
before the Decider answered any request on a study
item (Appendix R). The Decider is a fine-tune of
Qwen3.8-27B with Apache-2.0 open weights, and
530,103 of its 626,033 training rows come from the
tasksource collection, HelpSteer2’s attribute labels
among them. Its released training code labels syn-
thetic rows with OpenAI’sgpt-5.6-sol, a sibling
tier of GPT-5.6 Luna, and its data manifest does
not say how many rows are synthetic. Shared train-
ing signal is one route to correlated errors. Jev’s
training data are not disclosed, nor are those of the
LLM judges, so shared priors cannot be ruled out.
Judge configuration.The main runs’ LLM
judges are flash-tier models with the harness’s de-
fault prompt, used zero-shot, and Luna runs at its
enforced temperature (Section 3.2). This matches
the question of whether a decision model can re-
place an LLM judge in its default configuration,
but it does not measure the best obtainable LLM
judge. No few-shot prompt, wrapper for Jev on
ordinal criteria, or LLM run without the prompt’s
instructions on middle levels and self-contradiction
was tried, and Jev Choice was not run without the
NA option. The Decider received Jev’s framings
and wrapper unchanged, with none tuned for it,
and its documentation describes no retrieval. Each
vendor’s higher tier ran only with per-criterion judg-
ments at vendor-default reasoning, and no flagship
tier such as GPT-5.6 Sol, no model from a vendor
other than OpenAI, Google, and DeepSeek, and
no higher tier judging whole rubrics served as a
fallback. For Google and DeepSeek the higher tier
is an older model than the flash-tier judge it re-
placed, and Gemini 3.1 Pro’s documented default
thinking level differs from Gemini’s, but it emit-
ted about as many reasoning tokens as Gemini’s
main run (Appendix P). The judges also receive dif-
ferent instructions and option orders (Sections 3.1
and 3.2). We measured the LLM judges in both
judging modes, but the decision models only as
designed: each receives a unit’s criteria together in
a single request, and we did not test whether the
questions of one request influence one another’s
answers. Reasoning effort was requested only at
medium (on all nine panels for DeepSeek, on the
repeat units alone for Luna and Gemini) and at
high, and only with per-criterion judgments; no
LLM judge ran without reasoning or above high.
Running the LLM judges without reasoning would
narrow the cost ratios in either judging mode, with
an unknown effect on accuracy.
Runs.The main runs judged each ordinal panel
once. Jev and the LLM judges are not deterministic.
The Decider gave identical verdicts and probabil-
ities on every repeated pair in two runs minutes
apart, but its stability over days is untested and its
vendor documents occasional differences. Across
five passes over RiceChem, about 3% of Jev’s pairs
flipped verdict at least once, and in a spot check
of 82 pairs from the ordinal panels two Luna runs
gave the same verdict on 71 (Appendix A). The
repeat runs gave every LLM judge a second whole-
rubric pass over 106 units (105 for DeepSeek), per-
criterion judgments were repeated only in the effort
calibration on the same units (Appendix O) and in
that spot check, and Jev was not rerun on an ordinal
panel. The Decider ran on 7 October against the
LLM verdicts recorded on 19 and 27 September
and 2 October, with no LLM judge rerun. It has
a repeat on the ordinal panels, which Jev lacks,
and was not part of Jev’s five-pass study. Because
the whole-rubric and effort runs followed the main
runs by eight days under the same undated model
names, a model update or a change in routing could
contribute to their differences from the main runs.
DeepSeek’s medium and high runs overlapped in
time, so we do not compare its wall times across
effort levels, and because provider prompt caching
9

and OpenRouter’s routing moved some costs, effort
levels are compared on tokens and at fixed prices
as well as on billed costs (Appendix O). Open-
Router routed DeepSeek’s whole-rubric run to a
different mix of upstream providers than its main
run and billed those requests at 1.70 times their
cost at DeepSeek’s list prices, against 1.34 times
for the per-criterion ones (Appendix N), so rout-
ing, not only the judging mode, may lie behind
DeepSeek’s smaller saving. We do not compare
wall times between judging modes, since on the
ordinal panels the per-criterion runs also sent more
requests at a time than the whole-rubric runs. The
higher-tier runs came 13 days after the main runs
and allowed each request 600 seconds instead of
60. OpenRouter sent most of DeepSeek V4 Pro’s
requests to one upstream provider; two of its re-
quests hung, and its resumed run’s wall time was
not measured (Appendix P).
Samples.Results for single criteria rest on 25
to 258 units, because the ordinal panels are 10%
or 20% samples. Two results rest on very little
data, the 8 error_recovery pairs that Jev Choice
answered and the three USR-TC contexts that hold
no fact, behind the _nofact result (Appendix H).
The confident-error sample takes only 12 errors
of each decision model from each ordinal panel,
whereas the band analysis of Figure I1b uses every
error.
Statistics.The main intervals resample units, not
the larger sampling groups, and Section 4.1 and
Appendix C report what changes when the groups
are resampled instead. The 5-point equivalence
margin is post hoc (Section 3.4), and because it
is absolute it is lenient where accuracy is low, as
on ELLIPSE. The criteria singled out in the con-
fidence analysis and the cut-offs of Appendix G
were chosen after inspecting the data. The offset–
gap analysis of Section 5.1 is leave-one-out, but
the one-level ELLIPSE shift of Appendix G and
the USR shift of Appendix H were chosen on the
pairs they are scored on. Under oracle thresholds
cascade gains are optimistic, and the cross-fitted
estimates use halves as small as 12 units. Holm
corrections run within each decision model’s com-
parisons. Cascade costs are estimates under the
rules of Appendix K, which do not charge for what
a live cascade would also defer because its first
stage abstained. Only deferral on the confidence
of a decision model’s Choice or Noul framing was
tried, and the LLM judges’ token probabilities or
verbalized confidence were not elicited.
Labels and text.Nearly half of the ELLIPSE
labels depend on the rounding rule (Section 3.3).
Our rubric versions depart from the benchmarks’
own questions and rubrics (Appendix A), and we
compared them with what the raters read for EL-
LIPSE alone. On the other six ordinal panels a
convention that our texts omit may be stated in
the raters’ own instructions. The HelpSteer2 and
LFQA labels partly reflect something other than the
named criterion. HelpSteer2 correctness tracks
overall helpfulness at Pearson r= 0.94 , and prove-
nance alone predicts LFQA labels about as well
as the judges do on exact accuracy. A judge may
also have seen the items or labels of these public
benchmarks in training.
Scope.We tested only two decision models, from
two vendors, with Jev as the reference, so noth-
ing here is shown to hold for decision models in
general. Their Decision Index scores come from
classification benchmarks and may not carry over
to rubric judging. Appendices E to I study Jev’s
copy in depth, and several of their analyses were
run for Jev alone (Appendix R). Later requests un-
der the same model names may return different
verdicts, because no request except DeepSeek V4
Pro’s and the Decider’s named a fixed model ver-
sion, and Gemini 3.1 Pro is a Preview (Section 3.2
and Appendices A and P). The ordinal panels are
all English, four of the seven are dialogue, and the
longest ordinal unit is 1,114 words. Prices are list
or billed prices on the dates of the runs, with the
Decider’s read from its documentation a day earlier,
and the LLM judges’ cost depends on reasoning
defaults that providers can change.
Ethics Statement
This study uses public benchmarks as their authors
released them and collects no new human-subject
data. HealthBench’s health-related conversations
serve only as evaluation material. We report ev-
ery judge’s cost from recorded token usage and
list or billed prices (Appendix B). The authors are
not incentivized by TypeSafe, which develops Jev,
by Perplexity, which develops the Decider, or by
their industry competitors. We release the verdict
records of every judge so that the paper’s numbers
can be recomputed without any request to a model
10

API, and Appendix M lists the records and the val-
ues that need other inputs.
Generative AI Use Disclosure
Generative AI tools assisted at different stages of
preparing this paper. The corresponding author has
checked their outputs and takes complete responsi-
bility for any resulting inaccuracies.

Table A1: Construction of the nine panels. The sample gives sampled units and the sampled fraction. Half-way:
labels whose rater mean is a half level. Rater reference (Section 3.3): pooled exact accuracy (%) and mean QWK.
Unit words: whitespace words in the submission; our RiceChem records do not hold the submissions, and our
HealthBench records give completion length in characters only. HealthBench’s 406 pairs by theme: hedging 108,
context seeking 66, health data tasks 60, communication 54, emergency referrals 50, complex responses 38, global
health 30; its units by number of criteria: one 30, two 134, three 36. Criteria per panel, with the shortest and longest
criterion sentence in words: RiceChem 27 (3–21), HealthBench 34 (7–481), ELLIPSE 6 (27–36), FED-Turn 7 plus
1 binary (4–13), FED-Dialogue 9 plus 1 binary (6–13), HelpSteer2 4 (15–32), LFQA 3 (14–33), and USR-TC and
USR-PC 3 plus 2 binary each (11–43). The ordinal panels hold 3,778 of each matched judge’s 5,003 pairs.
of Table A1. Sampling groups are kept whole. The
four answers to an LFQA question, the responses to
one USR context, and the two responses of a Help-
Steer2 preference pair enter the sample together.
LFQA and the two USR panels have no strata and
are simple random samples of questions or con-
texts. The RiceChem panel is the test part of an
80/10/10 split of each question’s responses, the pro-
portions that Sonkar et al. (2024) used, and Rao
and Callison-Burch (2026) evaluate AutoRubric
on the same held-out set. Its criteria come from
the RiceChem rubrics. HealthBench’s 34 criteria
are consensus criteria, which form the physician-
validated subset of its criteria. They use 30 dis-
tinct texts because some criteria share a text across
themes. HealthBench’s completions are the val-
idation and test sets, 100 completions each, of
HealthBench-100, a sample drawn by a script dis-
tributed with AutoRubric (Rao and Callison-Burch,
2026) and stratified by theme and by whether the
labels of a completion’s criteria are all MET, mixed,
or all UNMET.

Table A2: What each judge receives. {requirement} is the criterion text, verbatim. DMET = “The thing described
in the criterion IS present in the submission”; DUNMET = “The thing described in the criterion IS NOT present
in the submission”; DCA = “Insufficient evidence to determine either way (use rarely)”. Jev’s definitions are
copied verbatim from the harness’s binary system prompt; every criterion has positive weight, so the harness’s
definitions for negative criteria were never used. The wrapper of Section 3.1 is the Jev Noul instruction with
DMET and DUNMET. Bare Noul is reported in Appendices D and R and, where Decider bare Noul ties for the
most accurate judge on HealthBench, in Table 3 and Appendix P. The two LLM rows describe per-criterion
judgments; for whole-rubric judgments the judge receives the same criterion texts and options together (Ap-
pendix N). The LLM judges’ model identifiers, as their APIs report them, aregpt-5.6-luna, gemini-3.8-flash,
and deepseek/deepseek-v4.1-flash, and those of the higher-tier judges (Appendix P) are gpt-5.6-terra,
gemini-3.1-pro-preview, and deepseek/deepseek-v4-pro-0813. The Decider received the five rows above
the rule unchanged, the first asDecider bare Noul, and every reply reported the model that its requests named,
pplx-decider-v1.1-27b.

Leakage.The harness passes a judge only a
unit’s input and its submission, and a decision
model’s state contains only these two fields. To
keep them from leaking, human labels sit in a la-
bel field and holistic scores, strata, and the identity
of the system that produced a unit in a metadata
field, and no judge receives either field. Nor does
any judge see an option’svalue, the number from
0 to 1 that the harness attaches to a level. No or-
dinal panel has a reference submission. Health-
Bench’s physician-written ideal completion is re-
moved from every judge’s input, except in one abla-
tion of Jev Noul and Decider Noul that lies outside
the protocol (Appendix D).
Criterion text.ELLIPSE calls its criteria traits,
and HelpSteer2 calls them attributes. On the ordi-
15

nal panels every judge receives the criterion and
option strings of the rubric versions of Section 3.3.
These versions recast the FED and USR questions
as statements, so a FED question reads “To the
average person, the response is interesting.” Help-
Steer2’s level wording is paraphrased, and LFQA’s
level descriptions come from the rating prompt that
the benchmark’s authors wrote for GPT-4. On EL-
LIPSE the versions keep the level descriptions of
the corpus’s scoring rubric, typographic artifacts
such as “capitalizatio n” and “inappropri ately” in-
cluded, and give each trait a criterion sentence that
ends by labeling level 5 “native-like facility”, a
phrase that the corpus’s rubric uses only for its
holistic top level. Every criterion of the ordinal
panels carries the same weight.
Prior automatic evaluation on these bench-
marks.Earlier automatic evaluation on the di-
alogue sets reports correlations with the rater mean.
FED and USR introduced reference-free metrics on
them (Mehri and Eskenazi, 2020a,b), and G-Eval
and LLM-Eval report LLM evaluators there (Liu
et al., 2023; Lin and Chen, 2023). On ELLIPSE,
supervised models reach a mean QWK of 0.854
on the six analytic traits (Aljuaid et al., 2025),
and zero-shot GPT-4 reaches 0.307 on the holistic
score (Hou et al., 2025), a different target. Cal-
ibration examples help GPT-4 rate short second-
language essays (Yancey et al., 2023). Section 2
cites evidence that essay graders built on LLMs
score below human raters. JUDGE-BENCH, which
includes both USR sets, scores ordinal ratings by
correlation (Bavaresco et al., 2025). A correlation
with the rater mean does not register a constant
shift. We therefore also report exact accuracy and
the offset, for four judges under one protocol.
Labels.Ratings of N/A are dropped before
the mean is taken, which affects FED-Dialogue
error_recovery (Appendix F) and one FED-Turn
fluent pair, whose label rests on four raters. Half-
way means are kept, and Table A1 counts them;
outside ELLIPSE, at most two labels on any panel
are half-way means. ELLIPSE labels average
the two raters’ individual scores rather than tak-
ing the benchmark’s released score. HelpSteer2’s
labels are the released ones (Table A1), not the
per-annotator ratings published separately. On an
embedded binary criterion a tie among the raters
would have removed the pair, and none occurred.
Rater reference.The rater reference of Sec-
tion 3.3 is scored with the same metrics as the
judges and pools over units with at least two raters.
Each rater on a binary criterion is compared with
the majority of the others, and ties are skipped.
Panel exact accuracy pools the ordinal criteria in
proportion to their rater comparisons, and panel
QWK is mean QWK as defined in Section 3.4. The
leave-one-out target of Section 3.3 matters most
when a unit has few raters, since with three raters
it is the mean of two ratings. With three raters per
unit on the USR panels, the reference is 100.0% on
unanimous pairs and 34.4% (USR-TC) or 42.0%
(USR-PC) on split pairs, so its value is set largely
by the share of unanimous pairs. Of the leave-one-
out factuality targets on LFQA, 48.3% are half-
way means of two ratings and round up, whereas
no three-rater label on LFQA is half-way. Label
uncertainty of this kind can distort any comparison
with human agreement (Elangovan et al., 2025).
LLM judges.The harness routes all three LLM
judges through LiteLLM. Each runs as a single
judge in the harness, with a strict JSON response
schema and no few-shot examples. As the harness’s
documentation describes, the shuffle of Section 3.2
draws from a generator seeded with the logged per-
run seed, the submission, the criterion index, and
the judge. It moves the NA option with the others,
and the chosen option is mapped back to its level.
Only the effort runs and the effort calibration’s
explicit-medium runs sent a reasoning or thinking
parameter (Section 3.2; Appendix O); like the main
runs, the higher-tier runs sent none (Appendix P).
All three judges still emitted reasoning tokens in
the main runs, Luna far fewer than the other two.
On the ordinal panels Luna emitted 337,696, Gem-
ini 2,556,073, and DeepSeek 3,046,094. Luna’s
count includes the first run of the spot check de-
scribed below, which shared the response cache.
With the cache on, twelve of Luna’s embedded bi-
nary verdicts were served from that spot check’s
cached responses, and every other recorded verdict
comes from a single pass over its panel.
Tier descriptions.The quotations below,
accessed 27 September 2026, give the vendors’
own descriptions of the flash tier’s speed and price;
Appendix P quotes the vendors on the higher tier,
and Table B1 gives the wall times we measured.
OpenAI called GPT-5.6 Luna “our fastest and
most affordable model” on 30 July 2026 (https:
//openai.com/index/advancing-the-price-
16

performance-frontier-with-gpt-5-6/), and
its model page rates Luna’s speed “Fast” and says
it is “designed for cost-sensitive, high-volume
workloads” (https://developers.openai.com/
api/docs/models/gpt-5.6-luna). Google
offers Gemini 3.8 Flash “with the speed and cost
efficiency of Flash” (https://ai.google.dev/
gemini-api/docs/models/gemini-3.8-flash)
and on 2 September 2026 described it as “often
approaching the performance of higher-cost
frontier models”
(https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/
models-and-research/gemini-models/3-8-
flash-and-3-8-flash-cyber/). DeepSeek
introduced V4.1 Flash with “faster inference” and
“lower API prices” (https://www.deepseek.
com/en/news/deepseek-v4-1-flash/).
System prompts.The LLM judges receive the
harness’s default system prompts (Table A2). The
multi-choice prompt, which they receive for ordi-
nal criteria, tells the judge to “Pay careful attention
to negation, scope, and qualifying language” and to
“Be strict about factual accuracy but flexible about
wording”, and not to retreat to a middle option
when unsure (“Do not default to middle options out
of uncertainty”). When a submission contradicts
itself and no stance dominates on an ordered scale,
the prompt asks for the lower-quality level (“se-
lect the option reflecting the weaker (lower-quality)
reading”). It reserves the NA option for submis-
sions that point to inaccessible content, cannot be
judged on the question, or are too garbled to eval-
uate, and it warns against choosing NA when the
judge is uncertain but has some evidence.
Routing and failures.OpenRouter’s default rout-
ing spread DeepSeek’s main-run requests on the or-
dinal panels over 11 upstream providers. DeepSeek
returned an empty response on six ordinal pairs
(four on ELLIPSE, one on HelpSteer2, and one
on LFQA), which are excluded from every ordinal
metric. It did the same on one RiceChem pair and
two HealthBench pairs. These three are the one
exception to the exclusion of judge-call failures
from the denominators, because our scoring of the
binary panels took the UNMET verdict that the har-
ness recorded for each failed per-criterion request
of DeepSeek and did not check the failure recorded
with it. They count as verdicts in the analyses of
the main runs (Table F1). No request to either de-
cision model failed, and Appendix R counts the
Decider’s retries. Appendix N gives the routing
and the failures of the whole-rubric requests, Ap-
pendix O those of the effort runs, and Appendix P
those of the higher-tier runs.
What the decision models receive.The two
fields of Jev’s state (Section 3.1) are namedinput
and submission, matching the tags that mark the
input and the submission in the LLM judges’ user
message (Table A2). The input is the unit’s context.
It is the preceding conversation on FED-Turn and
HealthBench, the conversation with the fact or per-
sona the response is conditioned on for the USR
panels, the user’s prompt on HelpSteer2, the ques-
tion on LFQA and RiceChem, and the title of the
essay prompt on ELLIPSE. On FED-Dialogue it is
the fixed sentence “A conversation between a User
and a System.” The same code built the Decider’s
requests, so they carry the same state and questions
as Jev’s. Perplexity’s API takes a request body of
the same form as TypeSafe’s, with fields for the
model name, the state, and the named questions.
Unlike the LLM judges, neither decision model
receives the criterion type. Appendix R describes
the Decider’s client, rate limit, retries, and repeat.
Run-to-run variation of Jev.Requests named
the jev-latest alias, and the main runs did not
log the version that answered them. A stability
study on the day of the main runs logged it: five
interleaved passes over the RiceChem panel and a
59-pair HealthBench subset, under all three binary
framings, used 2,250 requests, and every one of
them reports jev-1.13.0. On RiceChem, 2.9% of
pairs changed verdict at least once under Jev Noul
and 3.3% under Jev Choice. Under every framing,
each change occurred on a pair whose mean prob-
ability of MET lay within 0.1 of 0.5 (Figure D1b
shows Jev Noul). Across passes, the standard de-
viation of accuracy was 0.36 points for Jev Noul
on RiceChem, 0.76 points on the HealthBench sub-
set, and at most 0.93 points for any framing. The
share of pairs with identical probabilities in all five
passes ranged from 4.2% of RiceChem pairs under
bare Noul to 35.8% under Jev Choice. The median
request latency was 0.18 to 0.20 seconds in every
pass.
Repeated Luna judgments.In a spot check be-
fore the main runs, Luna judged 82 pairs twice,
all the pairs of two units from each ordinal panel,
the second time with the response cache bypassed.
The two runs agreed on 71 of the 82 pairs (86.6%).
Luna’s main run is a later, separate pass and was
17

not compared with either. The second default runs
of Appendix O later repeated every LLM judge’s
per-criterion judgments on the 106 repeat units.
B Cost and Wall Time
How cost is computed.Section 3.4 gives the cost
rule behind Table B1. Each decision model’s cost
is the input-token count that its API reported for
each request, at its price in Section 3.1. The De-
cider’s counts agree to the token across its run sum-
maries, its records of each unit, and a log of every
request. For Luna and Gemini, cost is LiteLLM’s
completion cost, computed for each request from
the token counts the API reported, reasoning to-
kens included. Gemini’s recorded cost is repro-
duced exactly by $0.75 per million prompt tokens
and $3.75 per million completion tokens, and its
prompt-token price is about 18 times Jev’s. A re-
sponse served from the cache carries the cost of its
original request. Over the seven ordinal panels Jev
Choice cost $0.043 and Jev Score $0.042, about
the same. Appendix R gives the Decider’s costs
and wall times on each panel, and the launch price
in Perplexity’s changelog.
Prices of the higher tier.The costs of the higher-
tier runs (Appendix P) rest on the vendors’ list
prices of 2 October 2026. In US dollars per million
uncached input, cached input, and output tokens,
Terra and Gemini 3.1 Pro both list at $2.00, $0.20,
and $12.00, and DeepSeek V4 Pro at $0.66, $0.022,
and $1.98 at DeepSeek’s off-peak rates. OpenAI
also charges $2.50 per million prompt tokens writ-
ten to its prompt cache for Terra and $0.25 for Luna,
whose other prices are $0.20, $0.02, and $1.20.
DeepSeek V4.1 Flash lists at $0.15, $0.003, and
$0.60, and Gemini 3.8 Flash at the two prices given
above. Priced in this way, the recorded costs of
Luna, Terra, and Gemini 3.1 Pro are reproduced ex-
actly, and Table P1 uses these list prices. DeepSeek
V4 Pro, like DeepSeek, is reported at its Open-
Router bill.
DeepSeek’s billed cost.A panel’s billed cost
sums the costs that OpenRouter reported with each
response. The harness’s own estimate prices 4,747
of the 4,994 answered requests at DeepSeek’s list
prices, whichever upstream provider served them,
and it is lower (Table B1, last column). The billed
cost is 1.36 times the estimate on RiceChem and
1.23 times on HealthBench. On single ordinal pan-
els the factor runs from 1.31 to 1.46, and it is 1.34
over the seven together. The billed totals exclude
the six failed requests on the ordinal panels and
the three failed binary ones, which left no cached
response.
Criteria per unit and unit length.Across the or-
dinal panels, Gemini’s cost ratio rises with every in-
crease in criteria per unit, from 255× on LFQA (3.0
criteria per unit) to 770× on FED-Dialogue (10.0).
Over all nine panels it runs from 121× on Health-
Bench (2.0 criteria per unit) to FED-Dialogue’s
770×. RiceChem departs from that order. With 6.8
criteria per unit, its ratio (326×) is below those of
ELLIPSE and both USR panels, which have fewer.
The Luna and DeepSeek ratios follow the order less
closely. Among Luna’s ratios, ELLIPSE’s (29×) is
below HelpSteer2’s (33×), and among DeepSeek’s
billed ratios, HelpSteer2’s (65×) is above those of
both USR panels (62× and 59×), although Help-
Steer2 has fewer criteria per unit than either. Unit
length has little effect. ELLIPSE’s essays are the
longest ordinal units and the USR responses among
the shortest (Table A1), yet with one criterion per
unit more, ELLIPSE’s Gemini ratio (396×) is close
to the USR panels’ (373×and 349×).
Output and reasoning tokens.For each crite-
rion of a unit, Gemini writes an explanation and
spends reasoning tokens in either judging mode,
all billed at its output price, whereas a criterion
adds only a question to Jev’s request and nothing
to Jev’s output, which is free. With per-criterion
judgments each criterion also puts another copy
of the system prompt and of the unit’s text into
Gemini’s prompt tokens, none of which Gemini
reported as cached (Appendix N). The number of
requests alone does not set the order of Gemini’s
ratios, since its whole-rubric ratios, at one request
per unit as for Jev, mostly keep that order (Ap-
pendix N). Gemini’s per-token price and its reason-
ing tokens widen its cost ratio. Completion tokens,
which include reasoning tokens, make up 60.0%
of Gemini’s per-criterion bill over the nine panels,
$6.66 of $9.46 (70.4%) on ELLIPSE, and 47.8%
of its $1.64 on FED-Turn.
Wall time.Wall time was measured around each
panel’s evaluation and includes checkpoint writes.
On the ordinal panels each judge ran in its own pro-
cess. The three LLM processes ran simultaneously,
each against its own provider, and the Jev Choice
and Jev Score processes ran together against Jev’s
API. On each binary panel the LLM judges ran

C Paired Comparisons and Secondary
Metrics
Section 4.1 rests on the unit-level comparisons of
Table C1. Table C2 adds every pair-level sign test,
and Table C3 gives secondary metrics for the or-
dinal panels, with rows for both decision models.
The comparisons of the first two tables are Jev’s,
and Appendix R gives the Decider’s.
Comparisons by panel.Among its matched
judges, Jev Choice ranks first on RiceChem and sec-
ond on HealthBench, behind Gemini (Table 1), and
is separated from Luna on both and from Gemini on
RiceChem (Table C1). On the ordinal panels 16 of
21 comparisons are not separated; of the other five,
Gemini leads on ELLIPSE, FED-Dialogue, and
HelpSteer2, most on ELLIPSE (16.4 points), and
Luna leads on USR-PC and trails on HelpSteer2.
DeepSeek is never separated from Jev Choice.
Pair sets.Because abstentions and judge-call fail-
ures leave the denominators (Section 3.4), two
judges’ accuracies share a denominator only in
a paired comparison. The pair set also differs be-
tween analyses, so one judge’s accuracy on a panel
differs between tables. Table 1 counts an ordi-
nal panel’s ordinal pairs. The comparisons of Jev
Choice in Table C1 add its embedded binary pairs
and keep the pairs both judges scored. Table 3
and the cascade analyses of Appendix K count ev-
ery pair that the decision model in the first stage
answered, keeping its verdict where the cascade’s
fallback judge (Section 6) abstained. The one kind
of judge-call failure that stays in the denominators,
DeepSeek’s on the binary panels, is described in
Appendix A.
Pair-level sign tests.For two judges, the test
counts the pairs both scored that only the first got

Table C1: Paired comparisons with units resampled (Section 3.4). Diff: the second judge’s accuracy minus Jev
Choice’s, in points, on the pairs both scored; on the ordinal panels the upper block includes the embedded binary
pairs (Section 3.1), and the lower block uses ordinal pairs only. 95% interval: percentile interval over 10,000
resamples of units. Bound: the smallest symmetric margin, in points, that contains the 90% interval. J : O: units on
which Jev Choice is ahead against units on which the other judge is ahead, and punit the exact two-sided sign test
over them, with tied units set aside. Class: separated (Section 3.4), parity (Section 3.4), both, or neither (–). Parity
needs a bound below 5 points, so USR-PC Jev Score, with a bound of exactly 5.0, is not at parity. ‡: the bootstrap
p-value survives Holm correction within its block of 27 or 7 comparisons.
right and those that only the second got right, and
applies an exact two-sided binomial test with prob-
ability 0.5 to these discordant counts, the exact
McNemar test. Jev Choice is tested on the pair
sets of Table C1. Tests involving Jev Score use
ordinal pairs only, since on embedded binary pairs
Jev Choice and Jev Score both answer through
Jev Noul (Section 3.1). Unlike the intervals of
Table C1, which resample units because the pairs
of one unit share its text, these tests ignore that
clustering.
Unit-level analysis.Each comparison of Ta-

generator. The bootstrap intervals separate as many
of the 27 comparisons as the uncorrected pair-level
tests do, four in each direction, and seven of the
eight separations coincide. The exception is Gem-
ini’s pair-level lead on FED-Turn ( p= 0.032 ),
which the intervals do not separate. Of its 75 turns,
17 favor Jev Choice, 25 favor Gemini, and 33 are
tied (punit = 0.28). The intervals separate Luna’s
lead on USR-PC instead, whose pair-levelp is 0.10.

also separated, so at a 7.5-point margin they would

Resampling by sampling group.The sampling
groups are the 45 HelpSteer2 prompts, the 30
LFQA questions, and the 12 contexts of each USR
panel. Resampling them in place of units widens
nine of the twelve intervals with an LLM judge on
these panels and changes four outcomes. Luna’s
lead on USR-PC is no longer separated, since its
interval now runs from −0.3 to +8.7 points. Jev
Choice’s lead over DeepSeek on LFQA becomes
separated, with an interval from −6.5 to −0.6.
USR-TC Luna loses parity with a bound of 5.3
points, and LFQA Gemini with one of 5.1. That
leaves eight separations, five of them Jev Choice’s
leads and the other three all Gemini’s. With 12 to
45 groups these percentile intervals are rough.
Jev Score against Jev Choice.The unit sign test
favors Jev Score on ELLIPSE, FED-Turn, and FED-
Dialogue, and Holm correction keeps the first two.
The bootstrap intervals also separate USR-TC (Ap-
pendix E). The ELLIPSE difference of 1.9 points
is both separated and at parity.
Multiple comparisons.Bootstrap p-values,
twice the smaller share of replicates on either side
of zero, are Holm-corrected within each family
of comparisons, such as Jev Choice’s 27 with the
LLM judges. Decider Choice’s 27 form a family
of their own (Section 3.4), so adding the Decider
changed no corrected value in Jev’s copy, and
Appendix R gives the Decider’s. Holm correction
multiplies the i-th smallest of the 27 p-values
of Jev Choice against an LLM judge by 28−i ,
takes a running maximum, and caps the result at
1. Applied to the bootstrap p-values, it keeps four
separations, two in each direction (‡ in Table C1):
Gemini’s leads on ELLIPSE and on FED-Dialogue,
and Jev Choice’s leads over Luna on HelpSteer2
and over Gemini on RiceChem. Of the pair-level
tests it keeps three, Gemini’s lead on ELLIPSE
and Jev Choice’s leads on HelpSteer2 against Luna
(pHolm = 0.011) and on RiceChem against Gemini
(pHolm = 0.016 ). Gemini’s FED-Dialogue lead,
which remains significant when the bootstrap p-
values are corrected, has a pair-level adjusted value
of 0.19. The other 34 pair-level tests in Table C2
are reported without correction. Among them, Jev
Score is significantly more accurate than an LLM
judge in 7 of its 21 comparisons on ordinal pairs
and significantly less accurate in 2, both against
Gemini, on ELLIPSE and HelpSteer2. These 21
comparisons were not repeated at the unit level.
Secondary metrics.Within-one accuracy(Ta-
ble C3a) is exact accuracy with a one-level miss
also counted as correct. In Jev’s copy, every off-
set outside USR-TC is negative against the un-
rounded rater mean, as it is against the rounded
label, so the rounding rule does not produce the
sign of the offset (Table C3c). Each Decider fram-
ing’s offset also has the same sign against both
targets on every panel, although on ELLIPSE the
offsets of both framings turn positive against la-
bels rounded down (Section 5.1; Appendix G). Jev
Noul’s MET rate over the embedded binary pairs
(74.2%) is close to the labels’ (74.7%), largely
because two USR-TC criteria err in opposite di-
rections, understandable with 17 false positives
and uses_knowledge with 16 false negatives. The
unit aggregate correlates with HelpSteer2’s helpful-
ness score at Spearman 0.12 to 0.27 for Jev’s five
judges, 0.31 for Decider Choice, and 0.40 for De-
cider Score, against 0.44 to 0.92 for every judge of
either copy on the other ordinal panels (Table C3d),
although HelpSteer2’s correctness label alone
tracks helpfulness (Table H1).
D The Binary Panels
Framings and the full RiceChem set.Table D1
gives the three Jev framings of Table A2, the De-
cider’s three, and the three LLM judges on both
binary panels.Bare Noulis Jev Noul without the
wrapper (Section 3.1). Under every framing both
decision models also judged thefull RiceChem set,
all 1,240 responses to the four RiceChem questions
(8,392 pairs), on which no LLM judge was run.
Jev Noul against Jev Choice.Jev Noul and Jev
Choice return nearly the same verdicts, with a
Cohen’s κ of 0.945 between them over the full
RiceChem set. On the panels their accuracies differ
by 0.9 points (RiceChem) and 0.2 (HealthBench).
The wrapper matters more than which primitive is
asked, adding 10.7 points to bare Noul’s accuracy
on RiceChem but only 1.0 on HealthBench.
The wrapper on RiceChem.On the full set the
wrapper’s gain is 9.6 points, against 10.7 on the
panel. The gain comes from fewer false positives.
Bare Noul predicts MET on 78.4% of the panel’s
pairs against a labeled rate of 59.5%. The wrap-
per lowers the false-positive rate from 61.1% to
34.9% and leaves the false-negative rate near 10%.

models only); false-positive and false-negative rates; false positives against false negatives; predicted MET rate;
expected calibration error of P(MET) (decision models only; ten equal-width bins on RiceChem, eight on Health-
Bench); cost. P(MET) is Noul’s returned probability of yes and, for a Choice framing, the returned probability
of the MET option. The MET-rate offset (Section 3.4) is +8.4 points on RiceChem and +6.4 on HealthBench
for Jev Noul, and +3.4 and +10.3 for Decider Noul. DeepSeek’s three failed requests count as UNMET verdicts

Per criterion on the full set, the largest gain, 43.2
points, is on the nine-word criterion “Explaining
sentence 2: any additional energy becomes kinetic
energy”. It states content without saying what to
check, which may be why the definitions help there.
Only two of the 27 criteria lose accuracy under the
wrapper, by 5.0 and 2.1 points.
The wrapper on HealthBench.Of Health-
Bench’s 34 consensus criteria, the wrapper raises
accuracy on 5, lowers it on 9, and leaves 20 un-
changed. A 466-word context-seeking criterion
of 23 pairs gains the most, rising from 39.1% to
82.6%, yet two other context-seeking criteria of
similar length each lose 10.0 points. Criterion
length does not predict the direction of the effect,
since the criteria that lose accuracy run from 32
to 481 words. The three largest losses, of 20.0 to
30.8 points, fall on criteria of 13 or fewer pairs.
The largest is on a global-health criterion that is
met when the response contains no inaccuracies
that could lead to harm. MET there means that
something is absent, which the wrapper’s definition
of MET as something “present in the submission”
may not capture. These differences rest on 4 to
25 pairs per criterion and show only that a fixed
wrapper can lower accuracy on individual criteria.
The wrapper with the Decider.Sent to the
Decider unchanged, the wrapper raised Decider
Noul’s accuracy over Decider bare Noul by 3.4
points on the RiceChem panel and 5.1 on the full
set, but lowered it by 7.6 points on HealthBench,
where Decider bare Noul outscores both other De-
cider framings (Table D1).
CANNOT_ASSESS.Jev Choice did not choose
CANNOT_ASSESS on any of the 8,392 RiceChem
pairs or the 406 HealthBench pairs. Its highest
probability for that option was 0.29 on RiceChem
and 0.17 on HealthBench. Decider Choice did
not choose it either, with highest probabilities of

Direction of error.Jev Noul’s false positives
outnumber its false negatives by 2.5 to 1 on the
RiceChem panel, 1.8 to 1 on HealthBench (Ta-
ble D1), and 3.1 to 1 on the full RiceChem set.
Decider Noul’s errors lean the same way, with
1.4 false positives for every false negative on the
RiceChem panel, 1.8 on the full RiceChem set, and
2.2 on HealthBench. The LLM judges differ in
direction. On RiceChem Luna and DeepSeek err
toward MET and Gemini toward UNMET. Luna
errs toward UNMET on HealthBench, DeepSeek
slightly so, and Gemini’s errors there are balanced.
Probabilities.Discrimination and calibration
need a probability for each verdict, so they are
reported for the decision models alone. For ev-
ery Jev framing, the AUROC of P(MET) between
MET and UNMET pairs is 0.80 to 0.86 on the
two panels, and the expected calibration error is
0.056 to 0.096 there and 0.062 to 0.089 over all
8,392 RiceChem pairs (Table D1). Jev Noul’s error
rate falls as |P(MET)−0.5| , its distance from one
half, grows. In the band nearest 0.5 it is 46.1% on
the full RiceChem set and 49.2% on HealthBench,
and at margins of 0.4 or more it is below 10%
(Figure D1a). Figure D1b shows where verdicts
changed in the stability study of Appendix A. For
the Decider’s framings the AUROC is 0.78 to 0.89
and the expected calibration error 0.084 to 0.148
on the panels, and 0.056 to 0.107 over the full set.
On HealthBench, Decider Noul is overconfident at
both ends of the probability scale (Appendix R).
A threshold set on training data.The Jev Noul
threshold was also set, post hoc, to maximize accu-
racy on the 80% training portion of RiceChem. The
chosen threshold, 0.58, gives 81.5% on the training
portion. On the panel it gives 78.9%, below the
80.1% of the fixed threshold of 0.5.
Non-English completions.The completion is
not in English on 36 of HealthBench’s 406 pairs,
and the criterion is in English on every pair. Jev
Noul is correct on 77.8% of the non-English pairs,
against 77.3% on the English ones. The three LLM
judges are less accurate there, by 7.2 points (Luna),
11.1 (Gemini), and 7.6 (DeepSeek). With 36 pairs,
the standard error of each non-English accuracy is
at least 6.9 points, so no single difference is estab-
lished, and the common direction across the three
LLM judges is suggestive only.
A physician reference.Because HealthBench
records each physician’s label, the binary rater
reference of Appendix A can also be computed
for the physicians. Over all 406 pairs this gives
98.1%, an artifact of selection. Pairs on which
the physicians tied were removed from the pool
before sampling. All 356 two-physician pairs are
therefore unanimous, and they contribute 712 of
the 838 comparisons, every one an agreement. The
50 pairs with three or more physicians, from 20
completions, are free of this selection. On them the
physician reference is 87.3% (95% interval 79.7 to
93.3 over completions), with κ= 0.75 . Against
the same leave-one-out targets, Jev’s framings and
the LLM judges reach 73.0% (bare Noul) to 84.1%
(Jev Choice), which is 3.2 to 14.3 points below
the reference. The Decider’s three framings reach
89.7% (Decider bare Noul and Decider Noul) to
91.3% (Decider Choice), 2.4 to 4.0 points above the
reference. Every judge’s 95% interval for its differ-
ence from the reference reaches zero, the Decider’s
framings included, so these pairs show only that no
judge is detectably below or above the physicians.
The physician-written ideal.In an ablation out-
side the protocol, HealthBench’s physician-written
ideal completion was added to Jev Noul’s state, and
accuracy rose from 77.3% to 79.1%. Because no
LLM judge received the ideal, Jev’s result enters no
comparison. Decider Noul also received the ideal,
but we report no accuracy from those requests and
use their answers only to check the Decider’s repeat
(Table R6).

E Choice Against Score
This appendix analyzes Jev’s framings alone; Ap-
pendix R compares Decider Score with Decider
Choice.
Accuracy of the two framings.Jev Score and
Jev Choice are separated on four ordinal panels,
ELLIPSE, FED-Turn, FED-Dialogue, and USR-
TC, with Jev Score the more accurate on each
(Table C1). Jev Score’s within-one accuracy is
the higher of the two on all seven ordinal panels
(Table C3a), while its exact accuracy is lower on
HelpSteer2 and USR-PC, with neither difference
separated. Only on FED-Turn does the difference
between the framings, 10.5 points, exceed the 2.9-
point range of the three LLM judges (Table 1). The
exchange of decoding rules below places most or
all of that difference in the probabilities the two
primitives return rather than in the rules that read
them.
Framings against the LLM judges.Both bi-
nary framings lie above the LLM judges’ range
on RiceChem and inside it on HealthBench (Fig-
ure E1). Among the ordinal panels, Jev Choice
falls below that range on ELLIPSE, FED-Turn,
and USR-PC, and Jev Score only on USR-PC. Jev
Score rises above it on FED-Turn and USR-TC.
On the remaining ordinal panels each framing lies
inside the range.
The middle level on FED-Turn.FED-Turn’s
ordinal criteria have three levels, and the two fram-
ings differ most in how often they choose the mid-
dle one, “Somewhat.” Jev Score chooses it on
29.1% of pairs and Jev Choice on 11.2%, where
the averaged labels place 32.6% (Table E1). In-
dividual raters chose it for 26.5% of their ratings,
less often than the labels. Jev Choice, Gemini, and
DeepSeek use the middle level least. On correct
and fluent, two criteria that state a yes-or-no prop-
erty, Jev Choice never predicts “Somewhat.” in 75
pairs each.
Metrics on FED-Turn.On FED-Turn, Jev Score
is ahead of every matched judge in exact accuracy,
within-one accuracy, mean QWK, and mean ab-
solute error (Tables 1 and C3). It does not lead
on offset, where Gemini’s is smallest (Tables G1
and C3h), or on the unit aggregate’s rank correla-
tion with the holistic score, where Jev Choice has
0.603 and Jev Score 0.597. Scored against the same
leave-one-out targets as a rater, Jev Score reaches
