Exchanging the decoding rules.In a post-hoc
analysis, the bottom of Table E1 exchanges the two
decoding rules on FED-Turn’s ordinal pairs. Jev
Choice’s probabilities over the three levels, with
the NA probability removed and the rest renor-
malized, give 55.4% exact accuracy when read by
Score’s rule, the rounded expected level (rounding
halves up or to even gives the same 55.4%). Read
by argmax, as Jev Choice runs, they give 56.2%.
Jev Score’s probabilities give 64.6% when read
by argmax and 66.7% when read by its own ex-
pected level. Under the argmax rule, exchanging
the probabilities accounts for 80% of the 10.5-point
difference between the framings as run. Under
Score’s rule the share is 107%, because that rule
does slightly worse than argmax on Jev Choice’s
probabilities. Averaged over pairs, Jev Choice puts
more probability than Jev Score on “No.” (0.316
against 0.205) and less on “Somewhat.” (0.171
against 0.210) and “Yes.” (0.513 against 0.585).
What Score computes.The vendor documenta-
tion states that for Score “every level is evaluated
separately” and that the model does not see a level’s
number or its neighbors. It also describes Score’s
levels as weak in numerical calibration.5 Score’s
expected level thus weights the level numbers by
probabilities obtained from level descriptions eval-
uated one at a time. We do not read Jev Score’s
advantage as interpolation along an ordered scale.
The exchange of decoding rules locates the differ-
ence in the probabilities but says nothing about how
they arise.
Disagreements on ELLIPSE.Jev Choice and
Jev Score give different levels on 215 of ELLIPSE’s
1,548 pairs and never differ by more than one level.
Jev Score’s level is the higher one on 179 of the
215. On the 215 disagreements Jev Choice is ex-
act on 8.4% and Jev Score on 21.9%. Because
each of Jev’s five judges places ELLIPSE essays
below their labels (Section 5.1), the framing that
answers one level higher gains exact accuracy on
its disagreements.
Rounding of Jev Score.Score returns an ex-
pected level on levels numbered from 0, which Jev
Score rounds half to even (Section 3.1), whereas
5https://docs.typesafe.ai/primitives/score and
https://docs.typesafe.ai/model-jaggedness/jev-1.
13.
the labels round halves up (Section 3.3). Of Jev
Score’s 3,414 answers on the ordinal panels, 27 are
exact halves, and the two rules give different levels
on 12 of them. Ten lie halfway between the first
and second level and two halfway between the third
and fourth, where half to even rounds down. The
other 15 halves lie between the second and third
level, where both rules round up.
Embedded binary answers.Jev Choice and Jev
Score obtain their Jev Noul verdicts on embed-
ded binary pairs (Section 3.1) in separate requests.
These verdicts differ on 4 of the 364 pairs and
change a panel’s binary accuracy only on USR-PC
(Table C3f).
F Abstention
Where Jev Choice abstains.Jev Choice’s 28 ab-
stentions, among 3,414 ordinal pairs (Table F1),
fall into three groups. The largest, 17 FED-
Dialogue error_recovery pairs, is discussed be-
low. The eight HelpSteer2 correctness pairs are
mostly responses with little checkable factual con-
tent: two answer a request for “a long list of wacky
and wild superpowers”, one is the reply “You’re
welcome! I’m glad I could help.”, three offer emo-
tional support, and one is a role-play reply. The
remaining response, a blog outline naming brew-
eries in Rogers, Arkansas, does contain checkable
facts. Six of the eight are labeled with the top level,
“All pertinent facts are included and the response
contains no errors.” HelpSteer2’s annotators had
no abstain option. The last group holds three pairs.
Two LFQA factuality answers, both written by
Reddit users, make no factual claim (one is a one-
line joke, the other advice to search before post-
ing). A USR-PC engaging response, “why did you
choose pink out of all colors ?”, can be assessed
and was labeled “Interesting”, the top level.
Raters’ N/A answers.FED offered its raters
an N/A answer on every question. On
error_recovery (“The system is able to recover
from errors that it makes.”), the one criterion on
which they often gave it, 29 of the 125 sampled rat-
ings (23.2%) are N/A answers, most of them with a
note that the system made no error. The more N/A
answers a dialogue received, the more probability
Jev Choice put on the NA option (Figure F1; Spear-
man ρ= 0.62 over 25 dialogues, p= 9×10 −4).
Jev Choice abstained on all 7 dialogues with two
or more N/A answers, on 7 of the 8 with one, and

Figure F1: FED-Dialogue error_recovery: Jev

The conditional label.Jev Choice answered the
other 8 error_recovery dialogues, chose “No.”
on each, and was wrong every time. Four of them
carry the label “Somewhat.” and four the label
“Yes.” Decider Choice likewise answered “No.” on
all 7 error_recovery dialogues it did not abstain
on, and none of these answers was right. The label
on this criterion averages only the numeric ratings.
Because most N/A answers report that no system
error occurred, the numeric ratings likely come
mainly from raters who saw an error to recover
from. If so, the label rates recovery under a condi-
tion that the criterion text does not state.
Two kinds of abstain option.The binary and or-
dinal panels offered different abstain options, and
Jev Choice used only the ordinal one. On the binary
panels the option was CANNOT_ASSESS, whose
definition in Table A2 describes the evidence avail-
able to the judge and asks for rare use, and no judge
chose it there (Appendix D). The ordinal panels of-
fered the NA option instead. Its wording can also
say that the criterion does not apply to the unit, and
Jev Choice’s abstentions fall mostly on pairs where
that reading fits. The two options differ further
in the instruction to use the option rarely, in scale
type, and in panel, so this contrast does not isolate
the wording. Offering one wording on both kinds
of panel would separate the wording from these
differences, but we made no such run.

G The Offset in Detail
This appendix analyzes Jev’s copy of the study
(Section 3.1), and its figures are drawn from that
copy. Where a sentence or table row names the
Decider, it reports the same quantity from the De-
cider’s own copy. Table G1 lists the offset of every
matched judge of either copy on each of the nine
panels.
Location.A judge’slocationis where on the
scale it places a criterion’s units. Over the 31 crite-
ria with a rater reference, the matched judges’ mean
offset tracks their mean gap (Spearman ρ= 0.92 ;
Figure G1), in part by construction, since an offset
costs exact accuracy. A leave-one-out shift of each
criterion’s predictions removes the relation (Leave-
one-out and best shifts, below). The Decider’s
copy gives 0.90 before that shift and 0.17 after it
(p= 0.35 ). The offset leaves the essays’ order
largely intact (ρ= 0.62 to 0.72 with ELLIPSE’s
holistic score; Table C3d), and moving every EL-
LIPSE prediction up a level lifts Jev Choice from
13.4% to 50.4%, though the raters need no shift
(Figure G2), and neither do the Decider’s framings
(Constant shift, below).
Criteria far below the reference.Figure G1
plots, criterion by criterion, the relation between
offset and gap described under Location. The
shortfall is concentrated in a minority of crite-
ria. With cut-offs chosen only to describe the
pattern, 12 of the 31 lie more than 15 points
below the reference, and 11 of these have a
mean offset below −0.5 levels. They are the
five ELLIPSE traits other than cohesion; FED-
Dialogue depth, error_recovery, informative,
and inquisitive; FED-Turn interesting; and
USR-PC engaging. The twelfth, FED-Turn
engaging, has a mean offset of −0.34 levels. Con-
versely, none of the 15 criteria whose mean offset
lies within 0.3 levels of zero is more than 6 points
below the reference (the shaded band of Figure G1).
The four matched judges also agree on the sign
of the offset, which is negative for all four on 25
of the 35 ordinal criteria, HelpSteer2’s included,
and positive for all four on 2. In the Decider’s
copy the same 12 criteria lie more than 15 points
below the reference, and FED-Turn engaging is
again the only one whose mean offset is not be-
low −0.5 levels, at −0.45. Its 16 criteria with a
mean offset within 0.3 levels of zero include one
more than 6 points below the reference, FED-Turn

Leave-one-out and best shifts.Both shifts move
every prediction a judge makes on a criterion, post
hoc, by the whole number of levels that maximizes
its exact accuracy, clipped to the scale. Thebest
shiftpicks that number on all of the criterion’s pairs.
The leave-one-out shift used under Location picks
it for each pair on the criterion’s other pairs, so
a pair’s own label never sets its shift. After the
leave-one-out shift the mean gap no longer tracks
the mean offset (Spearman ρ= 0.16 , p= 0.40 ;
Table G3), and no criterion stays more than 8.4
points below the unshifted reference. Comparing
a shifted judge with the unshifted rater reference
favors the judge where most labels sit at the top of a
three-level scale. There a one-level shift, clipped at
the top, partly amounts to predicting the top level,
which would raise a rater’s agreement as well.
Gap measures that ignore location.Table G3
compares the gap measure of Figure G1 with
four that a constant shift cannot move. The
unshifted correlation does not rest on ELLIPSE
or on error_recovery. It stays at 0.887 with-
out ELLIPSE (25 criteria) and at 0.918 without

from the unrounded rater mean, every offset is
smaller in magnitude, by 0.19 to 0.27 levels, which
is the amount by which the half-up rule raises each
trait’s labels.

10.7% of its ELLIPSE predictions at these levels,
and 19.4% of its conventions predictions. De-
cider Score places 10.8%, and at most 15.9% of
one trait’s (syntax). At the bottom of the scale the
pattern reverses. The rounded label is level 1 on
one pair in 1,548, and the individual raters used
level 1 on 11 of 3,096 ratings, whereas Jev’s five
judges predicted it on 30 (Gemini) to 376 (Luna)
pairs, ongrammarmore than on any other trait.
Compression.The judges compress the scale.
Pooled over the 1,548 pairs, the regression slope of
Table G2d runs from 0.29 (Jev Score) to 0.44 (Gem-
ini), with Jev Choice at 0.37. The same regression
of one rater’s level on the other’s, over 3,096 or-
dered pairs of ratings, gives 0.50. This slope is
below one because the two raters disagree. It is
not a like-for-like benchmark for the judges, whose
target, the two-rater label, is less noisy than a single
rating. A judge with one rater’s noise would show
a slope above 0.50 against the label, so a compari-
son with 0.50 understates the judges’ compression.
Per trait, the judges’ slopes run from 0.14 to 0.54
and the raters’ from 0.44 to 0.54 (Table G2d). Jev
Choice’s within-one accuracy is 64.3%, and one
rater is within one level of the other on 98.2% of
ratings. For labels of level 3 to level 5, Jev Choice
most often predicts level 2, and Gemini most often
predicts one or two levels below the label (Fig-
ure G3).
Rank order.Averaged over traits, Jev Choice’s
Spearman correlation with the unrounded rater
mean is 0.481, and one rater’s correlation with the
other is 0.483. By this measure Jev Choice ranks
essays about as well as a single rater. As with the
slopes, the comparison favors the judge.
Holistic band.The ELLIPSE sample was strati-
fied by bands of the holistic score, with 432 pairs at
2.5 or below, 888 at 3 to 3.5, and 228 at 4 or above.
In both copies every judge’s offset grows more neg-
ative from band to band, from −1.02 levels in the
lowest band to −1.63 in the highest for Jev Choice,
from −0.34 to −0.68 for Decider Choice, and from
−0.59 to −1.09 for Gemini. Compression predicts
this pattern. When predictions rise by less than
one level per label level, the offset grows with the
label. The growth in Jev’s copy is consistent with
the population-norm account of Appendix H, but
it does not by itself distinguish that account from
other causes of compression.
32

Constant shift.A post-hoc analysis moves every
prediction by s levels, clipped to the scale (Fig-
ure G2). For each of Jev’s five judges, exact accu-
racy peaks at s= +1 , at 46.8% (Luna) to 59.6%
(Gemini) against the rater reference’s 51.4%, and
falls to at most 33.5% at s= +2 and below 2% at
s=−1 . Their mean QWK also peaks at s= +1 ,
at 0.31 to 0.48, below the rater reference’s 0.50,
and Jev Choice’s rises there from 0.15 to 0.34.
Apart from clipping, a constant shift leaves the
regression slopes above unchanged. The raters’
own best shift on ELLIPSE is zero, so for these
judges the shift corrects only their location. The
Decider’s framings, like the raters and unlike the
three LLM judges, are most accurate unshifted. De-
cider Choice reaches 47.1% at s= 0 and 45.6% at
s= +1 , and Decider Score 51.0% at s= 0 and
41.3% ats= +1.
Rounding.Half-up rounding of the labels ac-
counts for about 0.23 levels of Jev Choice’s EL-
LIPSE offset. Table G4 isolates the rounding rule
of Section 3.3. Every judge of either copy is less ac-
curate on the half-way labels than on the others, Jev
Choice at 6.8% against 19.3% and Decider Choice
at 30.9% against 61.4%. Against the unrounded
rater mean, four of Jev’s five judges are 0.92 to
1.04 levels low and Gemini is 0.54 levels low.
Rounding the half-way means down instead, as a
post-hoc analysis, changes the order of two of Jev’s
five judges. Jev Choice then overtakes Luna (29.1%
against 28.6% exact), and Gemini’s lead over Jev
Choice grows from 16.4 to 28.4 points. The down-
ward rule lowers each half-way label by one level,
which raises every offset by 0.47 levels (one level
times the half-way share of 46.8%). That is less
than the smallest offset magnitude among Jev’s five
judges, Gemini’s 0.77 levels, so all five stay neg-
ative. It is more than either Decider framing’s, so
the Decider’s ELLIPSE offset changes sign with
the rounding rule. Decider Choice’s offset is−0.46
levels under the half-up rule and +0.004 under the
downward one, and Decider Score’s moves from
−0.40to+0.07.
USR engaging.Table G5 gives each judge’s ex-
act accuracy on USRengaging before and after the
post-hoc shift of Appendix H, Jev Score included.
On PersonaChat every shifted judge exceeds the
unshifted rater reference, a comparison that favors
the judges for the reason given under Leave-one-
out and best shifts. The rater reference’s own best
shift raises it there from 61.1% to 80.0%. Gemini’s
shifted accuracy equals that value, the other four
judges fall 1.7 (Luna) to 10.5 points (Jev Choice)
short of it, and the four matched judges average 5.1
points below it. On Topical-Chat no shift raises the
rater reference or any matched judge. The matched
judges order the responses similarly on both cor-
pora (Spearman ρ= 0.50 to 0.72 with the rater
mean), and part of the PersonaChat gain comes
from moving predictions onto the top level, where
63.3% of its labels sit. Holding the criterion text
fixed leaves two other differences between the cor-
pora. Topical-Chat responses are grounded in facts,
which the top level’s text (“presents an interesting
fact”) mentions, and raters were assigned to con-
texts in rotation on Topical-Chat and in fixed triples
on PersonaChat.
H Where the Conventions Live
Table H1 lists the candidate conventions that Sec-
tion 5.1 introduces. They are of three kinds (a
population norm on ELLIPSE, a corpus-relative
standard, and annotation habits), and the raters’ in-
structions may state some (we checked ELLIPSE
only). Table H2 quotes in full the criterion texts
behind its rows. Judge sets and ranges here are
those of Jev’s copy (Section 3.1) unless the De-
cider is named; Table H5 gives the same rows in
the Decider’s copy.
Natural contrasts.Four natural contrasts in the
panels bear on the candidate conventions, and none
was designed as an experiment. They vary (1)
the judge, with the pairs fixed; (2) the corpus,
with the criterion text fixed (USR engaging); (3)
the criterion within a rubric (Contrasts within a
rubric, below); and (4) the form of the criterion,
binary or ordinal (Appendix L). Only the first three
hold one factor approximately fixed. In the first,
the offsets share their sign across the three LLM
judges in both judging modes (Table N5). Jev
Choice, which never sees the harness prompt, and
Jev Score, which decodes by expected level, share
it too, so neither a shared prompt nor a decod-
ing rule is their common cause. In the second,
USR engaging, worded identically on Topical-
Chat and PersonaChat, draws matched-judge off-
sets of −0.73 to −1.03 levels on PersonaChat,
whose raters gave the top level in 59.4% of rat-
ings, and −0.01 to −0.31 on Topical-Chat, where
they gave it in 34.7%. A one-level shift lifts every
matched judge on PersonaChat from at most 26.7%
to at least 69.5% and lowers it on Topical-Chat (Fig-

verbatim. The third column is our interpretation of the evidence in the fourth. Ranges span the four matched judges
of Jev’s copy unless stated, and every shift is a post-hoc analysis. The two rows below the rule are contrasts in

Label noise.Label noise would scatter errors on
both sides of the label and gather them on split
pairs, so it cannot explain ELLIPSE, where 99.5%
of Jev Choice’s errors lie below the label, LFQA
factuality, where Jev Choice misses unanimous
pairs more often than split ones (an exact accu-
racy of 17.4% on the 23 unanimous pairs against
46.3% on the 95 split pairs; Table H1), or USR-
TC’s almost unanimous _nofact pairs, whose
context holds no fact. Beyond ELLIPSE, LFQA
factuality, and USR-TC uses_knowledge, the
evidence that the judges place units lower than the
raters do is weaker. On a three-level scale whose
labels are mostly at the top, a judge that uses fewer
levels than the raters shows a negative mean off-
set even without placing units lower. Four panel
properties (criterion length, unit length, rater exper-
tise, and scale width) do not order the panels by Jev
Choice’s gap (Table H4). The panel with the largest
gap, ELLIPSE, is the one rated by trained raters.
This is weak evidence against an account based
on label quality. A shared reluctance to grant top
levels and shared training priors (Kim et al., 2025)
remain possible, and no experiment varied the text,
so the conventions account is observational.
Population norm.ELLIPSE’s labels may carry
a population norm (Table H1). Its trained raters
scored the essays of grade 8 to 12 English learn-
ers (Crossley et al., 2023). A norm-referenced
scale reads a level against a population (Glaser,
1963), and training instills such expectations (Wei-
gle, 1994). The gloss of level 5 in our crite-
rion sentence, “native-like facility”, and LLM es-
say graders’ penalty for small errors (Mathew
et al., 2026) may also contribute. ELLIPSE’s
level descriptions are those of the corpus’s English-
34

Panel and criterion Criterion sentence, then level descriptions from lowest to highest
ELLIPSE grammarCriterion.The essay demonstrates strong command of grammar and usage, per the ELLIPSE
English-proficiency analytic rubric’s Grammar trait (level 1 = lowest proficiency, level 5 = native-like
facility).
Levels.Level 1: Errors in grammar and usage throughout. | Level 2: Many errors in grammar and usage.
| Level 3: Some errors in grammar and usage. | Level 4: Minimal errors in grammar and usage. | Level
5: Command of grammar and usage with few or no errors.
USR-TC and
USR-PC
engaging
Criterion.The response is interesting rather than dull: it engages the reader (for example, by offering an
opinion, a thought, or an interesting fact) rather than being generic.
Levels.Dull: the response is generic and dull.|Somewhat interesting: the response could engage the
reader in the conversation (e.g., an opinion or a thought). | Interesting: the response is very interesting
or presents an interesting fact.
FED-Dialogue
depth
Criterion.The system discusses topics in depth.
Levels.No.|Somewhat.|Yes.
LFQA
factuality
Criterion.The information provided in the answer is factually correct and aligns with well-established
information.
Levels.Completely inaccurate: the answer is entirely false or contains significant factual errors. It
contradicts well-established information or provides misleading information.|Partially accurate: the
answer has some correct information but also contains significant inaccuracies or lacks important
details. It requires additional verification or correction. | Mostly accurate: the answer is predominantly
correct and provides relevant information. However, it may still have minor inaccuracies or omissions
that do not significantly impact the overall accuracy. | Entirely accurate: the answer is entirely accurate
and factually correct. It aligns with well-established information, provides clear and precise details, and
can be relied upon as a trusted source of information.
HelpSteer2
correctness
Criterion.The response includes all pertinent facts relevant to the prompt and contains no factual errors.
Levels.Major errors and/or missing pertinent facts; the response cannot be relied on for correctness.|
Several errors or omissions of pertinent facts.|A mix of correct and incorrect or missing content;
correctness is inconsistent.|Mostly correct and complete in its factual content, with at most minor
errors or omissions.|All pertinent facts are included and the response contains no errors.
USR-TC
uses_knowledge
Criterion.Given the fact the response is conditioned on, the response uses that fact well (rather than not
mentioning or referring to it at all).
Verdicts.MET or UNMET (binary criterion; the LLM judges could also return CANNOT_ASSESS).
USR-TC
understandable
Criterion.The response is understandable in the context of the conversation history: a reader can tell
what the person is trying to say (for example, its pronouns and references resolve sensibly). This is
independent of whether the response is on topic or otherwise well-formed.
Verdicts.MET or UNMET (binary criterion; the LLM judges could also return CANNOT_ASSESS).
ELLIPSE
cohesion
Criterion.The essay demonstrates strong text organization and use of cohesive devices to connect ideas
across sentences and paragraphs, per the ELLIPSE English-proficiency analytic rubric’s Cohesion trait
(level 1 = lowest proficiency, level 5 = native-like facility).
Levels.Level 1: No clear control of organization; cohesive devices not present or unsuccessfully used;
presentation of ideas unclear.|Level 2: Organization only partially developed with a lack of logical
sequencing of ideas; some basic cohesive devices used but with inaccuracy or repetition.|Level 3:
Organization generally controlled; cohesive devices used but limited in type; Some repetitive,
mechanical, or faulty use of cohesion use within and/or between sentences and paragraphs.|Level 4:
Organization generally well controlled; a range of cohesive devices used appropriately such as
reference and transitional words and phrases to connect ideas; generally appropriate overlap of ideas|
Level 5: Text organization consistently well controlled using a variety of effective linguistic features
such as reference and transitional words and phrases to connect ideas across sentences and paragraphs;
appropriate overlap of ideas.
HelpSteer2
verbosity
Criterion.The response’s amount of detail, relative to what the prompt asked for, ranging from minimal
to extensive.
Levels.Minimal detail; extremely terse relative to the prompt.|Below the level of detail one would
typically expect for this prompt. | A moderate, middling level of detail. | Above the level of detail one
would typically expect for this prompt.|Extensive, exhaustive detail; highly elaborated relative to the
prompt.

panels), with that gap in the last two columns. Criterion length: range of criterion words and the Spearman

proficiency scoring rubric. The judges received
those same descriptions, so a population norm
could reach the raters only through their training.
A zero-shot judge has nothing but the descriptions
and our criterion sentence, which ends “(level 1 =
lowest proficiency, level 5 = native-like facility)”
(Table H2). Read against native-like writing, “some
errors” would put a learner essay low, so the off-
set may reflect this gloss of level 5 as well as the
missing population. These data do not distinguish
a population norm from the small-error penalty of
LLM essay graders. The Decider received the same
texts with no exemplars, yet on ELLIPSE it comes
close to the raters (The Decider, below).
36

The ELLIPSE example of Table H3 is one of Jev
Choice’s confident errors (Appendix J). Both raters
put the essay at level 2 on grammar, and all five
judges put it at level 1. Gemini’s explanation re-
ports errors in nearly every clause, which it matches
to the level 1 description “Errors in grammar and
usage throughout.” The level descriptions give no
base rate, so they do not say how many errors sepa-
rate “throughout” from “many” in essays by grade
8–12 English learners. Rater training on that popu-
lation would supply such a base rate. The judges
lack it and placed this essay one level lower, as the
population-norm account predicts.
Contrasts within a rubric.Within a rubric the
judges, prompt, and units stay the same, so the
third contrast varies only the criterion. ELLIPSE’s
best and worst traits differ in what their level de-
scriptions name. Each of Jev’s five judges is most
accurate on cohesion, whose level descriptions
name observable devices, and least accurate on
grammar, whose levels differ only by the quanti-
fiers “throughout”, “many”, “some”, “minimal”,
and “few or no” (Tables H1 and H2). Both Decider
framings are most accurate on syntax instead, and
Decider Choice is least accurate on phraseology.
The cohesion trait still carries an offset of −0.52
to −0.62 levels, and with six traits the level descrip-
tions are confounded with other trait differences,
such as criterion length (see What does not sepa-
rate the panels, below). Every matched judge, in
either copy, also agrees best with the HelpSteer2
labels on verbosity, which describes an amount
of detail visible in the response.
Annotation habit: an absolute top level.
LFQA’s raters grant the topfactuality level far
more often than Jev Choice does, to 50 of the
120 answers against 12 of the 118 that Jev Choice
scored (Table H1). Table H3 quotes two ChatGPT
answers that two of the three raters placed at the
top level and on which all five judges chose “Par-
tially accurate”. The three LLM judges name the
same false claim in each answer: that LED TVs
create images with light-emitting diodes, and that
an SD card needs no power supply. In both cases
the label departs from the text of the top level, and
the judges reject the top level for the reason its text
gives. HelpSteer2 shows a related pattern at a level
that is neither the top nor absolute. None of Jev’s
five judges chose complexity level 4 or 5 in 450
predictions, although the labels put 10 of the 90
responses at level 4. Decider Choice chose them 5
times and Decider Score 4 times.
Annotation habits on USR-TC.Three of the
twelve sampled USR-TC contexts carry the lit-
eral string _nofact in place of a fact, and the 18
uses_knowledge pairs from these contexts are the
ones counted in Table H1. The LLM judges’ ex-
planations show that they read the string as the ab-
sence of a fact (Table H3). The understandable
criterion text excludes well-formedness from the
judgment. The raters nonetheless marked self-
contradictory responses such as the one quoted
UNMET, and every judge is correct on at most
34.8% of the 23 pairs labeled UNMET.
The USR-TC errors.Jev Choice makes 117 er-
rors on USR-TC, counting its Jev Noul verdicts
on the two binary criteria. Of these, 16 are the
_nofact pairs of uses_knowledge and 17 are
understandable pairs labeled UNMET. Another
63 are ordinal errors on split pairs. The remaining
21 errors (17.9%) are 19 ordinal errors on unani-
mous pairs and two other binary errors. They are
the only errors that fall neither on a split pair nor
under a candidate convention, and those conven-
tions were themselves identified from the judges’
errors.
No-read baselines.Both no-read baselines of
Section 3.3 are fitted on the evaluated pairs, and
HelpSteer2’s names each attribute’s most frequent
level. On two panels such a baseline is competitive
on exact accuracy. The HelpSteer2 correctness
text asks for a fact check, but its label tracks over-
all helpfulness (Pearson r= 0.94 over the 90 re-
sponses; Table H1). HelpSteer2’s constant predic-
tor has a QWK of zero by construction. It beats
each of Jev’s five judges in exact accuracy, signif-
icantly in pair-level sign tests for all but Gemini
(p≤2.1×10 −4; Gemini p= 0.075 ), so sep-
arations on HelpSteer2 in Jev’s copy (Table C1)
rank judges that trail a constant. Decider Choice,
at 63.1%, is above the constant without a signif-
icant difference (right alone on 45 pairs against
the constant’s 36, p= 0.37 ), and Decider Score,
at 55.3%, is below it, also not significantly (56
against 73, p= 0.16 ). The Decider’s HelpSteer2
leads are thus over LLM judges that trail the con-
stant. The LFQA provenance baseline is most ac-
curate on amount_info (74.2%) and formality
(73.3%) and least on factuality (56.7%). With-
out provenance, the modal level of each criterion
37

scores 61.1% over the panel, against the prove-
nance baseline’s 68.1%. In pair-level sign tests
(Appendix C), the p-value of each of Jev’s five
judges against the provenance baseline is at least
0.27, Decider Choice’s is 0.83, and Decider Score’s
is 0.19. Every matched judge’s mean QWK, 0.43
to 0.51, is above the baseline’s 0.28, a difference
we did not test.
What does not separate the panels.Table H4
gives the four properties named under Label noise
next to Jev Choice’s gap to the reference in exact
accuracy and in mean QWK. The two measures put
Jev Choice on the same side of the reference on
four panels, above it on LFQA and USR-TC and
below it on ELLIPSE and USR-PC. On the two
FED panels Jev Choice is below the reference in
exact accuracy and above it in mean QWK, and part
of this pattern may come from the rater reference’s
leave-one-out target (Section 3.3).
Within panels, criterion length shows no consis-
tent relation with Jev’s per-criterion accuracy, and
with few criteria per panel the test is weak. The
correlations of Table H4 are not significant on the
binary panels ( p= 0.18 on RiceChem, 0.12 on
HealthBench) or on the FED panels (p= 0.83 on
FED-Turn, 0.43 on FED-Dialogue). ELLIPSE is
ambiguous. Its six criterion sentences share one
template and differ in length by at most nine words,
yet their length orders Jev Choice’s per-trait accu-
racy perfectly (Spearman 1.00). Level-description
length, which ranges from 44 to 133 words, cor-
relates at 0.60 (p= 0.21 ). With six traits, length
cannot be separated from what each trait’s levels
describe (Table H1).
By quartile of unit length, Jev Choice’s accu-
racy on ELLIPSE falls from 16.4% in the shortest
quartile to 11.2–12.3% in the two longest, and on
FED-Dialogue it is lowest in the shortest quartile
(37.5%). Elsewhere it moves by up to 18.9 points
without a consistent direction, so unit length has no
common effect. The ELLIPSE offset also grows
with the holistic band (Appendix G), and this slice
does not separate essay length from essay quality.
Across panels, the three with the shortest units,
FED-Turn, USR-PC, and USR-TC, have gaps that
run from 16.2 points below the reference to 0.3
points above it.
ELLIPSE, the only ordinal panel with trained
raters, has Jev Choice’s largest gap (see Label
noise), but among the crowd-rated panels the gap
runs from +13.3 points on LFQA to −16.3 on
FED-Dialogue (Table H4). Rater expertise does
not order the gaps either.
Scale width changes the chance level of exact
accuracy (Section 3.4), but panels with the same
scale can still differ widely. USR-TC and USR-PC
share their three-level scales and five byte-identical
criterion texts, among them the engaging text of
the second natural contrast, and Jev Choice’s gap
is +0.3 points on USR-TC and −16.2 points on
USR-PC.
The harness prompt.The first natural contrast
excludes the harness prompt as the common cause
of the offsets, but its instructions, such as its rule for
self-contradicting submissions (Appendix A), may
still add to the LLM judges’ offsets. Jev Choice,
which never receives them, is nonetheless offset
further on ELLIPSE than Gemini and DeepSeek
(Table G1). The paragraph on label noise above
leaves open the possibility that all judges share a
reluctance to grant high levels. Such a reluctance
would produce a larger offset where raters use the
top level more, and USR engaging shows that pat-
tern. The embedded binary criteria rule out only
a general bias toward failing verdicts, because all
judges over-grant MET onunderstandable.
The Decider.Four of the seven candidate rows
go the same way in the Decider’s copy and three
do not (Table H5). Decider Choice grants LFQA’s
top factuality level to more answers than the
raters did, and it is more accurate on that crite-
rion’s unanimous pairs than on its split ones, as
label noise would predict. It places HelpSteer2
responses above their correctness label on av-
erage, and both Decider framings agree with that
label more closely than any LLM judge, at a QWK
of 0.49 against 0.18 to 0.30. The Decider’s data
manifest lists HelpSteer2’s attribute labels among
its training sources (Appendix R), so the model
had been trained on HelpSteer2’s rating scheme
and labels, whether or not our sampled responses
were among those rows, and on that panel it was
not judging the raters’ conventions zero-shot. On
the _nofact pairs, Decider Noul answers MET, as
the raters did, on 16 of 18. On ELLIPSE, Decider
Score’s exact accuracy comes within half a point
of the rater reference, and both Decider framings,
like the raters, do best unshifted (Appendix G).
No source in that manifest names ELLIPSE or the
essay corpus it samples, and nothing disclosed ac-
counts for this result. Of the three observations set
against label noise above, only ELLIPSE’s holds

What the contrasts suggest.How close a judge
comes to the raters appears to depend on what the
raters knew beyond the text the judges read. In
the contrasts within a rubric, ELLIPSE cohesion
and HelpSteer2 verbosity, the judges come clos-
est where a level description points to something
a reader can see in the unit. Where, on our read-
ing, a level’s meaning depends on a population, a
corpus, or an annotation habit, Jev and every LLM
judge, the best included, fall short in the same di-
rection, and the Decider does so on four of the
seven rows. If every judge reads the criterion text
as written, agreement with labels on an ordinal
rubric measures in part whether a judge was given
the raters’ conventions, such as reading ELLIPSE
essays against the population of learner essays they
come from. Section 7 counts such literal reading
among several possible contributors to this shared
shortfall, together with shared training priors and a
reluctance to grant top levels, and our data cannot
tell these contributors apart.
I Confidence
The returned field.The vendor describes the
confidence field that Choice and Score return as
a number computed from how the probabilities
are spread but gives no exact formula for either
primitive. Its interactive demo approximates the
field for three options by (3pmax −1)/2 .6 For
Jev Choice the field matches the general form
(Kpmax −1)/(K−1) , where pmax is the largest
returned probability and K counts every option,
the NA option included; it is 0 for a uniform dis-
tribution and 1 when one option receives all the
probability. The largest deviation from this for-
mula is 0.0225 over Jev Choice’s 3,414 answers on
ordinal pairs, with a mean of 0.006, and 0.015 over
the 8,392 binary answers on the full RiceChem set
(Appendix D), where K= 3 . These deviations are
larger than two-decimal rounding of the returned
values would produce, so the formula approximates
the field closely without describing it exactly. The
formula does not describe Jev Score’s confidence
(median deviation 0.10, maximum 0.46), which
is used as returned. Noul returns no confidence
field, and the Noul confidence of Section 3.1 is the

Decider Choice (right) against (a) its error rate and (b)
the share of LLM verdicts on its wrong pairs that repeat

same normalization with K= 2 . The embedded
binary pairs of the ordinal panels enter every Jev
Choice analysis with this Noul confidence. Decider
Choice’s field follows the formula to floating-point
precision on its 3,414 ordinal answers, and Decider
Score’s, which does not, is also used as returned
(Appendix R).
Panels.Outside ELLIPSE, Jev Choice’s error
rate falls between the 0.25–0.5 band and the top
band on every panel, by 13.3 to 39.6 points (Ta-
ble I1a), and the three largest falls are on FED-Turn,
LFQA, and USR-TC. On ELLIPSE, where the er-
ror rate is flat across bands (Section 6.1; Figure I1a),
confidence is also unrelated to the size of the error
(Spearman −0.03 between confidence and absolute
level error, p= 0.23 , over 1,548 pairs). The lowest
band is small on every panel and holds a single pair
on ELLIPSE and on LFQA.
Criteria.The criterion-level AUROCs of Ta-
ble I1e show where confidence fails inside a panel
whose errors it otherwise ranks. The criteria
named below were singled out after inspecting
these values. The AUROC is below 0.45 on five
criteria with at least five pairs in each outcome:
ELLIPSE phraseology and conventions, FED-
Turn interesting, FED-Dialogue informative,
and LFQA factuality. All five carry a nega-
tive offset for Jev Choice, from −0.58 levels on
factuality to −1.38 on phraseology, which
suggests that confidence fails where Jev Choice errs
mostly in one direction. ELLIPSE vocabulary is
an exception, where Jev Choice’s offset is −1.28
levels yet the AUROC is 0.62. LFQA factuality,
with an AUROC of 0.41 against 0.70 for its panel,
also concentrates the panel’s errors. Jev Choice
is wrong on 59.3% of its 118 pairs, against 18.3%
and 16.7% on the panel’s other two criteria. On
USR-PC engaging (AUROC 0.52), 83.1% of Jev
Choice’s verdicts on the 59 pairs are wrong at a
mean confidence of 0.72, and the error rate is flat
over the three occupied bands. Without engaging,
USR-PC’s error rate falls steeply with confidence
(Table I1a). Confidence ranks errors well on Help-
Steer2 correctness (AUROC 0.76), a criterion
whose labels follow overall helpfulness (Table H1).
Jev Score.Jev Score’s error rate is lower in the
top band than in the lowest on six panels and rises
between the two upper bands on ELLIPSE (Ta-
ble I1b). On FED-Turn’s ordinal pairs alone, with-
out the embedded binary pairs that Table I1d in-
cludes, Jev Score’s most confident quarter is 86.3%
exact against 66.7% overall, and Jev Choice’s
71.0% against 56.2%.
Most confident pairs.Table I1d restricts Jev
Choice to the 25%, 50%, and 75% of pairs on
which it is most confident. At 25% coverage, accu-
racy is 9.8 to 20.2 points above the full-coverage
value on five panels, 3.2 points above on FED-
Dialogue, and 1.0 point above on ELLIPSE. These
accuracies include the embedded binary pairs, so
on the FED and USR panels the full-coverage col-
umn differs from Table 1.
The Decider’s confidence.Decider Choice’s
confidence, which Table I1 does not show, ranks its
errors on the six panels other than ELLIPSE, with
an AUROC of 0.72 on FED-Turn, 0.60 on FED-
Dialogue, 0.70 on HelpSteer2, 0.78 on LFQA, 0.69
on USR-TC, and 0.75 on USR-PC. On ELLIPSE its
AUROC is 0.52 and its error rate is flat (Figure I1a,
right). It is 53.2% in the 0.25–0.5 band and 51.9%
in the 0.5–0.75 band, which together hold all but
two of its pairs, and no pair reaches the top band.
Table R3a gives its error rate in every band, and
Appendix R the intervals of the ELLIPSE AUROC
and of FED-Dialogue’s, which includes 0.5.
Binary panels.We computed no calibration error
for the ordinal panels. On the binary panels, the
ECE column of Table D1 and Figure D1a give the
calibration of P(MET) (Appendix D).

Table J1: The confident-error sample (Section 5.2).
Conf.: range of Jev Choice’s confidence over the 12
errors of a panel. LLM repeats: verdicts of Luna, Gem-
ini, and DeepSeek that equal Jev’s wrong answer, out
of 36 per panel, and Expected: the number the indepen-
dence baseline predicts. Same answer: errors on which
all five judges give Jev Choice’s answer, out of 12. Unan.
raters: errors whose raters all gave the same level, out
of 12 (undefined for HelpSteer2, which releases a sin-
gle label). HealthBench: the separately sampled errors
described below, with physician unanimity.
J Repeated Errors and Juries
Sampling rule.The pool is every pair Jev Choice
answered wrongly, abstentions excluded, with con-
fidence as in Appendix I. Pairs are sorted by confi-
dence, highest first, with ties broken by unit iden-
tifier and then by criterion name. The sample
holds 84 errors, 83 on ordinal pairs and one on
an embedded binary pair, a _nofact pair of USR-
TC uses_knowledge. On four panels (ELLIPSE,
FED-Turn, HelpSteer2, and USR-TC) one further
error shares the twelfth-highest confidence value
and falls outside the sample. Selecting the tied er-
rors in the order the pairs are stored gives the same
LLM repeat counts on every panel.
Repeated errors.Luna repeats 81 of the 84 er-
rors, Gemini 80, and DeepSeek 81 (Table J1 gives
the panels). Under the independence baseline the
three would repeat 126.7 of their 252 verdicts.
The expected count per panel is highest on FED-
Dialogue, 27.6 of 36, and lowest on USR-TC, 11.8.
Jev Score gives Jev Choice’s answer on all 84, but
it is the same model asked a different question, so
it is not counted as corroboration.
The Decider’s sample.Decider Choice’s 84 con-
fident errors, drawn by the same rule (Table R3c),
get 218 repeats among the LLM judges’ 252 ver-
dicts, against 120.7 expected. On each panel but
ELLIPSE the LLM judges repeat 30 to 36 of their
36 verdicts, where the baseline expects 13.7 to 20.4.
On ELLIPSE they repeat 16 against 19.5 expected,
and the count stays at or below the expected one
in every condition of Table 2, down to 9 against
16.7 with higher-tier judges. Appendix R describes
these 12 errors and the LLM verdicts on them. De-
cider Noul’s HealthBench sample, drawn like Jev
Noul’s below, gets 16 repeats against 8.9 expected,
13 of them on its six false positives and 3 on its six
false negatives.
Composition.In all 12 ELLIPSE errors Jev
Choice put the essay at level 1 (eight on grammar,
three on syntax, and one on conventions), and
all five judges gave that level on 10 of them. Nine
of these labels are level 2 and three are level 3. The
two raters agreed on six, each time at level 2, and
the other six labels are half-way means that the
half-up rule rounded up, from ratings of 1 and 2
on three errors and of 2 and 3 on the other three.
Every FED-Dialogue error is a “No.” prediction
shared by all five judges, eight of them on depth,
and 11 of the 12 FED-Turn errors are “No.” pre-
dictions too. The five judges also agree on each
of the 12 LFQA errors, ten on formality and two
on amount_info. On USR-PC the errors are 12
engaging pairs on which Jev Choice chose “Dull”
against a label of “Somewhat interesting” (nine) or
“Interesting” (three). One of them has unanimous
raters, and so do three USR-TC errors, the_nofact
pair among them. Five HelpSteer2 coherence er-
rors lie one level above the label, where all five
judges chose the top level, “Fully clear and inter-
nally consistent throughout.”
Split and unanimous pairs.One reading is that
the LLM judges repeat these errors because Jev’s
confidence measures how clearly the criterion text,
applied to the unit, points to one answer, so LLM
judges given the same text reach that answer as
well and all miss a label that departs from it. A
second is that the errors sit on pairs whose raters
disagreed, and that some of the apparent repeats
reflect that disagreement. The last column of Ta-
ble J1 leaves the second reading open for most of
the sample, since on the six panels with several
raters per pair only 10 of the 72 errors have unani-
mous raters, and none of the FED or LFQA errors
has. That reading cannot apply to those ten, the
USR-TC _nofact pair among them, or to Health-
Bench, whose physicians were unanimous on 11 of
its 12 sampled errors. In the Decider’s sample 5 of
the 72 errors on those panels have unanimous raters,

When every matched judge is wrong.Table J2
also gives, over every ordinal pair the four matched
judges all scored, the share on which all four are
wrong. In Jev’s copy it is 17.2% to 31.5% on ev-
ery ordinal panel but ELLIPSE, where it is 64.0%,
so choosing a correct judge for each ELLIPSE
pair would reach 36.0%. Gemini, the best of Jev’s
matched judges there, reaches 29.8% exact accu-
racy (29.9% on the pairs all four scored), against
51.4% for the rater reference (Table 1; Section 5.1).
Of the panels with a rater reference, ELLIPSE is
also the only one on which choosing a correct judge
wherever one exists falls short of that reference. In
the Decider’s copy the share is 45.8% on ELLIPSE
and 11.9% to 30.3% elsewhere, and choosing a
correct judge exceeds the reference on every such
panel, ELLIPSE included (Table R4c).
Repetition in every band.The sample covers
only the most confident errors. For all of Jev
Choice’s errors, Table I1c gives the shares of re-
peated errors by band, which Figure I1b plots, next
to their independence baseline. The baseline rises
with confidence on several panels, from 49.1% in
the 0.25–0.5 band to 63.7% in the top band on EL-
LIPSE and from 24.8% to 54.6% on USR-PC. Part
of the rise in Figure I1b therefore follows from
each LLM judge’s own tendencies given the crite-
rion and the label. The excess over the baseline is
nonetheless largest in the top band on every ordinal
panel (Table I1c). Two variants leave the rise with
confidence intact. Restricting the count to ordinal
pairs changes the top band only on USR-TC, to
83.3%. It lowers USR-TC’s 0.5–0.75 band from
66.4% to 58.3%, because the LLM judges also give
Jev’s answer on its embedded binary errors there,
and moves no other band of Figure I1b by more
than 4.0 points. Counting LLM abstentions and
judge-call failures as non-repeats lowers no band
by more than 1.9 points. Table R3b gives the same
bands for Decider Choice’s errors.
HealthBench.HealthBench’s confident errors
were selected by a different rule and are reported
separately. They are Jev Noul’s six false positives
with the highest probability of yes, from 0.91 to
0.96, and the six false negatives with the lowest,
from 0.08 to 0.12. The three LLM judges give Jev’s
answer in 32 of their 36 verdicts on them, against
10.0 that the independence baseline expects (Ta-
ble J1): Luna in 11, Gemini in 10, and DeepSeek
in 11. Over all 92 of Jev Noul’s HealthBench er-
rors the three LLM judges repeat 55.8% of their ver-
dicts, against a baseline of 27.5%. For Jev Choice’s
errors the repeat share is 58.4% and the baseline
27.1%.
Juries.In Jev’s copy, the three LLM judges’ me-
dian level (their majority on binary pairs) never
beats the most accurate matched judge (chosen
on the same pairs, which favors it) and trails it
by up to 12.8 points (ELLIPSE), significantly so
on ELLIPSE, HelpSteer2, and RiceChem (95%
intervals below zero). Jev Choice as a fourth ju-
ror never helps significantly, and the median gives
Jev’s wrong answer on 82 of the 84 confident errors
(Table J2). No median jury’s interval lies above
that judge in the other conditions (Tables N5, O4,
and P4). The most accurate matched judge of Ta-
43

ble J2 is the best single judge of the cascades (Sec-
tion 6.2) on eight panels; on FED-Turn that role
goes to Jev Score, which is not a matched judge.
A median of four levels is not unique when the
four judges split evenly, which happens on 7.4% to
22.9% of a panel’s pairs. We therefore score the
four-judge jury with both the lower and the upper
median, so that the tie rule favors no judge (Ta-
ble J2). The upper median is the most accurate of
the three juries on every panel except USR-TC. Its
best result, on USR-PC, is 1.0 point above the most
accurate matched judge, with an interval of−2.3 to
+4.3 points. Restricted to ordinal pairs, the three-
LLM median is above that judge only on USR-TC,
by 1.4 points (interval −2.8 to +5.1). Of the two
confident errors on which the three-LLM median
does not give Jev’s answer, it gives the label on a
USR-TC pair and a third level on an ELLIPSE pair.
The four-judge lower median is right on none of
the 84 confident errors and the upper median on
one.
Juries in the Decider’s copy.Table R4c repeats
Table J2 for the Decider’s copy, with Decider
Choice as the fourth juror. The three-LLM me-
dian falls significantly short of the most accurate
matched judge only on ELLIPSE and HelpSteer2,
where that judge is Decider Choice. In none of the
four conditions is a median jury significantly more
accurate than that judge, although in the higher tier
the USR-TC upper median’s interval starts at ex-
actly zero (0.0 to +5.9). The three-LLM median
returns the Decider’s wrong answer on 73 of its 84
confident errors.
K Cascades
Threshold rules.The replay uses the cascade
and the framings of Section 6.2. On the ordinal pan-
els the cascade threshold τ runs from 0 to 1 in steps
of 0.05. On the binary panels a pair is deferred
when its distance from one half, |P(yes)−0.5| ,
which is half the Noul confidence of Section 3.1,
is below τ, and there τ runs from 0 to 0.5 in steps
of 0.02. The pair set is that of Appendix C. Where
the fallback’s request failed, the cascade also keeps
Jev’s verdict. For each fallback, the reported oracle
threshold is the τ with the highest accuracy, with
ties going to the lower deferred share. A panel’s
best cascade is the one whose fallback, at its ora-
cle threshold, gives the highest accuracy (bold in
Table K1). With Jev first, its fallback is Gemini
on every panel but LFQA, where it is Luna, and
its deferred share runs from 12.1% of the pairs on
RiceChem to 99.9% on ELLIPSE. Appendix P re-
peats the replay with each vendor’s higher tier as
fallback, gives the terms of Equation (K1) for each
such fallback at its own oracle threshold on Help-
Steer2 and HealthBench, and also measures those
cascades against each panel’s best single judge of
either tier.
Decider-first cascades.With the Decider in Jev’s
place, these rules give the Decider-first cascades
of Table 3, which put Decider Choice first on the
ordinal panels and Decider Noul on the binary ones.
The tables and figure of this appendix describe the
Jev-first cascades, and Table R4 gives the Decider-
first ones, with their fallbacks, deferred shares, kept
and deferred pairs, halvings, and held-out results.
Cross-fitted thresholds.Each of the 50 halvings
of Table 3 splits a panel’s units into two halves with
a seeded permutation. Each half in turn makes four
choices on its own pairs: (1) a threshold for each
fallback, (2) the best cascade’s fallback and thresh-
old under the rule above, (3) the best single judge,
and (4) the smallest threshold at which a cascade
with that half’s best LLM judge as fallback matches
that judge. The other half is scored with these
choices, and a halving pools its two scored halves.
The same code reproduces Tables 3 and K1 when
it makes the oracle-threshold choices. Table K3
gives each panel’s share of halvings with a gain
and the held-out result of choice (4). With Jev first,
cross-fitted gains over the fallback alone stay large
only where the fallback is far less accurate than
the panel’s best LLM judge, 12.2 points over Luna
on HelpSteer2 and 8.3 over Luna on HealthBench.
With each panel’s best LLM judge as fallback they
run from −2.1 to +1.3 points. Jev Score is the best
single judge in 99 of the 100 FED-Turn halves. On
the six panels on which oracle thresholds match
the best LLM judge for 10% to 59% of that judge’s
cost (seeReaching the fallback), the cross-fitted
cascade matches that judge on the held-out half in
only 46% to 64% of halves. When the fallback is
the best LLM judge, cross-fitted Jev-first cascades
cost 16% to 48% as much as that judge on these six
panels for 0.2 to 1.8 points less held-out accuracy,
and 6.6% on RiceChem, where Jev by itself beats
it, for 1.2 points more (Tables 3 and K3).

the deferred pairs, so

Let adef
fallback be the fallback’s accuracy on the de-
ferred pairs. On ordinal panels the fallback’s ac-
curacy counts Jev’s verdict wherever the fallback
abstained. The accuracies of the cascade and of its
fallback are
acascade =s kept akept
Jev + (1−s kept)a def
fallback,
afallback =s kept akept
fallback + (1−s kept)a def
fallback,
and their difference is Equation (K1), in which
adef
fallback cancels. Equation (K1) thus bounds a cas-
cade’s gain over its fallback by Jev’s lead over the
fallback on the kept pairs, and the fallback’s lead
on deferred pairs, which lifts a cascade above Jev,
never lifts it above the fallback. Recomputing both
sides from the recorded verdicts gives equal values
at the reportedτon all nine panels.
Kept and deferred pairs.Table K2 applies the
identity to each panel’s best Jev-first cascade.
Against that cascade’s fallback, Jev alone is right
on only 12 to 37 pairs of an ordinal panel (Ta-
ble C2), and on kept pairs the two are mostly right
or wrong together. The fallback leads Jev on the
deferred pairs of every panel, significantly on six
in two-sided sign tests (all but RiceChem, LFQA,
and USR-TC). The five ordinal panels where Jev
keeps more than two pairs are also the five on which
the best cascade beats its fallback. On their kept
pairs Jev leads the fallback by 0.7 to 1.9 points,
and no lead is significant (p≥0.45 ). Jev’s confi-
dence keeps few of the pairs on which Jev is right
and the fallback wrong. It keeps none of them on
ELLIPSE and FED-Dialogue, a minority on FED-
Turn, LFQA, and USR-TC, and half on HelpSteer2,
whereas on USR-PC, the ordinal panel with the
largest gain, 10 of the 16 such pairs are kept. Cor-
related errors make the cascades cheap. Where the
46

fallback would mostly repeat Jev’s errors, keeping
Jev’s confident verdicts costs little accuracy, which
is why most cross-fitted cascades avoid most of the
best LLM judge’s cost (Table 3).
RiceChem is the one panel where Jev leads sig-
nificantly on its kept pairs (63 to 26,p= 1×10 −4).
The best RiceChem cascade defers to Gemini,
which Jev Noul outscores, so the cascade gains 4.5
points over Gemini. It still falls below Jev Choice
alone. HealthBench’s 2.0-point gain over Gemini
rests on a kept-pair lead of 18 to 10 (p= 0.18).
To illustrate the identity, the last row of Table K2
repeats HelpSteer2 with Gemini atτ= 0.30 , the or-
acle threshold on this panel with Luna or DeepSeek
as fallback. Gemini wins the deferred pairs 16 to
1 (p= 3×10 −4), an advantage that drops out of
the cascade’s difference from Gemini because the
cascade gives Gemini’s own verdicts there. On
the kept pairs Jev is behind Gemini (54.5% against
56.1%), so the cascade (52.8%) ends below Gemini
alone (54.3%).
Fixed threshold and flat curves.The last col-
umn of Table K1 gives cascade accuracy at a fixed
τ= 0.75 on every ordinal panel. For the best cas-
cade’s fallback this is already the oracle threshold
on FED-Turn, LFQA, and USR-TC, and it matches
the oracle accuracy on HelpSteer2 and USR-PC,
whose oracle threshold is 0.50. The fixed threshold
falls below Gemini alone on ELLIPSE and FED-
Dialogue. Near their maxima the curves are flat
(Figure K1). With Gemini as fallback, accuracy
stays within 1.4 points of the oracle threshold’s for
every τ from 0.55 up on FED-Turn, from 0.30 up
on USR-PC, and from 0.40 up on HelpSteer2.
Reaching the fallback.With the best LLM judge
of each panel as fallback and oracle thresholds, the
reach of Table K1 stays below 60% on Health-
Bench and on the five ordinal panels other than EL-
LIPSE and FED-Dialogue. Those two need nearly
every pair deferred, because Gemini’s lead over
Jev Choice is separated on both and, on ELLIPSE,
Jev Choice’s confidence does not rank its errors.
As a share of that judge’s cost, with Jev’s own
cost added to the deferred share, these matches
cost 17.5% on HealthBench, 49.2% on FED-Turn,
30.5% on HelpSteer2, 38.8% on LFQA, 59.4% on
USR-TC, and 10.3% on USR-PC, against 100.1%
on ELLIPSE and 99.7% on FED-Dialogue. Where
Jev alone is at least as accurate as a fallback, as for
all three RiceChem fallbacks, the reach is zero, so
on RiceChem Jev alone already matches the best
LLM judge, DeepSeek. On FED-Turn, Jev Score
alone reaches 70.7% on the cascade pair set, above
every cascade, at about 1/650 of Gemini’s cost.
Criterion routing.LFQA’s cascade does not re-
duce to sending one criterion to the fallback. Rout-
ing every factuality pair to Luna and keeping
Jev Choice’s verdicts elsewhere gives 69.8%, be-
low both the confidence cascade (72.1%) and Luna
alone (71.5%). Routing any one or two of the three
criteria to Luna gives at most 70.9%, reached with
factualityandamount_infotogether.
Cost.The linear cost estimate of Table K1 as-
sumes that a deferred pair costs the fallback’s av-
erage per pair. Deferred pairs may be longer or
shorter than average and need more or fewer rea-
soning tokens.
L Binary-Panel Expectations
Re-examined
Several expectations formed on the two binary pan-
els did not hold on the ordinal panels, and others
held only in narrower forms (Table L1). The di-
rection of Jev’s errors, for instance, reverses be-
tween the two kinds of panel (see the last para-
graph of this appendix), and Jev Choice, which
never chose CANNOT_ASSESS on the binary pan-
els, abstained on 28 ordinal pairs. Besides the scale,
the two binary panels share checklist criteria (Sec-
tion 3.3), expert labels, and one wording of the
abstain option. Because the ordinal panels also
change the domain, the options Jev receives, and
Jev’s instruction, we attribute no failure to scale
type alone.
The expectations in the first column of Table L1
are those we formed on the binary panels and wrote
down before any judge was run on the ordinal pan-
els (see Limitations). They concern Jev alone. The
Decider, added after these analyses, played no part
in forming them, and the claims we checked on
it were recorded separately before it had judged
any study item (Appendix R). The six status labels,
and the assignment of each expectation to one of
them, were decided after the ordinal panels had
been analyzed. The evidence column gives the
statistic behind each status, and a reader can apply
a stricter or a looser rule to it. Since the two kinds
of panel differ in several ways at once, a status
records whether an expectation carried over and
leaves open which difference decided the outcome.

48

Binary and ordinal criteria.The fourth natu-
ral contrast of Appendix H varies the form of the
criterion, although it also changes the panel, the
domain, and the label source. For Jev, moving from
binary to ordinal criteria reverses the direction of
the error. Jev over-grants MET on both binary
panels (Appendix D), on which the LLM judges’
MET-rate offsets have no common sign (Table G1).
On ordinal criteria Jev’s offsets are negative out-
side USR-TC. The two embedded binary criteria of
USR-TC split in direction. Every judge over-grants
MET on understandable (Jev Noul’s MET rate
is 90.3% against 68.1% labeled) and under-grants
it on uses_knowledge (37.5% against 58.3%). On
the same responses, then, the direction of every
judge’s error follows the criterion, so Jev’s reversal
need not come from the change of form itself.
M Release and Derived Statistics
Release.The release contains the code that runs
every judge, the frozen panel samples, and one
verdict record per judge and pair. A panel sample
holds each unit as the judges received it, the cri-
terion texts (with level descriptions on the ordinal
panels), the label, and the individual rater scores
behind it. A verdict record gives the judge’s level
or verdict, whether it abstained, and any judge-
call failure. For a decision model it also holds
the returned probabilities and confidence, and for
the LLM judges the explanation each one returned.
The per-panel summaries that the runners wrote,
which record cost and wall time, accompany the
verdict records.
Scripts.A driver script regenerates the paper’s
numbers and figures from these files, sending no
request to any model. It runs per-panel analysis
scripts and then the analysis script, which com-
putes the values that the paper quotes from the
records and the per-panel outputs. The analysis
script writes them to a JSON file and a readable
summary, which names for each value the file and
key it is read from, or the rule that computes it.
Two figure scripts draw every figure at its printed
size from these outputs. One draws each copy of
the study’s figures, and the other draws Figures 1
and I1, which set the two decision models side by
side; Figure N1 also reads the judging-mode com-
parison. The four comparison scripts described
below likewise send no model request, and each
records the source of every value it writes.
The analysis script checks itself against the per-
panel analysis outputs. Its recomputed discordant
counts for the 21 ordinal-panel tests between Jev
Choice and the LLM judges and the 7 between
Jev Choice and Jev Score, its confidence-band ta-
bles for the seven ordinal panels, and its 21 cas-
cade curves on the ordinal panels all equal those
that the per-panel analysis computed. The paired
comparisons, group resampling, juries, cross-fitted
cascades, and physician reference draw from one
fixed seed, with a separate generator for each quan-
tity. The code behind them recomputes pair counts,
shares of repeated errors, rater references, oracle-
threshold cascades, and physician comparisons,
and each equals the value computed without re-
sampling. The ELLIPSE marginal intervals of Ap-
pendix C use a seed of their own and one generator
shared by the judges.
Inputs beyond the panel records.Some val-
ues need records other than the panel verdicts
and the per-panel summaries. The LLM judges’
cached responses hold the per-response costs that
sum to DeepSeek’s billed cost (Appendix B), the
reasoning-token counts, the upstream providers
that served DeepSeek, and the reasoning effort that
each request of the effort runs carried. They also
show which twelve Luna verdicts were served from
the cache (Appendix A). Jev’s runs outside the pan-
els (the full RiceChem set, the five-pass stability
study, and the HealthBench ideal-completion abla-
tion; Appendices A and D) have records of their
own. So do Luna’s two spot-check runs.
Whole-rubric runs.The whole-rubric runs of
Appendix N form a second results tree in the re-
lease, with the same layout as the first. Its Jev runs,
panel samples, and rater files are links into the per-
criterion tree, so only the three LLM judges’ verdict
records, run summaries, and cached responses are
new. The repeat runs form a third tree, which also
keeps DeepSeek’s failed first attempt on ELLIPSE.
The release lists the units of the repeat runs and
of the smoke test. A setup script for these runs
builds the second tree, draws the two sets of units,
and reads each judge’s logged seed from the per-
criterion records. A launch script runs the three
judges on the smoke-test units, the full panels, and
the repeat units, and a comparison script reads the
three trees and writes the judging-mode compari-
son, like the analysis script, as a JSON file with a
readable summary. A single setting tells the scripts
which results tree to read. With the setting pointed
49

at the second tree, the scripts that summarize each
run, the analysis script, and the figure script run
unchanged on the whole-rubric verdicts, and with
it unset they reproduce the per-criterion outputs
exactly.
Effort runs.Four more trees, laid out like
the first, hold the effort and calibration runs of
Appendix O, one tree each for the high runs,
DeepSeek’s medium run, the calibration’s explicit-
medium runs, and the calibration’s second default
runs, the last two on the repeat units. Each tree
records the effort its requests named, and each cali-
bration tree also records which units it covers. As
in the second tree, links into the per-criterion tree
supply Jev’s runs, the panel samples, and the rater
files, and the medium tree also links Luna’s and
Gemini’s main runs as their medium runs. The
medium tree’s response caches contain DeepSeek’s
responses only, so the values read from caches,
the reasoning and cached prompt tokens, are ab-
sent for those two linked runs and are reported as
not applicable rather than as zero. When a setting
names an effort, the code that configures the LLM
judges passes it to the harness, which adds it to
every request. Without that setting the requests are
unchanged. Another launch script runs the three
judges for each tree, and the setup script builds
these trees too, links included. A second compari-
son script reads all the trees and compares the runs,
the calibration, accuracy, and the paper’s statistics
across conditions. With the setting pointed at the
high or the medium tree, the analysis and figure
scripts run unchanged on that condition, and these
reruns supply the agreement and offset counts that
Table O4c gives for the medium and high condi-
tions.
Higher-tier runs.The higher-tier runs of Ap-
pendix P fill a further tree, again of the same lay-
out and linked into the per-criterion tree for Jev’s
verdicts, the panels, and the rater files. The tree
records which model replaced each flash-tier judge,
the seeds read from that judge’s records, and the
settings of the runs. A log in the tree gives the start,
end, and exit status of every session of every run,
with a note on each of the two stopped sessions, and
the summed session times are formed from it. A
third launch script runs the three higher-tier judges,
and a third comparison script reads this tree, the
per-criterion tree, and the high tree. It compares
every higher-tier judge with its flash sibling, as
run and at high effort, on accuracy, repeated errors,
cascades, and juries, the cascades also against the
per-panel best single judge of either tier. For each
run, the pilot included, it records the tokens, the
costs (as recorded, as billed, and at list prices), the
wall times, and their ratios to Jev Choice and to
the flash sibling. The analysis and figure scripts
need no change to cover the higher-tier judges once
pointed at this tree.
Decider runs.The Decider’s runs of Appendix R
have a results tree of their own, laid out like the
first. They keep the names of Jev’s runs, so that the
analysis, figure, and comparison scripts read them
unchanged, and in this tree such a name means the
Decider in that framing. Each run’s record names
the grader that sent the Decider’s requests, and no
run in the tree links into one of Jev’s. A short dec-
laration in the tree gives the model identifier, the
price, the documented rate limit, and the client’s
settings; it was frozen after the runs and before any
analysis read it. The requests went through a small
client that sends the request bodies built for Jev to
Perplexity’s endpoint and keeps the request rate un-
der the documented limit. It retries, within a fixed
budget, attempts that hit the rate limit, time out, or
fail on the server’s side, and it logs every attempt
with its send time, wait in the queue, latency, status,
reported model, and input tokens. A summary step
turns these request logs into each run’s counts of
requests, attempts, retries, and rate-limit responses,
together with its latencies, the share of time spent
waiting for the rate limit, and its dates. Four more
Decider trees pair the Decider’s runs, linked from
this tree, with the LLM judges’ runs of the whole-
rubric, high, medium, and higher-tier trees, and
a sixth holds its repeat of the 106 units. A script
pairs each repeated request with its first sending
by the request body and compares the two answers.
Pointed at a Decider tree, the analysis, figure, and
comparison scripts produce the Decider’s copy of
the statistics they compute, and their JSON output
keeps Jev’s names as keys, which there stand for
the Decider. A fourth comparison script reads both
models’ trees and sets their values side by side,
key by key, with each model’s run details, and it
computes no difference, ratio, or test between the
two models. It also checks that every value depend-
ing only on the LLM judges is identical in the two
copies and that Jev’s column reproduces Table 2.
Derived statistics.Differences between reported
values are computed before rounding, and stated
bounds such as “at most” compare rounded values.
50

The analysis script derives the following statistics
from the recorded verdicts, labels, and summaries,
and marks the post-hoc analyses among them.
• The paired comparisons with units resampled:
bootstrap intervals, equivalence bounds, sign
tests over units, Holm correction, and the
resampling of sampling groups (Table C1).
• Pair-level sign tests for Jev Choice, Jev Noul,
and Jev Score, with the Holm correction of Jev
Choice’s tests (Table C2).
• The paired accuracy differences and per-panel
cost ratios of Figure 1, and DeepSeek’s billed
cost per panel.
• Judge–judge and judge–label QWK (Table 1),
and the all-wrong shares (Table J2).
• The per-criterion aggregates of Figure G1: the
Spearman correlation over 31 criteria, the counts
of criteria beyond the 15-point cut-off and of
criteria with near-zero offsets, the sign
agreement of the four offsets on 35 criteria, and
the gap measures that ignore location (post hoc;
Table G3).
• MET-rate offsets and Cohen’sκon the binary
panels, and MET rates on the embedded binary
criteria.
• For ELLIPSE, level usage, the sign structure of
the errors, slopes, offsets by trait and by holistic
band, accuracy on half-way labels, the post-hoc
downward tie-break and constant-shift curves,
and the marginal bootstrap intervals of
Appendix C.
• The USRengagingshift (post hoc) and rank
correlations, the_nofactcounts, the
decomposition of Jev Choice’s USR-TC errors,
and the rater reference split by rater unanimity.
• Each judge’s use of “Somewhat.” on FED-Turn,
the exchange of decoding rules there (post hoc),
and the number of Jev Score’s exact halves
(Appendix E).
• The relation between P(NA) and the raters’ N/A
answers on FED-Dialogueerror_recovery,
and the overlap of the judges’ abstentions
(Appendix F).
• The fit of the confidence formula, AUROC per
panel and per criterion, accuracy on the most
confident pairs, repeated errors by band with
their independence baseline, the confident-error
sample, and the median juries (post hoc;
Appendices I and J).
• For the cascades (post hoc), the oracle-threshold
and cross-fitted results, the kept and deferred
splits with their sign tests, accuracy at a fixed
threshold, the deferred share needed to reach
each fallback, cost estimates, and the LFQA
criterion-routing check (Appendix K).
• The HealthBench physician reference
(Appendix D).
• The no-read baselines and their sign tests,
criterion-length correlations on FED and
ELLIPSE, accuracy by unit-length quartile, and
the agreement between Luna’s two spot-check
runs.
Sums over panels.Several counts in the paper
add per-panel values: the 31 negative offsets (of
35, five judges on seven panels), the 242 repeated
verdicts (of 252, three LLM judges and 12 errors on
each of seven panels), the 28 abstentions (of 3,414
ordinal pairs on seven panels), and the 8 separated,
8 at-parity, and 11 inconclusive comparisons (of the
27 pairings of Jev Choice and an LLM judge on the
nine panels). The nine-panel costs and wall times
are sums of the per-panel values, with DeepSeek
at its billed cost. The class tallies, confident-error
totals, and nine-panel sums of Appendices O, P,
and R are formed the same way.
What the scripts do not compute.Some state-
ments in the paper are not produced by the analysis
script. Values published by the source benchmarks,
such as ELLIPSE’s grade band and rater design,
are cited to their papers. Facts about the decision
models, such as their prices and the description
of their primitives, come from the vendors’ doc-
umentation, the Decider’s backbone and training
data from its public model card, and both models’
Decision Index scores from the index itself. Other
values were read or computed directly from the
recorded per-pair results, rater files, or run logs:
• the HealthBench strata of Table A1, the
FED-Turnfluentpair with four raters, the
absence of ties among the embedded binary
labels, and the twelve Luna verdicts served from
the spot check’s cached responses (Appendix A);
• the rater-reference rows for within-one and
embedded binary accuracy in Table C3;
• the per-criterion wrapper effects on HealthBench
(Appendix D);
• the FED-Turn mean probabilities per level, the
embedded binary disagreements between the

which Jev Score’s exact halves fall, and the

• the labels of the error_recovery dialogues that

The readings of individual pairs in Appendices F,
H, and J quote the records they read and are not
