N Whole-Rubric Judgments
This appendix describes how the whole-rubric runs
and the repeat runs of Section 3.2 were made and
reports the whole-rubric results that Table 2 con-
denses into one column. Figure N1 and the tables
below also support the whole-rubric remarks of
Sections 5 and 6. Every per-criterion value in these
tables is that of the main runs, and Jev’s verdicts,
costs, and times are the same in both modes. The
tables give Jev’s copy of each analysis. The De-
cider’s verdicts do not change with the mode either,
and Appendix R gives its copy of this condition
beside the other conditions of Table 2.
Prompt and reply.In the whole-rubric runs the
harness sends one request per unit. The system
prompt states the task and quotes as guides those
system prompts of Table A2, binary or multi-
choice, that match the kinds of criterion the unit
carries. It ends with the reply format: a JSON ob-
ject with one judgment per criterion identifier, each
holding a verdict or an option number and an ex-
planation. The user message lists the criteria first,
each tagged with its identifier and worded as in a
per-criterion request, options included, and then
gives the input and the submission once. Because
the whole-rubric runs reused the model names and
each judge’s logged seed, and the option shuffle
was keyed as in Appendix A, every criterion’s op-
tions appeared in their recorded order. The units,
model identifiers, temperatures, reasoning defaults,
and the harness’s retries were those of the main
runs, the response cache was on, and requests timed
out after 600 seconds.
Failed and incomplete replies.The harness
reads a reply one criterion at a time. A judgment
that is missing, duplicated, or unusable fails only
its own criterion, as a parse failure, and the pair is
excluded from every denominator like any other
failed pair. A request that fails, or whose reply
is not a readable JSON object, fails every pair of
its unit, and the unit then has no verdict record
at all, so none of its pairs can stand as UNMET
(compare Appendix A). All of Luna’s and Gem-
ini’s replies were complete. DeepSeek’s five failed
requests fell on RiceChem (two units, 14 pairs),
HealthBench (one unit, 2 pairs), LFQA (one unit,
3 pairs), and USR-TC (one unit, 5 pairs). The four
on RiceChem, HealthBench, and LFQA ran long
and then returned nothing, and the USR-TC re-
ply was cut short inside its JSON. DeepSeek’s two
single-criterion failures, both on ELLIPSE, were
a missing and a duplicated judgment. The judges
abstained about as often in either mode (Table N1),
except DeepSeek on USR-TC, whose abstentions
rose from 1 to 16.
52

Repeat runs.Each of the twelve runs, one per
ordinal panel, one per RiceChem question, and one
for HealthBench, contributed max(1,⌈0.1n⌉) of
its n units, drawn without replacement by a gener-
ator seeded with a CRC-32 checksum of the run’s
name. All three judges repeated the same units: 14
on RiceChem, 20 on HealthBench, 26 on ELLIPSE,
8 on FED-Turn, 3 on FED-Dialogue, 9 on Help-
Steer2, 12 on LFQA, 8 on USR-TC, and 6 on USR-
PC, 529 pairs in all. The repeat runs started from an
empty response cache and kept every other setting
of the whole-rubric runs. DeepSeek’s first repeat
attempt on ELLIPSE failed on all 26 units with
local network errors, before any request reached
the model and with nothing billed. We reran those
units with the same settings and keep the failed
attempt in the release. One DeepSeek repeat unit
on FED-Dialogue hit the 600-second timeout and
was left failed, so DeepSeek’s repeat covers 105
units and 519 pairs. A smoke test of three units
per run (36 units) preceded the runs and enters no
analysis.
Cost, tokens, and time.Whole-rubric judgments
bring the requests of each LLM judge down from
5,003 to 1,021 (Table N1). Prompt tokens fall only
to about a third, because a whole-rubric request,
with its longer system prompt and every criterion
of the unit (seePrompt and reply), carries about
1.6 times the prompt tokens of a per-criterion one.
Completion tokens fall by 29% to 34%, mostly
through reasoning tokens, which drop by about half
for Luna and a third for Gemini and DeepSeek. The
rest of the completion tokens, which in either mode
carry a verdict and an explanation for each criterion,
fall by 12% for Luna and rise by 19% for Gemini
and 7% for DeepSeek. On the ordinal panels the
cached whole-rubric replies hold 162,354 reason-
ing tokens for Luna, 1,635,010 for Gemini, and
1,939,805 for DeepSeek, against the per-criterion
counts of Appendix A. Cost, computed for both
modes by the rule of Section 3.4, falls by 45%
(Luna), 44% (Gemini), and 19% (DeepSeek). With
4.9 times as many requests, per-criterion judgments
cost Luna 1.82 times as much as whole-rubric judg-
ments, Gemini 1.79 times, and DeepSeek, by its
bills, 1.23 times.
Like its per-criterion cost ratio, Gemini’s whole-
rubric cost ratio is smallest on HealthBench (91×,
2.0 criteria per unit) and largest on FED-Dialogue
(312×, 10), but it drops from LFQA (157×, 3) to
HelpSteer2 (144×, 4), and RiceChem (155×, 6.8)
sits below every ordinal panel with five or more
criteria per unit. With whole-rubric judgments, a
unit’s median duration across the ordinal panels is
4.0 to 6.6 seconds for Luna, 4.9 to 15.7 for Gemini,
and 6.5 to 30.1 for DeepSeek. The 90th percentile
of DeepSeek’s unit durations reaches 127.1 sec-
onds on ELLIPSE and 160.2 on FED-Dialogue.
Table N1 gives wall times for both modes, but they
do not measure the judging mode (see Limitations).
The whole-rubric runs sent as many requests at a
time as Jev did. Whole-rubric Luna, Gemini, and
DeepSeek cost 16, 182, and 54 times as much as
Jev Choice and, at that concurrency, took 28, 45,
and 350 times as long (Table N1a). Equal concur-
rency leaves one difference between the runs that
favors the LLM judges in these time ratios. Jev
Choice and Jev Score shared one API, while each
LLM judge had its own provider.
Why cost falls less than requests.Completion
tokens, which fall least, make up most of each
judge’s cost. At the list prices of Appendix B,
which reproduce the recorded costs of Luna and
Gemini, they account for 56% of Luna’s per-
criterion cost and 68% of its whole-rubric cost,
and for 60% and 76% of Gemini’s. OpenRouter
splits the cost of each DeepSeek response between
prompt and completion, and by its bills completion
tokens took 79% of DeepSeek’s per-criterion cost
and 88% of its whole-rubric cost.
Prompt caching lowered the price of the sys-
tem prompt that every per-criterion request repeats.
The harness opens each per-criterion request with
a fixed system prompt, one for binary and one for
multi-choice criteria (Table A2), of 1,629 and 1,642
tokens as OpenAI’s o200k_base tokenizer counts
them. A provider’s prompt cache can serve such an
opening, at a lower price, to later requests that be-
gin with it. Luna’s provider served 79.7% of its per-
criterion prompt tokens from its prompt cache and
DeepSeek’s providers 67.5% (Table O1), against
72.0% and 57.6% of their whole-rubric prompt to-
kens. Gemini reported no cached tokens in either
mode.
On all but three of Luna’s per-criterion requests,
the prompt cache served no more than the opening
that the requests for one kind of criterion share. Of
the per-criterion replies in Luna’s response cache,
1,588 of the 1,589 for binary criteria report exactly
1,979 cached prompt tokens, and 3,480 of the 3,484
for multi-choice criteria exactly 1,895 (counts that
include the 70 spot-check replies of Appendix A

that the main run did not reuse). These counts, a
few hundred tokens above the length of the system
prompt, did not change with the unit or with its
number of criteria, which on the binary panels ran
from one to eight. Because each request gives
its criterion before the input and the submission,
it shared little more than that opening with the
requests for the unit’s other criteria, and all but
three of Luna’s per-criterion requests paid for the
unit’s text in full. OpenAI billed more than 99%
of Luna’s uncached prompt tokens in either mode
as writes to its prompt cache, at $0.25 per million
tokens instead of the uncached $0.20 (Appendix B),
so Luna paid more for the unit’s text than it would
have without the cache. Whole-rubric requests also
open with a fixed system prompt for each mix of
criterion kinds (seePrompt and reply), and on 985
of Luna’s 1,021 whole-rubric requests the prompt
cache served a constant opening of 2,305, 2,324, or
4,054 tokens.
Had Luna paid its uncached input price for ev-
ery prompt token, its per-criterion run would have
cost $3.45 instead of $1.84 and its whole-rubric
run $1.46 instead of $1.01, so that per-criterion
judgments would have cost 2.36 times as much
as whole-rubric ones instead of 1.82 times. At
DeepSeek’s list prices, its per-criterion run would
have cost $3.12 with its cached tokens and $4.24
without them, and its whole-rubric run $2.00 and
$2.31, which puts the per-criterion run at 1.56 times
the whole-rubric run’s cost with the prompt cache
and 1.83 times without it. Caching thus brought
54

the cost of per-criterion judgments closer to that of
whole-rubric ones for Luna and DeepSeek. Even
so, without the prompt cache the 4.9-fold rise in
requests would have raised Luna’s cost only 2.36-
fold, and Gemini’s cost, with no cached tokens,
rose 1.79-fold, close to Luna’s 1.82. We think the
gap between requests and cost comes mostly from
the completion tokens, which a judge spends on
every criterion in either mode, and otherwise from
the longer whole-rubric prompt and, for Luna and
DeepSeek, from the prompt cache.
On panels with more criteria per unit, per-
criterion judgments tend to cost more relative to
whole-rubric ones, though far less than in propor-
tion to their extra requests. On single panels, per-
criterion judgments cost Luna 1.35 (HealthBench
and LFQA) to 2.33 times (RiceChem) as much as
whole-rubric judgments and Gemini 1.33 (Health-
Bench) to 2.47 times (FED-Dialogue), while send-
ing 2.0 to 10.0 times as many requests (Tables B1
and N1b). By DeepSeek’s bills, they cost it 1.02
(USR-TC) to 2.06 times (FED-Dialogue) as much,
a range that may also reflect the routing described
below.
DeepSeek’s bill in the two modes.To split
DeepSeek’s billed cost by panel, each cached
whole-rubric reply is matched to its panel through
the explanations it shares with the verdict records,
and every successful reply found a match. For the
whole-rubric requests the billed cost is 3.7 times
the harness’s estimate over the nine panels, and 2.8
(FED-Dialogue) to 4.1 times (USR-TC) on single
panels, against 1.3 times for the per-criterion re-
quests over the nine panels (Appendix B). The two
estimates are not comparable, because LiteLLM’s
price map changed between the runs, as it did for
the effort runs (Appendix O). The per-criterion es-
timate prices tokens close to DeepSeek’s list prices,
whereas the whole-rubric estimate prices comple-
tion tokens at about $0.29 per million, under half
the list price of $0.60, so the harness-estimate row
of Table N1a overstates DeepSeek’s saving. Mea-
sured against its cost at list prices, DeepSeek’s bill
is 1.34 times as high for the per-criterion requests
and 1.70 times for the whole-rubric ones, and at list
prices whole-rubric judgments would have saved it
36%, less than Luna and Gemini saved, which fits
the larger share of DeepSeek’s cost that lies in com-
pletion tokens. OpenRouter’s routing may account
for part of DeepSeek’s smaller saving as billed. Its
698 answered whole-rubric requests on the ordinal
panels (two more failed) came from 15 upstream
providers, chiefly Parasail (256 requests), Together
(224), and CoreWeave (129), whereas Morph and
DeepInfra together had served just over half of its
per-criterion requests there.
Accuracy by judging mode.Table N2 sets each
judge’s two runs side by side on their common
pairs, using the paired statistics of Section 3.4 with
one Holm family of 27 judge–panel comparisons.
Four of these changes are separated, 16 at parity,
and 7 inconclusive. Of the 27 differences, 13 are
positive, 3 are exactly zero, and 11 are negative.
The mode raised Luna’s ELLIPSE accuracy by 5.9
points and Gemini’s by 6.1, the only two of the 27
per-panel changes significant once their bootstrap
p-values are corrected. Besides these ELLIPSE
gains, whose 95% intervals run from 3.8 to 8.1 and
from 4.1 to 8.1 points, the separated changes are
two losses, Luna’s of 5.3 points on LFQA and Gem-
ini’s of 4.5 on HelpSteer2. None of DeepSeek’s
changes is separated. Holm correction of the sign
test over units keeps Luna’s LFQA loss as well
as the two ELLIPSE gains, although the bootstrap
p-value for that loss exceeds 0.05 after correction
(0.070). Gemini’s HelpSteer2 loss has a bootstrap
p-value of 0.021, which the correction raises to
0.50.
Across the nine panels together, Luna’s pooled
gain is 1.5 points and Gemini’s 1.4, but both pooled
differences are negative once ELLIPSE’s 1,548
pairs are left out, and their intervals then include
zero. Luna’s ELLIPSE offset moved toward zero,
from −1.28 to −1.05 levels, and Gemini’s from
−0.77 to −0.67, whereas DeepSeek’s moved from
−1.16 to −1.22 levels, with an accuracy change at
parity. On a panel with embedded binary pairs the
pooled measure of Table N2 can differ in sign from
the measure of Table 1, which counts ordinal pairs
only; on USR-TC, for instance, Luna’s difference
is −1.1 points pooled and+1.4 on the ordinal pairs.
The largest changes in mean QWK are Luna’s fall
on LFQA, from 0.51 to 0.39, and DeepSeek’s rise
on USR-PC, from 0.42 to 0.51. Luna’s ELLIPSE
gain did not extend to every trait. On cohesion
its exact accuracy fell from 44.6% to 32.6%, while
syntax rose from 13.2% to 30.6%, vocabulary
from 12.0% to 26.0%, and phraseology from
7.8% to 19.4%.

with per-criterion judgments (Appendix C). Luna
on USR-TC loses parity, its bound rising from 4.8
to 5.3 points.
In pair-level sign tests eight comparisons are
significant before correction, four in each direc-
tion. Jev Choice leads Luna and Gemini on
RiceChem and leads Luna on HealthBench and
HelpSteer2; Luna and Gemini lead it on ELLIPSE
and FED-Turn. After Holm correction of these
sign tests, Luna’s and Gemini’s ELLIPSE leads
and Jev Choice’s HelpSteer2 lead over Luna re-
main. As with per-criterion judgments, Gemini’s
FED-Turn lead is significant pair by pair but not
separated. HelpSteer2’s constant predictor is now
significantly more accurate than each of Jev’s five
judges in pair-level sign tests, Gemini included
(p= 0.002 ). Neither Decider framing, whose ver-
dicts come from its single run, differs significantly
from it (Decider Choice p= 0.37 , Decider Score
p= 0.16).
Agreement between runs.Over all pairs, the
whole-rubric run gives a different verdict from the
per-criterion run on 24.3% of Luna’s pairs, 11.6%
of Gemini’s, and 22.6% of DeepSeek’s, the com-
plements of the W = P column of Table N4. On the
repeat units, rerunning the identical whole-rubric
requests changes 13.8%, 7.4%, and 12.1% of their
verdicts (the R = W column). There, the two com-
parisons across modes (the whole-rubric run and
the repeat run, each against the per-criterion run)
agree less often, on average, than the two whole-
rubric runs agree with each other. The shortfall
is 9.5 points for Luna, 3.5 for Gemini, and 10.3
for DeepSeek, each with a 95% interval that ex-
cludes zero (the cross-mode excess of Table N4).
The repeat pairs are few on single panels, 20 to
156, so Table N4 gives per-panel shares without
intervals, and our comparison of the modes rests
on the pooled rows. The cross-mode comparison
spans eight days (see Limitations). Identical ver-
dicts between the modes are rarest on ELLIPSE for
every judge, 61.0% of the pairs for Luna, 82.9%
for Gemini, and 67.8% for DeepSeek. In 6 of the
27 judge–panel cells the mean of the two cross-
mode shares matches or exceeds the share between
the two whole-rubric runs (Luna and DeepSeek
on LFQA; Gemini on HealthBench, USR-TC, and
USR-PC; DeepSeek on FED-Dialogue), each on 42
or fewer repeat pairs. For comparison, Luna’s two
per-criterion runs in the spot check of Appendix A
agreed on 86.6% of the 82 pairs of the ordinal pan-
els, and its two whole-rubric runs on 83.7% of the
392 repeat pairs on those panels.
Repeated errors and cascades.Across all of
Jev Choice’s errors on each ordinal panel, the
whole-rubric LLM judges repeat its answer more
often than the independence baseline predicts (Ta-
ble N5a). Except on FED-Dialogue, the share
of repeated errors exceeds the baseline most in
Jev’s highest confidence band. On FED-Dialogue,
among bands that hold at least 20 of Jev’s errors, it
exceeds the baseline by 26.1 points between confi-
dence 0.25 and 0.5, 26.7 between 0.5 and 0.75, and
24.5 in the top band.
Equation (K1) holds for every whole-rubric cas-
cade, so where an ordinal cascade beats its whole-
rubric fallback, Jev beats that fallback on the kept
pairs, by 3.0 points on HelpSteer2, 2.5 on LFQA,
2.1 on USR-TC, and 1.9 on USR-PC. These kept-
pair leads on HelpSteer2, LFQA, and USR-TC are
larger than the 0.7 to 1.9 points Jev holds over
the per-criterion fallbacks (Appendix K). On Help-
Steer2, where the whole-rubric cascades’ oracle
and cross-fitted gains over the best single judge
both peak (Table N5a), the cross-fitted gain of 2.5
points is positive in all 50 halvings. Gemini’s loss
of 4.5 points on HelpSteer2, from which these gains
arise, is separated but does not survive Holm cor-
rection (Table N2). The best HelpSteer2 cascade
defers 14.5% of the pairs but touches 46.7% of the
units. Among its kept pairs, those that only Jev gets
right outnumber those that only Gemini gets right
by 35 to 26 (pair-level sign test, p= 0.31 ), and its
accuracy still trails that of HelpSteer2’s constant
predictor.
Billing a whole-rubric fallback a full request for
each unit that has a deferred pair erodes the sav-
ing, most where a unit carries many criteria. At
the threshold that matches Gemini on USR-TC, the
deferred 38.1% of the pairs fall on 81.9% of the
units. The cross-fitted cascade that matches the
best LLM judge costs 99.1% of that judge’s cost on
FED-Turn instead of 45.5%, 57.0% on RiceChem
instead of 6.6%, and 93.4% on FED-Dialogue in-
stead of 77.9%, though the fallback’s change in
accuracy also moves these shares. ELLIPSE had
no saving to lose. Only on four panels does such a
cascade stay below half of the judge’s cost, at 23%
to 45%, with held-out accuracy from 0.6 points be-
low the judge to 2.4 above. Also charging the units
whose only deferral is a Jev Choice abstention, as
a live cascade would, raises the cost of matching

Juries.A median of the three whole-rubric LLM
judges is less accurate than the most accurate
matched judge on every panel, by up to 14.7 points
(ELLIPSE), and its 95% interval lies below zero
on RiceChem and ELLIPSE (Table N5a). With Jev
Choice as a fourth juror, no gain has an interval
that excludes zero; the largest, 2.8 points on LFQA,
has an interval from 0.0 to 5.9. On all 83 confi-
dent errors that every judge scored, the three-LLM
median picks Jev’s wrong answer.
The HealthBench physician reference.On the
50 HealthBench pairs with three or more physicians
(Appendix D), whole-rubric Luna reaches 62.7%
against the leave-one-out targets, 24.6 points below
the physician reference, with a 95% interval of
−45.7 to −5.3 points. It is the only judge in either
mode whose interval excludes zero. The intervals
of whole-rubric Gemini (86.5%, 0.8 points below)
and DeepSeek (81.7%, 5.6 below) include zero,
and Jev’s values are unchanged. These pairs come
from 20 completions, so the result is fragile.
O Reasoning Effort
This appendix describes the effort runs and the
calibration of Section 3.2 and reports what high
effort changed. Its tables hold the evidence for the
high-effort column of Table 2 and for the effort
remarks of Sections 5 and 6. Throughout, Jev’s
verdicts, costs, and times come from the main runs,
as do Luna’s and Gemini’s values in the medium
condition. Appendix R sets out the Decider’s copy
of the high condition, and what follows is Jev’s.
Requests.The reasoning defaults of the main
runs stated in Section 3.2 are those the vendors doc-
ument.7 In the effort runs, and in the calibration
runs that requested medium, every request named
a reasoning effort, medium or high, in LiteLLM’s
reasoning_effort parameter. LiteLLM, in ver-
sion 1.101.0, passes the value unchanged to Ope-
nAI as Luna’s reasoning effort, turns it into Gem-

(Section 5.2). Confident errors repeated: LLM verdicts on the confident-error sample that give Jev’s answer, out
of those given; one DeepSeek verdict is missing on USR-TC with whole-rubric judgments. bHealthBench: Jev

judge.
ini’s thinking level of the same name, with thought
summaries requested, and forwards it to Open-
Router as the reasoning effort of each DeepSeek
request. The request parameters stored with every
cached response of these runs carry the level, and
those of the main runs carry none. For DeepSeek
we know only which level was requested. Ev-
erything else repeated the main runs: the units,
the model identifiers, the temperatures, the logged
seeds (so every pair’s options appeared in their
recorded order), and the requests in flight (16 for
Luna, 20 for Gemini, and 32 for DeepSeek on the
ordinal panels, and 8 on the binary panels). Each
run began with an empty response cache, and a
request could run for 600 seconds before it timed
out, against 60 seconds in the main runs.
The effort and calibration runs were all made
on 27 September 2026, and DeepSeek’s medium
run overlapped its high run in time. On RiceChem,
two of DeepSeek’s high requests received no reply
and were not ended by the timeout. Each time we
stopped the runner and restarted it from its check-
60

point, which completed the pending units, so every
pair has a verdict record. The run’s summary, how-
ever, covers only its last session, and DeepSeek’s
high wall time was therefore not measured.
Failures and costs.Table O1 sums each run over
the nine panels. Luna’s one failed request at high
effort, on ELLIPSE, was an infrastructure failure,
and its pair is excluded like any failed ordinal pair.
Of DeepSeek’s 17 failed requests at high, one re-
turned a reply that could not be parsed, and the
other 16 fell in the same unclassified category as
the empty responses of its main run (Appendix A).
On the binary panels the harness again recorded
DeepSeek’s failed requests as UNMET verdicts.
Tables O3 and O4 keep them as verdicts, as Ap-
pendix A describes for the main runs. Setting them
aside instead would change no class or Holm result
in Table O3. Failed and hung requests left no usage
record, so any charge for them is missing from the
costs, as it is for the main runs.
As in Section 3.4, costs are list prices for Luna
and Gemini, which the harness applied alike in ev-
ery run, and OpenRouter’s billed cost for DeepSeek.
The harness’s own estimates for DeepSeek do not
compare across runs, because LiteLLM loads its
price map from the network each time it starts and
the map changed between runs. Besides effort,
prompt caching affects the costs. Providers bill
the prompt tokens they serve from their prompt
cache at a lower price, and a cache serves the open-
ing that a request shares with recent ones, such as
the system prompt with which every request of a
run begins (Appendix N). Luna’s cached share of
prompt tokens barely moved (79.7% in its main
run, 80.3% at high), Gemini reported no cached
tokens, and DeepSeek’s share rose from 67.5%
to 76.0% at medium and 74.6% at high. Rout-
ing also affects DeepSeek’s costs, and OpenRouter
routed DeepSeek differently on the two dates. It
sent the main run’s requests mostly to DeepInfra
(1,316), Morph (1,311), and Wafer (714), and those
of both effort runs mostly to Parasail (2,039 at
high and 2,111 at medium) and Together (1,286
and 1,269). We therefore compare effort levels on
tokens and on costs at fixed prices. These price
each run’s uncached prompt, cached prompt, and
completion tokens with one table per judge, which
holds the list prices for Luna and Gemini and, for
DeepSeek, the mean effective prices of its main
run’s billed responses. At those prices DeepSeek’s
high run would have cost 1.12 times its main run
and its medium run 0.85 times. Measured against
the medium condition, high effort raised the cost
by 25% for Luna, 108% for Gemini, and 33% for
DeepSeek as billed (Table O1b). With completion
tokens up 41% to 180% over that condition, the
LLM judges in the high condition cost 36 to 675
times as much as Jev Choice (Table O1a).
DeepSeek’s medium run used 0.82 times the
main run’s completion tokens and 0.80 times its rea-
soning tokens, and its high run 1.16 and 1.17 times,
against high-over-main reasoning-token ratios of
1.8 for Luna and 3.0 for Gemini. DeepSeek’s
high run used 1.5 times the reasoning tokens of
its medium run, which suggests that its default was
already close to high. Luna’s and Gemini’s high
runs took 1.44 and 3.54 times as long as their main
runs with the same requests in flight, though on
another day and with the longer timeout.
Calibration.The calibration ran each LLM
judge twice more on the 106 repeat units of
Section 3.2, once at medium effort (theexplicit-
medium run) and once with no effort requested.
The latter repeated the main run’s requests eight
days later, and we call it thesecond default run.
The high runs cover these units too. Table O2 com-
pares each of the three runs with the main run in
two ways. The first is the share of pairs on which a
run returns the main run’s verdict, less the second
default run’s share on the same pairs. If request-
ing an effort changed nothing, a run that requested
one and the second default run, both made on the
same day, eight days after the main run, would be
exchangeable with each other, and this difference
would be zero in expectation. Pairs that a failed
request touched in any of the runs compared are
set aside, which removes four of DeepSeek’s pairs
from its medium comparisons and three from its
high ones, in both cases including the single pair
of a HealthBench unit whose second default run
failed as a whole. The second comparison divides
a run’s completion tokens summed over each unit’s
requests by the main run’s, and the table gives the
median of these ratios over units. For Luna and
Gemini each unit’s prompt-token count was also
identical in all four runs, which confirms that the
runs sent the same prompts.
Luna’s explicit-medium run used almost the
same completion tokens per unit as its main run
(median ratio 0.97, against 1.01 for its second de-
fault run), and it gave the main run’s verdict 2.5
points more often than the second default run did,

runs of 19 September; Medium and High: the effort runs of 27 September (Section 3.2). Failed requests: requests
without a usable reply, of which the harness recorded DeepSeek’s on the binary panels as UNMET verdicts (next
row). Abstentions: answers of CANNOT_ASSESS or NA, not counting failures. Cached: the share of prompt
tokens that the provider served from its prompt cache; Gemini reported none. Reasoning tokens, counted within
the completion tokens, come from the cached responses; unlike Appendix A, Luna’s main-run count leaves out
the 70 spot-check responses that shared its cache. Cost: Luna and Gemini at list prices and DeepSeek at its billed
cost, with ratios to Jev Choice’s cost and wall time over the nine panels (Table B1); the wall-time ratios carry the
concurrency caveat of Section 4.2. DeepSeek’s wall time is given for its main run only, because that of its high run
was not measured and DeepSeek’s wall times are not compared across effort levels. (b) For Luna and Gemini the
high run over the main run; for DeepSeek the medium run over the main run and the high run over the medium
run, which was made the same day. Fixed prices: one price table per judge for every run (see text). DeepSeek’s
billed-cost ratio to its main run is left out because OpenRouter routed the runs of the two dates to different upstream
providers, so the difference between its billed main and high costs does not measure effort.
although on 529 pairs that difference is too uncer-
tain to show parity. Gemini’s explicit-medium run
matched its main run on both counts. Reading the
two judges’ main runs as medium thus rests on
agreement and tokens for Gemini and chiefly on
tokens for Luna. DeepSeek’s explicit-medium run
agreed with its main run at parity with the second
default run but spent fewer completion tokens per
unit (median ratio 0.88, against 1.05), while its high
run came closer to the second default run (1.12).
High effort raised the median ratio to 1.39 for Luna
and 2.26 for Gemini. Luna’s high verdicts still
agreed with its main run at parity with its second
default run, whereas Gemini’s agreed 3.0 points
less often, a separated difference that stays within
the 5-point margin.
DeepSeek’s baseline also reflects eight days of
changes on the serving side. Although its requests
repeated those of the main run byte for byte, its
second default run’s prompt-token count matched
the main run’s on only 5 of 103 units. Its explicit-
medium and high runs, made the same day as its
second default run, return that run’s verdict on
86.7% and 86.3% of the pairs, against 80.4% be-
tween the main run and the second default run.
Each of DeepSeek’s effort runs is thus compared
with a baseline that spans the same eight days as
its own comparison with the main run.

medium comparisons another. Of the 27 high-
effort differences, 12 are positive and 15 nega-
tive. Twenty-three are at parity, two of them also
separated, two are separated without parity, and
two are inconclusive. The four separated differ-
ences are Gemini’s gain and DeepSeek’s loss on

ELLIPSE, Luna’s loss on LFQA, and DeepSeek’s
gain on USR-TC, and correction leaves only Gem-
ini’s ELLIPSE gain of 2.5 points significant, in
the bootstrap and in both sign tests. At high effort
each judge’s accuracy pooled over the nine panels
moved by at most 0.5 points. Without ELLIPSE,
Gemini’s pooled difference turns negative. Among
DeepSeek’s medium comparisons, its ELLIPSE

or the sign test over units.
Against Jev Choice, the high runs change the
class of nine of the 27 comparisons (Table O3b).
Three separations lapse (Jev Choice’s lead over
Luna on RiceChem, Gemini’s lead on HelpSteer2,
and Luna’s lead on USR-PC), and no comparison
becomes separated, so Jev Choice keeps five of its
eight separations, none reversed. Of Jev Choice’s
three separated leads at high, one is over Gem-
ini (RiceChem) and two over Luna (HealthBench
and HelpSteer2), and Gemini keeps its two (EL-
LIPSE and FED-Dialogue). In the medium con-
dition two comparisons become separated, both
with DeepSeek, which was never separated from
Jev Choice in the main runs. Jev Choice leads on
RiceChem by 3.3 points and DeepSeek on USR-
TC by 4.7, which brings the medium condition to
10 separated comparisons of the 27. Neither stays
separated at high.
Shared errors, cascades, and juries.Table O4
repeats the analyses of Sections 5 and 6 for each
condition. In all three conditions judge–label agree-
ment stays below judge–judge agreement on all
seven ordinal panels, and every offset of Jev’s five
judges outside USR-TC is negative, so the short-
fall of Section 5.1 persists. On USR-TC, Gemini’s
offset of −0.005 levels in the main runs becomes
+0.02 at high. At high the least-agreeing judge pair
also exceeds the best judge–label QWK on USR-
TC and USR-PC, but no longer on ELLIPSE, where
it falls short by less than 0.01. Counting every error
of Jev Choice, the excess of repeated errors over
the baseline moves by less than 4 points on each
ordinal panel, most on LFQA. The confident-error
sample is the same in every condition, since it de-
pends on Jev’s verdicts alone. On four panels its
cut-off splits errors of tied confidence (Appendix J).
With oracle thresholds the cascade’s accuracy at
high fell on five panels, rose on three, and stayed
the same on HealthBench. Its gain over the best
single judge grew most on HelpSteer2, LFQA, and
HealthBench, each time because the best single
judge lost more accuracy than the cascade, whereas
on RiceChem the cascade itself improved, from
80.6% to 81.2%, with DeepSeek in place of Gemini
as its fallback. On HealthBench the cascade with
Gemini as fallback is right on 331 of 406 pairs in
both conditions, and Gemini alone on 323 and then
320. Among its kept pairs, 15 are right for Jev
alone and 4 for Gemini alone at high ( p= 0.019 ,
pair-level sign test), where the main runs had 18
and 10 ( p= 0.18 ). Eleven of Gemini’s verdicts
on kept pairs changed, 7 from right to wrong and
4 from wrong to right. On the deferred pairs at
high, Gemini alone is right on 33 and Jev alone on
16 (p= 0.021 ). With cross-fitted thresholds the
HealthBench cascade’s held-out accuracy fell from
80.0% to 79.6% and the best single judge’s from
78.5% to 77.3%. Gemini was the fallback chosen
in 53 of the 100 halves at high, against 71 in the
main runs. The cross-fitted gain had already risen
to 1.8 points in the medium condition, where only
DeepSeek’s verdicts differ from the main runs.
On LFQA, Luna’s accuracy fell from 71.5% to
68.4%, which leaves Jev Score (69.6%) the best

P Higher-Tier Judges
Here we set out how the higher-tier runs of Sec-
tion 3.2 were made, what they cost, how long they
took, and how accurate they were. Table 2 sum-
marizes them in its higher-tier column, and Sec-
tions 5.2 and 6.2 draw on the tables below. Jev’s
and the flash-tier judges’ values all come from the
main runs, and those of the high condition from
Appendix O. Apart from the most accurate judges
of the Decider’s copy, named in the accuracy para-
graph below, the tables and comparisons here are
Jev’s copy; the rest of the Decider’s copy of this
condition is in Appendix R.
Requests.The higher-tier judges’
replies name the models gpt-5.6-terra,
gemini-3.1-pro-preview, and
deepseek/deepseek-v4-pro-0813, and no re-
ply names another. Section 3.2 pairs each with
the flash-tier judge of the same vendor, but none
is necessarily that vendor’s most capable or most
recent model. 8 OpenAI released GPT-5.6 Terra
together with Luna on 9 July 2026 as the GPT-5.6
model that “balances intelligence and cost”, and
it names GPT-5.6 Sol, not Terra, as the GPT-5.6
flagship. Google lists Gemini 3.1 Pro, the only
Pro model of its 3.x line, as a Preview, released
on 19 February 2026, whereas Gemini 3.8 Flash
became generally available on 2 September 2026.
The DeepSeek V4 Pro release we used (0813)
predates V4.1 Flash, released on 10 September,
and DeepSeek reports that “Tests by multiple
parties put V4.1-Flash ahead of V4-Pro”.
No request named a reasoning effort or a think-
ing level, as the request parameters stored with all
5,003 cached responses of each judge confirm, so
the vendors’ defaults applied. OpenAI documents
medium for GPT-5.6 models. For Gemini 3.1 Pro,
whose thinking cannot be switched off, Google
documents high, one level above the default of
8Vendor documentation, accessed 2 October 2026:
https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/
gpt-5.6-terra,
https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/changelog,
andhttps://developers.openai.com/api/docs/
guides/reasoning(Terra),
https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/models,
https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/changelog,
andhttps://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/

//api-docs.deepseek.com/quick_start/pricing,
https://api-docs.deepseek.com/news/news260910,
andhttps:
//api-docs.deepseek.com/guides/thinking_mode/
(DeepSeek V4 Pro).
Gemini’s main run (Section 3.2). Gemini 3.1 Pro
nonetheless emitted 1.01 times the reasoning to-
kens of that run and about a third of those of Gem-
ini’s high run (Table O1). DeepSeek documents
high for its own API, and through OpenRouter the
default is not documented, but DeepSeek V4 Pro
emitted as many reasoning tokens as DeepSeek did
(Table P1b).
From its flash sibling each higher-tier judge kept
the temperature, which every request carried (1.0
for Terra, as for Luna, and 0 for the other two);
the logged seeds, so that every pair’s options ap-
peared in the order recorded for that sibling; and
the number of requests in flight (16 for Terra, 20 for
Gemini 3.1 Pro, and 32 for DeepSeek V4 Pro on
each ordinal panel, against 8 on each binary one).
Requests timed out after 600 seconds, ten times the
limit of the main runs, and went through LiteLLM
1.101.0. The three judges ran in parallel, starting
at 13:20 UTC on 2 October 2026, and the full runs
ended 5,907 seconds after they began. A pilot on
36 units (211 requests per judge) with the same
seeds, made just before, had no failure and enters
no analysis. OpenRouter did not pin DeepSeek V4
Pro to a provider, and 13 upstream providers served
its 5,003 replies, Baidu 3,518 of them (70%), Ion-
stream 708, and Wafer 307. DeepSeek’s own API
served none.
Failures and costs.Every request of the three
judges returned a usable reply, and no unit failed
(Table P1a). The judges abstained with NA on 19
(Terra), 32 (Gemini 3.1 Pro), and 23 (DeepSeek V4
Pro) ordinal pairs, and with CANNOT_ASSESS
on 13 (Gemini 3.1 Pro) and 2 (DeepSeek V4 Pro)
embedded binary pairs, all on USR-TC; none ab-
stained on the binary panels. As with DeepSeek’s
high run, two of DeepSeek V4 Pro’s requests, one
on an ELLIPSE unit and one on a FED-Turn unit,
hung without a reply. After its runner had writ-
ten no verdict record for about six minutes (373
and 358 seconds), we stopped it each time and re-
sumed the run from its checkpoint; the resumed
sessions completed the pending units without du-
plicating any. DeepSeek V4 Pro therefore judged
the seven ordinal panels in three sessions, and its
run summaries, which cover only the last session,
do not measure its wall time over the nine pan-
els. Added up, the sessions took 5,826 seconds,
stalls included, and about 5,095 seconds without
the stalls. Four of its panels have measured times.
RiceChem (1,733 seconds) and HealthBench (942)

DeepSeek’s off-peak prices for DeepSeek V4 Pro; and the bill, which for DeepSeek V4 Pro sums the costs

each ran as a session of its own, and USR-TC (144)
and USR-PC (125) were judged wholly in the last
session. FED-Dialogue, LFQA, and HelpSteer2
finished within the second session, which no run
summary covers, and only ELLIPSE and FED-Turn
straddle a stopped session. Every other run went
through without a stop. Terra and Gemini 3.1 Pro
took 35 and 162 times as long as Jev Choice, at
the main runs’ concurrency (Table P1a). DeepSeek
V4 Pro’s times are not compared with DeepSeek’s,
whose main run had a 60-second timeout, failed
nine requests, and was routed to a different mix of
providers. The time ratios to the flash siblings in
Table P1b compare runs that kept the requests in
flight but were made 13 days apart.
Terra cost 233 times as much as Jev Choice,
Gemini 3.1 Pro 984 times, and DeepSeek V4 Pro
67 times as billed (Table P1a). The costs of Terra
and Gemini 3.1 Pro are their list prices, which the
harness recorded and which we take as billed. Ope-
nAI’s prompt cache served 80.3% of Terra’s prompt
tokens, and nearly all the rest, 2,359,194 tokens,
were written to that cache, which OpenAI prices at
$2.50 per million, 1.25 times its input rate. Gemini
3.1 Pro reported cached tokens on 31 replies only.
DeepSeek V4 Pro’s bill, $4.27, is 0.39 times its
cost at DeepSeek’s off-peak list prices, $11.05 (the
runs fell outside DeepSeek’s peak hours), because
OpenRouter sent most requests to Baidu. The har-
ness recorded $0.016 more than the list price, from
67

50 replies costed above the off-peak rates, a dif-
ference we could not trace. Priced at its recorded
cost, DeepSeek V4 Pro comes to 175 times Jev
Choice’s cost instead of 67 times, and to 3.52 times
DeepSeek’s recorded cost instead of 1.02 times its
bill. DeepSeek’s own bill exceeded the harness’s
estimate (Appendix B) and DeepSeek V4 Pro’s
fell below it, so the billed ratio between the two
DeepSeek judges reflects routing more than tier,
and the ratio at list prices is the better guide.
In tokens, Terra emitted 0.66 times Luna’s com-
pletion tokens and 0.45 times its reasoning tokens,
yet cost 7.99 times as much. The other two higher-
tier judges emitted about as many completion and
reasoning tokens as their siblings, and DeepSeek
V4 Pro read 1.12 times as many prompt tokens.
Completion tokens make up 76.3% of Gemini 3.1
Pro’s $31.41 on ELLIPSE. Over the nine panels,
Gemini 3.1 Pro’s mean request took 4.31 times as
long as Gemini’s, and its slowest, 584.3 seconds,
came within 16 seconds of the timeout. Requests in
flight when DeepSeek V4 Pro’s runner was stopped,
and the two failed attempts behind Gemini 3.1 Pro
requests that LiteLLM retried, left no usage record,
so their charges, if any, are missing here as well.
With the pilot, the higher-tier study thus cost at
least $83.99 as billed, against $91.11 as recorded
and $91.09 at list prices.
Accuracy.The higher tier is not reliably more
accurate. Each higher-tier judge is compared with
its flash sibling in Table P2a, on the pairs both
scored, and the 27 per-panel comparisons form one
family for Holm correction. The 27 differences
run from −5.4 points (Gemini 3.1 Pro on Help-
Steer2) to +7.5 (DeepSeek V4 Pro on ELLIPSE),
with a median of +0.5, and 14 of them are pos-
itive, 2 zero, and 11 negative. By the classes of
Section 3.4, 10 are at parity only, 6 separated only,
1 both separated and at parity, and 10 inconclusive.
Uncorrected pair-level sign tests favor the higher
tier in three comparisons (Terra and DeepSeek V4
Pro on ELLIPSE, and DeepSeek V4 Pro on FED-
Dialogue) and the flash sibling in four, all four
with Gemini ahead of Gemini 3.1 Pro (ELLIPSE,
FED-Turn, FED-Dialogue, and HelpSteer2). Af-
ter correction only the two ELLIPSE gains remain,
Terra’s (pHolm = 2×10 −8) and DeepSeek V4 Pro’s
(3×10 −10), in all three tests. Over all nine panels
together, Terra’s gain over Luna (2.1 points) and
DeepSeek V4 Pro’s over DeepSeek (3.4) are both
separated, but without ELLIPSE they shrink to 0.5
points (p= 0.52 ) and 1.5 ( p= 0.028 ). Gemini
3.1 Pro loses 1.8 points to Gemini, and 1.6 without
ELLIPSE (p= 0.002 ). Against Gemini’s high run,
whose requested level is Gemini 3.1 Pro’s docu-
mented default, Gemini 3.1 Pro loses 2.5 points
pooled. It trails that run on six panels, ties it on
USR-PC, and leads only on the binary panels, and
its ELLIPSE deficit of 4.7 points survives correc-
tion in all three tests. Against the high condition
Terra also gains on FED-Turn after correction, but
only in the pair-level test and the bootstrap (Ta-
ble P2b). Pooled over all their scored pairs, the
higher-tier judges are right on 52.2% (Terra), 55.4%
(Gemini 3.1 Pro), and 54.3% (DeepSeek V4 Pro)
of them, against 50.1%, 57.3%, and 51.0% for their
siblings and 51.1% for Jev Choice.
Among Jev’s framings and the judges of the
higher-tier condition, Gemini 3.1 Pro is the most
accurate on HealthBench, ELLIPSE, HelpSteer2,
and USR-PC; DeepSeek V4 Pro on FED-Dialogue;
Jev Choice on RiceChem; and Jev Score on FED-
Turn, LFQA, and USR-TC. Across Jev’s fram-
ings and every LLM judge of the main runs, the
high condition, and the higher tier, however, no
higher-tier judge is the most accurate on any panel.
On HealthBench, Gemini 3.1 Pro only ties Gem-
ini (79.6%). Elsewhere the most accurate judge
is Gemini on FED-Dialogue (56.9%) and Help-
Steer2 (54.6%), Gemini at high effort on ELLIPSE
(32.3%) and USR-TC (67.6%), Luna on LFQA
(71.6%), Luna and Gemini jointly on USR-PC
(60.6%), Jev Choice on RiceChem (81.0%), and
Jev Score on FED-Turn (66.7%). In the Decider’s
copy of the higher-tier condition, the most accu-
rate judges are Decider Choice (RiceChem and
HelpSteer2), Decider Score (ELLIPSE, LFQA, and
USR-TC), Terra (FED-Turn), DeepSeek V4 Pro
(FED-Dialogue), and Gemini 3.1 Pro (USR-PC),
while on HealthBench Gemini 3.1 Pro ties Decider
bare Noul. When the Decider’s framings replace
Jev’s in the pool of all three conditions, Terra is
the most accurate judge on FED-Turn (61.1%), and
Gemini, Gemini 3.1 Pro, and Decider bare Noul
tie on HealthBench (79.6%). Jev Choice’s 27 com-
parisons with the higher-tier judges fall into 6 sep-
arated, 9 at parity, and 12 inconclusive (Table P4b).
Its two separated leads are both on binary panels,
and its four separated deficits are the three EL-
LIPSE comparisons and Terra on FED-Turn, whose
lead’s interval starts only 0.17 points above zero.
Correction, with any of the three tests, keeps only
the three ELLIPSE deficits.

Shared errors, cascades, and juries.The higher
tier leaves the shortfall of Section 5.1 in place (Ta-
ble P4b). Judge–judge agreement exceeds judge–
label agreement on all seven ordinal panels (0.375
against 0.189 on ELLIPSE and 0.596 against 0.518
on USR-TC), and 31 of the 35 offsets of the five
judges on the ordinal panels are negative. On USR-
TC, Jev Choice’s offset is 0.00 levels and Terra’s
+0.13, and Gemini 3.1 Pro is the only judge with a
negative offset there. On the confident-error sam-
ple the higher-tier judges give Jev Choice’s answer
in 238 of their 251 verdicts, against 130.4 that the
independence baseline expects. Terra repeats 79
of the 84 errors (44.5 expected), Gemini 3.1 Pro
82 of its 83 verdicts (43.6), and DeepSeek V4 Pro
77 of 84 (42.3). Gemini 3.1 Pro’s missing verdict
is a genuine CANNOT_ASSESS on a USR-TC
uses_knowledge pair that Jev Choice judged UN-
MET at confidence 0.92 against a label of MET.
On HealthBench the higher-tier judges give Jev
Noul’s wrong answer in 32 of 36 verdicts on its
sampled errors (Terra 12, Gemini 3.1 Pro 9, and
DeepSeek V4 Pro 11), where 9.2 are expected.
Adding Jev Noul’s sampled HealthBench errors
to the confident-error sample gives 96 errors. On
these, no vendor’s higher tier repeats more than
four fewer than its flash sibling, and none signifi-
cantly fewer. Terra repeats 91 and Luna 92, Gemini
3.1 Pro 91 and Gemini 90, and DeepSeek V4 Pro
88 and DeepSeek 92 ( p= 0.29 , sign test on the
errors only one of them repeats). Compared with
DeepSeek’s high run, which repeats 95, DeepSeek
V4 Pro’s 88 gives a nominal p= 0.039 , uncor-
rected for the other comparisons. Pooled over the
seven ordinal panels, the share of repeated errors
rises with Jev Choice’s confidence band as in the

main runs, from 26.0% in the lowest band to 83.0%
in the highest (main runs: 29.2% to 84.5%).
The best higher-tier cascade gains 3.7 points
over the condition’s best single judge on Help-
Steer2, with an oracle threshold and with cross-
fitted thresholds alike, as that judge is weak there
(Table P3a). With DeepSeek V4 Pro as fallback at
τ= 0.30 , it defers 14.5% of the pairs and reaches
52.8%. Gemini 3.1 Pro, the condition’s best single
judge, reaches 49.1% on the cascade pair set, and
Gemini 54.3% on the same pairs, which the cas-
cade trails by 1.4 points. Measured against the best
single judge of either tier (Table 2), the cascade
thus loses 1.4 points with its oracle threshold and
3.0 with cross-fitted ones. That judge is, on every
panel, the one Table 3 names best with Jev first,
and on HealthBench Gemini 3.1 Pro ties it exactly.
There the best higher-tier cascade, Jev Noul with
DeepSeek V4 Pro at τ= 0.12 and 16.7% of pairs
deferred, reaches 81.3% against 79.6%. Its gain,
1.7 points with an oracle threshold and 1.3 with
cross-fitted ones, is the largest under the either-tier
yardstick. Under this yardstick, cross-fitted cas-
cades fall short on seven of the nine panels. Where
single judges tie on a training half, these cross-
fitted gains depend on the order in which the tied
judges are considered, by up to 0.4 points on four
panels, though the largest gain under this yard-
stick, with oracle or cross-fitted thresholds, does
not move. Because each half picks its best single
judge on its own units, a wider pool of candidates
can also raise a cross-fitted gain, as it does for the
main runs’ cascades (1.7 against 1.5 points; Ta-
ble P4b). On ELLIPSE the best cascade defers
98.5% of the pairs to Gemini 3.1 Pro and equals
it, and only DeepSeek V4 Pro’s curve peaks at full
deferral. On RiceChem the cascade with DeepSeek
V4 Pro ties Jev Choice exactly (81.0%).
Equation (K1) holds for every higher-tier cas-
cade, and Table P3b gives its terms for each fall-
back on HelpSteer2 and HealthBench. On Help-
Steer2 at τ= 0.30 , Jev keeps 85.5% of the pairs
and leads DeepSeek V4 Pro on them by 7.6 points,
which puts the cascade 6.5 points above that fall-
back. The gain thus comes from keeping Jev’s
verdicts on pairs where this fallback does worse.
Correcting Jev’s errors instead would require a fall-
back whose errors do not coincide with Jev’s. Gem-

HelpSteer2, trails Jev by 3.0 points on the kept
pairs, and on HealthBench Jev’s kept-pair lead of
1.9 points over Gemini 3.1 Pro yields a cascade
only 1.2 points above it. Pooled over the nine pan-
els, cross-fitted cascades are 1.9 points more ac-
curate with Terra than with Luna as fallback, 3.1
more with DeepSeek V4 Pro than with DeepSeek,
and 1.9 less with Gemini 3.1 Pro than with Gem-
ini (against the high condition, +2.0, +3.2, and
−2.5). Matching the condition’s best LLM judge,
a cross-fitted cascade costs less than half as much
as that judge on seven panels, from 5.6% of its cost
(RiceChem) to 46.8% (FED-Turn), and an oracle-
threshold cascade does so on six.

Q Practical Recommendations
Table Q1 turns the results into recommendations,
each marked with the status of its evidence. Where
the evidence names a decision model, it comes
from that model’s copy of the analyses (Section 3.1;
Appendix R), and a recommendation that names
Jev rests on Jev’s copy alone.
Rubric wording and offset correction.The
rubric-writing rows of Table Q1 rest on observa-
tions that we did not test by intervention. The
judges read top levels worded as absolutes (“En-
tirely accurate”, “Fully clear and internally con-
sistent throughout”) differently from the raters, in
both directions. Jev Choice seldom used LFQA’s
absolute top factuality level, which the raters of-
ten chose (Appendix H), whereas on five of its con-
fident errors all five judges chose HelpSteer2’s ab-
solute coherence top level where the label was one
level lower (Appendix J). Decider Choice went the
other way on LFQA, choosing the top factuality
level for 70 of the 117 answers it rated, where
the labels hold it for 50 of 120. Jev Choice never
predicted the middle level on FED-Turn correct
and fluent, whose texts state yes-or-no properties
(Appendix E). One global correction would not fit
every trait, since Jev Choice’s per-trait ELLIPSE
offsets in Table Q1 span more than a level.
Evaluating rubric judges.Beyond the rows of
Table Q1, evaluations of rubric judges should sepa-
rate unanimous from split pairs before interpreting
errors, report each judge’s framing and decoding
rule (and, for an LLM judge, its judging mode),
and, when attributing a property to one judge, com-
pare several judges on identical pairs. For com-
bined judges, such as cascades and juries, evalu-
ations should also report the share of repeated er-
rors against an independence baseline (Section 5.2)
alongside any gain in accuracy. Before trusting a
cascade, one can make the same comparison on
the labeled pairs that set its threshold (Jung et al.,
2025), to see how far its judges err alike. Cor-
related errors may also explain why our juries of
diverse judges (Verga et al., 2024) did not help
(Section 6.2).
R A Second Decision Model: Perplexity’s
Decider
This appendix records how Perplexity’s Decider
was run and reports its copy of the study. The
Decider judged the same 5,003 pairs as Jev with
the framings and wrapper of Table A2 unchanged,
and its answers passed through the same analysis
code. Its copy reuses the recorded LLM verdicts,
and a check of the 39,709 values that depend on
the LLM judges alone found each one identical in
the two copies. Every value below belongs to the
Decider’s copy, with the Decider’s matched judges,
five judges, and confidence (Section 3.1), and none
is set against Jev’s. Jev remains the reference. We
designed the study and chose its analyses on Jev,
added the Decider when those analyses were done,
and checked on it a list of claims fixed before its
first answer on a study item (Table R7).
Model and documentation.Each request asked
for the model pplx-decider-v1.1-27b, and each
of the 6,283 replies of the full run and the 266
of the repeat named it. Perplexity launched the
API in October 2026 and released version 1.1 on
5 October. Its API documentation9 lists that iden-
tifier and one other, pplx-decider-v1-27b, and
no alias. A request holds the model name, the
record of Section 3.1, and named questions of the
three primitives. The documentation describes no
web search or retrieval. Before the run, the code
that builds Jev’s requests built every body offline
with the Decider’s model name and checked it
against the documented limits, and every body sent
matched one built in that check. The manifest of
every run names the Decider, and no run reads an
answer of Jev’s. Perplexity calls the model a de-

cision model and mentions Jev only as a column
of the benchmark tables on its model cards. The
word “reproduction” comes from the community
Decision Index of Section 3.1, which tracks open
reproductions of Jev and describes itself as unof-
ficial and unaffiliated with TypeSafe. We quote
only the index’s edition 0.3, whose data and our
reading of them both date from 6 October 2026. It
ranks the Decider’s version 1.1 first of 112 entries
with a score of 62.75 and gives Jev 60.11, a score
it displays as tied for second. We do not quote the
score of an earlier edition that Perplexity’s model
card reports from its own run.
Lineage.The model card gives Qwen3.8-27B as
the base model, fine-tuned in full, with a decision
readout in place of the layer that generates text.
It credits version 1.1’s gains mainly to removing
the causal mask from the full-attention layers and
partly to added training data. Its data manifest
lists the sources behind the counts given in the
Limitations. Among them are HelpSteer2’s labels
for all four attributes that our HelpSteer2 panel asks
about, and rows from HelpSteer and HelpSteer3,
but the manifest does not say whether the responses
of our panel are among the HelpSteer2 rows. No
source it lists names ELLIPSE or its essays, FED,
USR, LFQA, RiceChem, or HealthBench, and the
rows it calls original are not itemized. Beyond
reading these source names, we did not check the
training rows against the panels’ items.
Runs.The full run lasted from 04:21:22 to
04:36:45 UTC on 7 October 2026. Because the
rate limit applies to the whole organization, its four
steps ran one after another. The ordinal panels
went first under Decider Choice and then under
Decider Score. RiceChem followed under its three
binary framings, for the panel and for the full set of
1,240 responses (Appendix D), and HealthBench
came last with four request sets, its three framings
and the ablation with the physician-written ideal,
whose answers serve here only to check the repeat.
The repeat began four seconds after the full run
ended and finished at 04:38:04. Each step kept 8
requests in flight and spaced them at no more than
9 per second, under the documented 10, and no
one-second window held more than 10 sends. An
attempt timed out after 30 seconds, and a request
could make up to 7 attempts within 180 seconds,
waiting as long as a rate-limit response asked.
Of the full run’s 6,724 attempts, 438 met a rate-
limit response and 3 timed out. Every request was
answered, so no unit, criterion, or framing has a
judge-call failure. Most rate-limit responses, 393 of
them and all but one on RiceChem, gave no waiting
time and reported that a maximum number of re-
quests had been reached, which fits the token limit
that the documentation mentions without a value.
The other 45 asked for a one-second wait. The
repeat met 13 rate-limit responses and no timeout.
Two probe requests on a synthetic one-sentence
submission preceded everything. A smoke run of
30 requests, one unit of each ordinal panel, of each
RiceChem question, and of HealthBench, under
each framing, then checked the answer types, the
probabilities, and the response headers. With the
probes and a burst of requests at the client’s rate,
it returned 92 replies, which enter none of the re-
ported results or costs except the check of duplicate
requests below.
Price, cost, and time.Decider Choice cost
$0.058 over the nine panels and took 149 seconds;
Luna, Gemini, and DeepSeek cost 32, 357, and
73 times as much and took 5.8, 6.4, and 42 times
as long (Table R1). In every condition the LLM
judges’ nine-panel totals came to at least 17.6 times
Decider Choice’s cost and, where measured, at
least 5.4 times its wall time, whole-rubric Luna
setting both minima (Table R5). The cost rule of
Section 3.4 prices the Decider as its quickstart, API
reference, and pricing page did on 6 October 2026.
The changelog’s launch entry alone gives another
price, $0.04 per million input tokens, and no page
calls either price introductory. At the launch price
each multiple of Decider Choice’s cost would halve
(Table R5). Luna’s per-criterion multiple would
fall from 32.0 to 16.0, and whole-rubric Luna’s
from 17.6 to 8.8. The full run and the repeat used
18.3 million input tokens and cost $0.367 at the
documented price, or $0.733 at the launch price.
That total leaves out the 92 replies that preceded
the run and three attempts that timed out, which
carried 6,176 input tokens. Most of the Decider
Choice run’s wall time was set by the rate limit. At
9 requests per second, the 1,044 attempts of the
nine-panel Choice run need 116 seconds, 78% of
its 148.6, and they spent 58% of their time in the
client’s limiter. On the ordinal panels alone, its
706 attempts at that rate need 98.5% of the 79.7
seconds they took. One HealthBench request of
that run timed out after 30 seconds and was an-
swered on its retry, which added about 22 seconds
to that run’s time in Table R1b, and we keep the

Confidence and abstentions.The documenta-
tion says only that the confidence returned by
Choice and Score is not the top probability. For De-
cider Choice it equals(Kpmax −1)/(K−1) of Ap-
pendix I on all 3,414 ordinal answers, up to floating-
point rounding. Decider Score’s confidence departs
from that form (median deviation 0.14) and is used
as returned, and Decider Noul’s comes fromP(yes)
as in Section 3.1. Appendix F breaks down Decider
Choice’s 36 abstentions, 18 of which fall on FED-
Dialogue error_recovery. On that criterion its
probability on the NA option rises with the number
of raters who answered N/A (Spearman 0.65 over
25 dialogues, p= 5×10 −4), and it abstained on all
7 dialogues that drew at least two N/A answers. It
never chose CANNOT_ASSESS on a binary panel,
the full RiceChem set included.

How firm are these 13 separations? Holm correc-
tion within the 27 comparisons keeps 11 (seven De-
cider leads and four deficits) and drops RiceChem
Luna and FED-Dialogue Luna. Resampling Help-
Steer2’s prompts, LFQA’s questions, and USR’s
contexts in place of units keeps all 13. The sign
test over units also finds 13 significant comparisons,
but it trades FED-Dialogue Luna for HealthBench
Gemini (20 units against 38), a deficit on a binary
panel. Gemini’s 4.2-point lead on HealthBench
falls just short of separation. With the seed of our
analysis code, its 95% interval ends exactly at zero.
Rerun with 200 fresh seeds, the bootstrap separated
it in 31.5% of them, and each such run would count
14 separated comparisons out of 27, one of them a
deficit on a binary panel. The pair-level sign tests
find 7 significant Decider leads and 4 significant
deficits. Over all 27 comparisons the equivalence
76

bound has a median of 8.9 points and is within 7.5
points for 11.
The separations follow the panels more than the
LLM judges. Nine of the 13 fall on ELLIPSE,
HelpSteer2, and FED-Turn, one against each LLM
judge, and the other four on RiceChem and FED-
Dialogue, against Luna and Gemini, so DeepSeek
is separated from Decider Choice three times. De-
cider Choice leads on RiceChem, ELLIPSE, and
HelpSteer2 (by 8 to 33 points on the last two) and
trails on FED-Turn and FED-Dialogue. Among the
matched judges, it is the most accurate on the three
panels where it leads, second on LFQA, third on
HealthBench, USR-TC, and USR-PC (tied there
with DeepSeek), and fourth on FED-Turn and FED-
Dialogue. Gemini leads the matched judges, or ties
for the lead, on FED-Turn, FED-Dialogue, USR-
TC, and USR-PC, and Luna leads on LFQA.
Decider Score is separated ahead of Decider
Choice on two ordinal panels and behind it on two,
and is the most accurate of the Decider’s five judges
on ELLIPSE, LFQA, and USR-TC. In 7 of its 21
pair-level sign tests against an LLM judge on or-
dinal pairs, it is significantly ahead, and in 7 it is
significantly behind. Its ELLIPSE lead over De-
cider Choice falls short of parity, with a bound of
5.2 points. Neither Decider framing differs signif-
icantly from a no-read baseline, as Appendix H
shows for HelpSteer2’s constant predictor. Against
LFQA’s provenance baseline, Decider Choice is
right alone on 44 pairs and the baseline on 41
(p= 0.83 ), and Decider Score on 48 against 35
(p= 0.19).
Offsets and agreement.Decider Score’s offsets
are negative on five of the seven ordinal panels (Ta-
ble C3h), so 28 of the 35 offsets of the Decider’s
five judges are negative. The three positive offsets
of Decider Choice have 95% intervals over resam-
pled units of +0.003 to +0.167 levels on Help-
Steer2, +0.062 to +0.213 on LFQA, and 0.000 to
+0.181 on USR-TC. The first two owe much to sin-
gle criteria, HelpSteer2 correctness (+0.41) and
LFQA factuality (+0.21), on which all three
LLM judges have negative offsets. For both De-
cider framings the offset largest in size is FED-
Dialogue’s, not ELLIPSE’s. Over the 35 ordinal cri-
teria, all four matched judges’ offsets are negative
on 22 and positive on one. Decider Choice’s own
QWK with the labels exceeds its mean QWK with
the three LLM judges on ELLIPSE (0.42 against
0.35) and HelpSteer2 (0.47 against 0.46), although
across all four matched judges agreement with one
another still exceeds agreement with the labels on
both panels (Table 1b). In the main runs even the
matched judges’ weakest pairwise agreement ex-
ceeds their best agreement with the labels on two
ordinal panels (Table R5).
Confidence and repeated errors.The AUROC
of 0.52 on ELLIPSE has a 95% interval of 0.487 to
0.550 over resampled units, and FED-Dialogue’s
0.60, from 227 pairs, an interval of 0.488 to 0.716
that also contains one half (Table R3a). On LFQA
factuality the AUROC is 0.60 over 117 pairs,
and on USR-PCengaging it is 0.43, where Decider
Choice is wrong on 88.3% of 60 pairs.
Over all errors, every ordinal panel has more
repeated errors than the baseline predicts, by 11.4
to 21.7 points on six panels and by 2.1 on ELLIPSE.
Wherever the top band holds errors, its repeat share
and its excess over the baseline are the largest of the
panel, 83.3% to 100% against 40.4% to 58.3%, but
only FED-Turn and LFQA have the 20 top-band
errors that Figure I1b needs. Two bands fall at or
below the baseline, ELLIPSE’s lowest, with one
error, and LFQA’s band from 0.25 to 0.5. Treating
abstentions and judge-call failures as verdicts that
do not repeat moves no band down by more than
1.6 points. The confident-error sample holds 76
ordinal and 8 embedded binary pairs, and Decider
Score gives Decider Choice’s answer on all 84. The
LLM judges repeat 23 of their 24 verdicts on the
binary pairs, and when the 12 errors of each panel
are drawn from ordinal pairs alone they still repeat
85.3% of their verdicts against 49.0% expected. Of
the 72 sampled errors on the six multi-rater panels,
67 fall on split pairs and 5 on pairs whose raters
were unanimous.
On ELLIPSE the 12 sampled errors are nine
grammar pairs placed at level 2 against a label of 3,
one placed at 2 against 4, and two cohesion pairs
placed at 3 against 4. The LLM judges are right on
none of their 36 verdicts. Of the 20 verdicts below
even the Decider’s level, Luna gives 11, DeepSeek
8, and Gemini 1, and 19 of Luna’s and DeepSeek’s
24 verdicts sit one level under the Decider’s. Over
all 819 ELLIPSE errors of Decider Choice, 1,266
LLM verdicts repeat its answer, 942 lie further be-
low the label, and 172 are correct. Luna is exact on
5.0% of the pairs where Decider Choice is wrong
and on 24.3% of those where it is right, Gemini
on 9.4% and 52.8%, and DeepSeek on 6.6% and
24.5%.

Table R3: The Decider’s confidence and the errors it shares with the LLM judges, with the definitions of Tables I1
and J1. (a) No ELLIPSE pair reaches confidence 0.75. (b) The ELLIPSE top band holds no error. (c) Conf.:

HealthBench: the six false positives of Decider Noul with the highest P(MET) and its six false negatives with the
lowest, whose physicians were unanimous on all 12.
On HealthBench, Decider Noul’s 114 errors are
78 false positives and 36 false negatives, and the
sampled ones sit at P(MET) of 0.983 to 0.988 and
0.0011 to 0.0028. Most of the LLM judges’ repeats
fall on the false positives (Appendix J). Five of the
six false negatives fall on one hedging criterion
whose 7 pairs are all labeled MET. The Decider
says UNMET on 6 of them, and Luna and Gemini
are right on 6 of the 7. Over all errors the LLM
judges repeat 43.6% of their verdicts on Decider
Noul’s and 46.7% on Decider Choice’s, against a
baseline of 25.6% for each. Decider Noul’s Health-
Bench probabilities are overconfident at both ends.
Its 155 pairs with P(MET) of at least 0.875 aver-
age 0.952 against a MET rate of 0.781, and its 77
pairs below 0.125 average 0.041 against 0.195.
Cascades.The best Decider-first cascades defer
to Gemini on six panels, to Luna on LFQA and
USR-PC, and to DeepSeek on RiceChem, and they
defer from 1.2% of the pairs on ELLIPSE to all
of them on HealthBench (Table R4a). On Health-
Bench the protocol’s first stage, Decider Noul, is
the Decider’s least accurate binary framing (Ta-
ble D1). Recomputed from the verdicts, Equa-
tion (K1) holds at every reported threshold, and
on ELLIPSE the cascade’s lead over Gemini, 17.4
points, is the kept share of 0.988 times the De-
cider’s 17.7-point lead on the kept pairs. Cross-
fitted Decider-first cascades trail the best single
judge on five panels (Table 3), and the largest ora-
cle gain, 1.7 points on LFQA, is not significant in a
kept-pair sign test (p= 0.20 ). The Decider’s lead
on kept pairs is significant only on ELLIPSE and

HelpSteer2, the two ordinal panels with Decider
Choice at the top of the matched judges, and the
fallback is ahead on the deferred pairs of all nine
panels, significantly on FED-Turn, FED-Dialogue,
and HealthBench (Table R4b). Counting kept and
deferred pairs together, the Decider alone is right
on 344 ELLIPSE pairs against Gemini’s 77, on 56
HelpSteer2 pairs against 28, and on 12 to 51 pairs
of each other ordinal panel against 23 to 77.
With an oracle threshold, a cascade matches
the best LLM judge without deferring any pair on
RiceChem, ELLIPSE, and HelpSteer2, but must
defer nearly every pair on FED-Dialogue and ev-
ery pair on HealthBench. Cross-fitted, the cascade
that matches that judge on one half beats it on the
other half of ELLIPSE by 17.2 points at 0.3% of its
cost, of HelpSteer2 by 8.0 points at 0.4%, and of
RiceChem by 1.3 points at 5.3%. On LFQA, USR-
TC, and USR-PC it costs 17.9% to 28.1% of that

judge for 0.4 to 1.0 points less, and on FED-Turn,
FED-Dialogue, and HealthBench 66.3% to 97.3%
for 0.3 to 2.0 points less (Table 3).
Juries.Appendix J discusses the juries of Ta-
ble R4c. Picking a correct matched judge wher-
ever one exists would reach 54.2% on ELLIPSE,
against Decider Choice’s 47.1% exact accuracy and
its 47.2% on the pairs all four scored. On ELLIPSE
and HelpSteer2, where the three-LLM median is
significantly less accurate than Decider Choice, it
also trails the most accurate LLM judge, by 12.8
and 8.1 points. With Decider Choice as a fourth ju-
ror, the upper median comes out above the most ac-
curate matched judge on HealthBench, FED-Turn,
and USR-PC, by at most 2.0 points, with no in-
terval above zero. The four-judge median is not
unique on 7.8% (RiceChem) to 38.6% (ELLIPSE)
of the pairs.
Conditions.Table R5 spells out the Decider’s
rows of Table 2. With the Decider’s verdicts
fixed, the LLM judges stay far costlier and slower
throughout, and 10 to 14 of Decider Choice’s 27
comparisons are separated in each condition, most
of them Decider leads. The Decider’s leads fall
on RiceChem, ELLIPSE, and HelpSteer2 in every
condition, and its deficits on FED-Turn and FED-
Dialogue, joined on HealthBench by Gemini in
the whole-rubric condition and by Gemini 3.1 Pro
in the higher tier. The sign test over units finds
that HealthBench deficit in all four conditions. Be-
tween the two judging modes 3 of the 27 classes
change between separated and not, and high effort
keeps 10 of the 13 per-criterion separations, revers-
ing none and adding none. The Decider’s matched
judges agree more closely among themselves than
with the labels on every ordinal panel, though every
matched offset is negative on only four of these pan-
els. Whatever the condition, the LLM judges also
repeat Decider Choice’s confident errors far above
baseline, and the confident-error share falls short
of the 80% fixed before the run (Table R7) only in
the higher tier, where 202 repeated verdicts rather
than 201 would have reached it. On ELLIPSE the
share never exceeds the baseline. No Decider-first
cascade gains as much as 2 points. With flash-tier
fallbacks the largest gain over a panel’s best sin-
gle judge in any condition is 1.7 points (oracle) or
1.2 (cross-fitted), and with higher-tier fallbacks the
largest over the best single judge from either tier
is 1.4 or 0.4. In every condition the panels with
a cross-fitted cascade under half the cost include
ELLIPSE and HelpSteer2. In the main runs De-
cider Choice by itself reaches the best LLM judge’s
accuracy on both, and every condition’s matching
cascade beats that judge on the held-out half.
The repeat.The repeat resent unchanged every
request that the full run had made for the repeat
units (Section 3.2), 266 requests covering 1,237
pairs, each matched to its first sending by the hash
of its body. A repeated request went out 98 to
924 seconds after the first (median 811), so the
repeat tests stability over minutes only. Every ver-
dict and every returned probability came back the
same (Table R6). So did the answers to all 659
duplicate requests of the study, 3,865 criterion an-
swers in all. Besides the repeat, these include the
RiceChem panel’s requests, sent a second time 85
to 99 seconds later as part of the full set, and the
30 requests of the smoke run. The client keeps
no response cache, the 7,000 request identifiers
that the API returned are all distinct, and repeated
requests took longer than first sendings (median
0.41 against 0.31 seconds), though a cache on the
server with ordinary latency cannot be ruled out.
Perplexity documents that identical requests usu-
ally get identical answers and occasionally differ
in the second decimal. In design, the repeat differs
from Jev’s five interleaved passes (Appendix A),
which we do not set beside it. For scale, the LLM
judges’ whole-rubric repeat runs reproduced 86.2%
to 92.6% of their verdicts (Table N4).
Claims fixed before the run.We recorded the
claims to check on the Decider’s copy at 01:49:40
UTC on 7 October 2026, each with a threshold
(Table R7) or with the test that would decide its
wording in the paper. Until then the Decider had
answered only the two probe requests. It first an-
swered on a study item at 04:19:49 UTC, in the
smoke run, and the full run started at 04:21:22.
Four of the seven thresholds were missed. Three
were missed outright, and the one on repeated con-
fident errors only in the higher tier. After the run,
we tested ten statements of the paper against the
Decider’s copy, each by the test recorded with the
thresholds. Three hold as worded for both decision
models, those on cost, wall time, and denominators.
Every LLM judge cost more than Decider Choice
on every panel in every condition, at either price,
and took longer on every panel whose time was
validly measured, and the denominators needed
only the Decider’s own counts. The other seven
hold for the Decider in another form, and the text
